# GOAL-T1 — the table

Synthetic content: invented characters in an invented campaign (Table C's Beregost files, docs/campaigns/baldurs-gate-en). Stage origin/claude/handoff at 23220d6 (docs/stages/GAME_PARTY_1.md, the amendment of 2026-10-27 16:36 LA); design sha256 `2fbb8c8be7456078894153366e0915a0f4c94f774e7c761fc220395191d9920c`.

Arm **P-GOAL**, triad T1; 25 round(s) of 25; ended by rounds. The DM: Claude Opus 5.5 with the owner's settings (Opus writes — Opus records, depth high → xhigh); the relay: the same model at medium. Started 2026-10-28T05:33:10.512Z.

Seats: 1. Bram Halloway (fighter) — GPT-6.1 Sol; 2. Sefa Marrin (warlock) — DeepSeek V4 Pro 0813; 3. Pell Dunmore (rogue) — Claude Opus 5.5. The players are not told what the others are.

Known condition: Seat 1's character (Bram) is the app's hero record; Sefa and Pell are party companions with full records. Known condition, not corrected: the DM's frame is written around the hero and may give that seat more attention; Heroic Inspiration is the hero's only; the allowed rerolls (2 per session) are the table's, shared by all three. The rotation puts each family in that seat once per triad.

**The frozen layer:** P-GOAL's hidden layer, prepared once before any table by the owner's DM settings (Claude Opus 5.5, the prep at high) through openrouter — sha256 `abeeb0ecc5ef9a6100ac6157a7fc33b2f49a6d70cc2ea532f41aa3d52940484a` (10370 characters, 26 threads), written into the DM's private notes before the first round. Its text is DM-private and is not in this file.

The world's snapshot: sha256 `1649b61c08e027ff44de2bb5065046f872e383a00c5d04803d95553f58568f5a` (the same wizard, sheets and opening for both triads).

## The opening

**The DM's opening (as committed by the wizard):**

````text
Beregost is already at work. Hammers ring from the smithies down the lane, a cart rattles past toward the Trade Way, and the last note of Lathander's morning bell fades over the rooftops.

The oak door opens a hand's width, then wider. Hesper Vallow stands in the gap. She is a spare woman in a plain grey coat, ink on two fingers of her right hand. She looks at each of you in turn: Bram with his painted-over shield, Sefa, and Pell. Then her gaze goes past you, up the lane toward the main street, and stays there a moment too long.

"All three of you. Good." Behind her the hall is dim. Every shutter on the ground floor is closed against the sun. "By nightfall, as I said. Not an hour later." She steps back from the threshold and holds the door, but she doesn't explain yet. She is waiting to see who comes in first, or whether anyone has something to ask her out here on the step.
````

## Round 1

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 1 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: I glance up the lane where Hesper is looking, then step inside first, keeping my sword sheathed. I move clear of the doorway so Sefa and Pell can follow.

“Best talk away from the street, then. What needs doing by nightfall—and who are you watching for?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa steps across the threshold first, unhurried, and gives Hesper a small nod—the kind you give a patient's family when they haven't said the worst of it yet.

"Nightfall, then," she says quietly. "We'll hear it inside, I think. Better than on the step."

Pell Dunmore: Pell follows her gaze up the lane before he looks at her again. He takes in the main street, the doorways, whoever's idling where they shouldn't be. Then he steps in, out of the sun, and keeps his voice low.

"You looked up that lane like you were expecting someone. Worth knowing who, before we hear the rest." He pauses on the threshold and nods at the closed shutters. "And if it's the sort of someone who watches doors, I'd rather we talk in a room without windows to the street."

Once he's inside, he moves aside so the others can come in. He stays near the door where he can still see the gap.

*(Passive Perception 14 on the lane as I glance up it: anyone loitering, anyone who turns away too quickly?)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram looks up the lane before he steps inside, and Pell's eyes follow his a moment later.

It's an ordinary Beregost morning. A coal cart is backing into the smithy yard at the bottom of the lane. At the top, where the lane meets the main street, a horse trough stands by the corner. A stable lad sits on its edge with straw on his sleeves, picking at a heel of bread and watching the carts. Across from him, in the shade of the cooper's doorway, a tiefling in a road-dusty coat leans on the wall. When Pell's look reaches him, he turns away quickly, toward the cooper's shut door, and finds something to study in the planking. The lad doesn't turn at all. He just keeps eating.

Bram goes in first, his sword sheathed, and steps to the left of the doorway. Sefa comes right behind him with her small, unhurried nod. Pell comes last, out of the sun. He stops where he can still see a hand's width of the lane through the gap.

The hall smells of beeswax and cold ashes. Hesper shuts the door, drops the bar, and stands a moment with her palm flat on the oak.

"Someone has stood at the top of the lane three mornings running," she says. "Young, I think. I see him from the upstairs window, and never well. I don't know who he is. I don't know who sends him." She looks at Pell. "You're right about the street. Come through."

She takes them down the hall, past a front room where the furniture sits under dust sheets, to a study at the back. Its one window looks onto a walled yard with a rain barrel and a pear tree. Shelves of bound ledgers cover two walls. A notary's desk sits under the window, tidy and unused, with a rack of seal-stamps and a dish of red wax that has gone dull with dust. Hesper doesn't sit. She stands behind the desk, her inked hand resting on its edge.

"My husband was Aubrey Vallow, notary of this town for twenty years. He died this winter. He fell on the steps of the Song of the Morning." She says it like an entry in a register. "Eight months ago he pledged an iron coffer to a moneylender on the main street, Gaspar Thole. I didn't know until last week, when Thole sent me a courtesy notice as the widow. The term of the pledge ends at sundown today. After that, Thole may sell it, and he has a buyer. A Master Corwen Ashby. I don't know him."

"I have been to Thole twice. The first time he was polite and told me nothing. The second time he didn't open the door."

From a drawer she takes a leather purse and sets it on the desk. It's heavy, and it stays where she put it.

"The terms. I want the coffer brought here, to this room, before sundown. Fifty gold now, a hundred more on delivery. How you get it from Thole is your affair, provided nothing is done that puts my name before the temple court. And one condition, which isn't negotiable." She raises her eyes to each of them in turn. "It comes to me shut. Not opened, not tried, not looked into. Aubrey asked me to keep it shut, and I mean to."

She waits with her hand beside the purse and doesn't push it toward any of them.
````

## Round 2

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 2 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: I leave the purse where it is.

“Is that fifty our advance, or the money to redeem the pledge? How much does Thole say he's owed—and have you papers showing your right to collect it?”

I glance at the dusty seal-stamps, then back to her.

“I can agree to leave it shut. But before we take your coin: what do you know of what's inside, and has anyone besides your husband warned you against opening it? If there's danger in carrying it, we need to know.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa folds her hands in front of her and makes no move toward the purse.

"We'll take it on. But before that purse changes hands, tell us three things plainly." Her voice is low and even. "Do you have Aubrey's note of pledge—the number of the debt, the surety written? If we walk into Thole's shop with nothing but a widow's word, he can name any sum and shut the door again."

She glances at the closed window and back to Hesper. "And the coffer itself. You said iron. Is it small enough to carry under one arm, or will we need a cart and two strong backs? Has it any mark we can swear to—a scratch, a lockplate, a letter, a way of proving it is your husband's and not some other man's coffer?"

A pause. "And the watcher at the top of the lane. These three mornings—when does he stop? Does he stay after the doors open, or only until someone shows at a window? Thole may not have been as courteous as his notice."

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't reach for the purse. He glances back down the hall toward the barred door, then at Hesper.

"Your watcher might have company. There's a tiefling in a road coat in the cooper's doorway, across from the trough. He turned away when I looked at him, quicker than a man with nothing to hide. The stable lad didn't turn at all. So either one of them is yours, or there are two of them." He shrugs. "Worth knowing before we walk out of here carrying anything."

He nods at the shelves of ledgers.

"Here's the part I care about. A pledge has a price to buy it back. What did Aubrey borrow against it, and what does Thole want by sundown? If you can just pay it, the easiest way to get your coffer is to walk in the front door with the money, and nobody goes near a temple court. And a notary of twenty years doesn't pledge anything without writing it down. Did he keep his copy of the contract? The pledge note, a receipt, a ledger entry with Thole's name on it? If that paper's in this room, it's worth more to us than any lockpick."

He leaves a beat, then asks the last question more quietly.

"I won't open it. You have my word, and I keep that one. But I'd like to know what I'm carrying, at least from the outside. How big is it, how heavy, and is there a key? Does Thole have one, or did Aubrey keep it?"

*(If she has no answer on the pledge papers, I'd like to search the ledgers for Thole's name or the pledge date. Investigation +4.)*
````

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Investigation: Aubrey's day-books — anything on Thole, the pledge, or where the ticket went · roll · presses: prose, [Roll the die]

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Investigation: Aubrey's day-books — anything on Thole, the pledge, or where the ticket went · decide · presses: prose, [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Nobody touches the purse. Hesper looks at it, then at Bram, as though he had asked the right question first.

"The fifty is yours. It's yours whatever Thole asks." She opens the drawer again and lays a folded sheet beside the purse. It has been opened and closed so often it lies flat along its creases. "His notice. Read it."

It's written in a careful moneylender's hand that takes care over the figures. *To the widow of Master Aubrey Vallow.* One coffer of iron, banded, small enough for one man to carry, its hasp sealed in yellow wax, deposited by the said Master Vallow eight months since, against a loan of one hundred and twenty gold pieces. Redeemable until sundown of this day *by the bearer of the ticket, or the lawful heir producing it.* After that it falls forfeit to the undersigned. A buyer, Master Corwen Ashby, has declared his interest. *G. Thole, in his own hand.*

"A hundred and twenty," Hesper says. "If he'll take it lawfully, I'll pay it, and your hundred still stands. I can't go far past that." She answers Bram's other question in the same even tone. "I'm his widow and his heir. The will was proved before the temple court in the spring, and I hold the copy. I showed it to Thole the first time. He read it twice and said the terms stand as written. He wouldn't tell me whether that meant he'd take my money." A pause. "I have no ticket. There's no copy of the pledge in this house. I've been through his papers. It's the only paper I ever knew him not to keep."

She turns to Sefa. "I never saw the coffer. What you've just read is all I know of it." She tips her chin at the rack of stamps and the dish of dull red wax. "Aubrey sealed in red. Every stamp in that rack is his, and every one takes red."

To Pell: "If there's a key, I haven't found it. The notice doesn't mention one."

Then back to Bram's last question. "I don't know what's in it. Nobody has warned me of anything. That notice is the only word anyone has sent me about it. Aubrey told me once, in this room: if it ever comes to you, keep it shut. He didn't say why, and I didn't ask." She answers that one faster than the others, and her eyes stay on the wax dish while she does.

The watcher next. "He's there when I open the upstairs shutters, before the bell. By the time I sit down to eat at noon, he's gone. It's been the same three days running. I don't know where he goes." She looks at Pell. "From upstairs I can see the corner, the trough, the cooper's step and a hat. I couldn't tell you which of them it is, or whether it's the same one every morning." To Sefa, flatly: "He was courteous the first time. The second time he was in. I saw his lamp."

At Pell's promise she holds his eyes a moment longer than she has held anyone's. "I'll hold you to that."

When he looks at the shelves, she hesitates. Then she takes down two narrow books bound in calf, this year's and last year's, and sets them on the desk. "The day-books. Not the registers. Those are his clients' business, not mine to open."

Pell sits down at the dead man's desk and starts turning pages: leases, wills, witnessings, appointments, all in a small square hand.

Pell finds it. Eight months back, between a lease on a malting-house and a will witnessed for a tanner, there's a line with nothing beside it: *G.T. — noon.* Hesper has seen that already; he can tell from the way she doesn't lean in. He reads on, though, back through the winter into the autumn before, and two entries a few weeks apart stop him. They read *Song. Archive. Sr. M.K.*, the same three words twice. Beside the second, in the margin, is something that isn't writing. It's a small inked circle, cut down the middle by an upright stroke that ends in a little hook at the foot, like the bit of a key. Nothing else in either book looks like it.

Hesper looks where his finger rests. "Sister Maudra Kell keeps the temple archive. Aubrey did the temple's leases and bequests for years. I saw those entries." Her eyes move to the margin and stay there. "That mark I don't know. It isn't one of his."

The bell of the Song of the Morning hasn't rung again. Half an hour has gone by since you came in off the lane, and the purse still sits on the desk where she put it.

 *(Out of game: none of the four needed a roll, since they're plain statements and questions with nothing uncertain riding on them, and all four already happened last turn: Bram left the purse alone, Hesper answered every question, and Pell's report about the tiefling and the stable lad got her reply.)*
````

Rolls: Pell Dunmore Investigation: Aubrey's day-books — anything on Thole, the pledge, or where the ticket went 19 vs 15 success

````text
Nobody touches the purse. Hesper looks at it, then at you, as though you had asked the right question first.

"The fifty is yours. It's yours whatever Thole asks." She opens the drawer again and lays a folded sheet beside the purse. It has been opened and closed so often it lies flat along its creases. "His notice. Read it."

It's written in a careful moneylender's hand that takes care over the figures. *To the widow of Master Aubrey Vallow.* One coffer of iron, banded, small enough for one man to carry, its hasp sealed in yellow wax, deposited by the said Master Vallow eight months since, against a loan of one hundred and twenty gold pieces. Redeemable until sundown of this day *by the bearer of the ticket, or the lawful heir producing it.* After that it falls forfeit to the undersigned. A buyer, Master Corwen Ashby, has declared his interest. *G. Thole, in his own hand.*

"A hundred and twenty," Hesper says. "If he'll take it lawfully, I'll pay it, and your hundred still stands. I can't go far past that." She answers your other question in the same even tone. "I'm his widow and his heir. The will was proved before the temple court in the spring, and I hold the copy. I showed it to Thole the first time. He read it twice and said the terms stand as written. He wouldn't tell me whether that meant he'd take my money." A pause. "I have no ticket. There's no copy of the pledge in this house. I've been through his papers. It's the only paper I ever knew him not to keep."

She turns to Sefa. "I never saw the coffer. What you've just read is all I know of it." She tips her chin at the rack of stamps and the dish of dull red wax. "Aubrey sealed in red. Every stamp in that rack is his, and every one takes red."

To Pell: "If there's a key, I haven't found it. The notice doesn't mention one."

Then back to your last question. "I don't know what's in it. Nobody has warned me of anything. That notice is the only word anyone has sent me about it. Aubrey told me once, in this room: if it ever comes to you, keep it shut. He didn't say why, and I didn't ask." She answers that one faster than the others, and her eyes stay on the wax dish while she does.

The watcher next. "He's there when I open the upstairs shutters, before the bell. By the time I sit down to eat at noon, he's gone. It's been the same three days running. I don't know where he goes." She looks at Pell. "From upstairs I can see the corner, the trough, the cooper's step and a hat. I couldn't tell you which of them it is, or whether it's the same one every morning." To Sefa, flatly: "He was courteous the first time. The second time he was in. I saw his lamp."

At Pell's promise she holds his eyes a moment longer than she has held anyone's. "I'll hold you to that."

When he looks at the shelves, she hesitates. Then she takes down two narrow books bound in calf, this year's and last year's, and sets them on the desk. "The day-books. Not the registers. Those are his clients' business, not mine to open."

Pell sits down at the dead man's desk and starts turning pages. He reads a long while, back through the year and into the one before, and at last his finger comes to rest on a page.

Hesper looks where his finger rests. "Sister Maudra Kell keeps the temple archive. Aubrey did the temple's leases and bequests for years. I saw those entries." Her eyes move to the margin and stay there. "That mark I don't know. It isn't one of his."

The bell of the Song of the Morning hasn't rung again. Half an hour has gone by since you came in off the lane, and the purse still sits on the desk where she put it.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
You find it. Eight months back, between a lease on a malting-house and a will witnessed for a tanner, there's a line with nothing beside it: *G.T. — noon.* Hesper has seen that already; you can tell from the way she doesn't lean in. You read on, though, back through the winter into the autumn before, and two entries a few weeks apart stop you. They read *Song. Archive. Sr. M.K.*, the same three words twice. Beside the second, in the margin, is something that isn't writing. It's a small inked circle, cut down the middle by an upright stroke that ends in a little hook at the foot, like the bit of a key. Nothing else in either book looks like it.

Hesper looks where your finger rests. "Sister Maudra Kell keeps the temple archive. Aubrey did the temple's leases and bequests for years. I saw those entries." Her eyes move to the margin and stay there. "That mark I don't know. It isn't one of his."

The bell of the Song of the Morning hasn't rung again. Half an hour has gone by since you came in off the lane, and the purse still sits on the desk where she put it.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 3

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 3 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: “What have you found, Pell?”

I step beside the desk to look at the entry and the unfamiliar mark without disturbing the pages.

“Mistress Vallow, we'll try the lawful road first. We'll need that notice, your proved copy of the will, and your written authority to act for you. Keep the redemption money ready until we know whether Thole will accept it without the ticket.”

I glance toward Sefa and Pell. “If he won't, we find out precisely why. Sister Maudra may be able to help us after that.”

Then I turn back to Hesper. “One other thing. Did Aubrey seem hurt or frightened when he told you to keep it shut? And was anyone with him when he fell?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa steps to the desk without taking the notice from Hesper's side, and bends a little over the open day-book.

"Show me exactly where the margin mark lies," she says to Pell. "The widow says it isn't Aubrey's hand. If it's writing of any kind, I may be able to read it."

She lets her eyes pass from the mark to the entries around it, then looks up at Hesper. "Thole's wording is a leash with two knots. 'The bearer of the ticket' he has never given you, and 'the lawful heir producing it'—producing the ticket, the will, or the coffer? A temple clerk could argue that for a month. We have until sundown, so we won't argue unless we must."

A pause. "His notice says the pledge ended because the ticket is lost. But a notary of twenty years kept a day-book. If Aubrey wrote what was pledged and to whom, and some other hand put a mark beside it, that mark may tell us whether Thole has been here once already to tidy his side of the shelf."

Pell Dunmore: Pell copies the mark onto a scrap of paper with Aubrey's own pen: the circle, the upright stroke, the little hook at the foot. He blows on it and tucks it inside his coat. Then he finally reaches over and takes the purse, weighs it once in his palm, and sets it down nearer the middle of the desk, where it belongs to all three of them.

"We'll take it." He turns the notice so the others can see one line, and taps it. "*By the bearer of the ticket, or the lawful heir producing it.* That's a moneylender's way of saying the ticket comes first. Aubrey kept every paper he ever touched except this one. So either he hid it, or he gave it to someone. If it's in a stranger's hand by sundown, Thole can hand the coffer over and call it lawful, and your will won't stop him. I'd wager Master Ashby knows where that ticket is, even if we don't."

He looks at Bram and Sefa.

"Here's how I'd cut it. Mistress Vallow goes to Thole with the hundred and twenty and the will, and she doesn't go alone. One of you walks in beside her. He shut his door on a widow. He won't do that to a man in chain mail with a writ in her hand, not in daylight on the main street. If he takes the money, we're done by noon." A pause. "If he won't, I want to know why. And I want to know where Thole keeps things, and whether Ashby has already been in to see it."

He nods toward the front of the house.

"Our watcher leaves before noon. I'd like to see where he goes. I'll slip out the back over the yard wall and pick him up from the corner. The tiefling first, since he's the one who flinched." He holds up the folded scrap. "Then the temple. Sister Kell saw Aubrey twice in her archive, and this mark sits beside the second visit. If it's a temple sign, she'll know it. If it isn't, her face will tell me."

To Hesper, quieter: "Is the yard gate barred, and does it come out on another lane? I'd rather nobody sees four of us leave by your front door."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram steps up beside the desk. The day-book is open where Pell left it, and the entry is easy to read: *Song. Archive. Sr. M.K.*, with the small inked figure in the margin beside it. It is a circle with one upright stroke through it, hooked at the foot.

Sefa leans in over the page from the other side, close enough to see the strokes of the ink. Her eyes have read every script ever put in front of them. They find nothing to read here: no letter, no glyph, no cipher. It is a drawing of something. The hand that made it went slowly and carefully, the way you copy a thing you have only seen once.

Hesper takes the questions in order, the way she takes everything.

"The notice is yours." She slides the folded sheet across to Bram. "The authority I'll write now." She sits for the first time since you came in, takes a clean sheet from the drawer and writes without stopping. She doesn't reach for the rack of stamps. "Those are his. I won't put his seal on my business." She signs, sands it and hands it to Bram. It's four lines: *I, Hesper Vallow, widow and proved heir of Aubrey Vallow, notary of Beregost, authorise the bearer, Bram Halloway, to treat on my behalf with Master Gaspar Thole for the redemption of the coffer named in his notice.* "The will doesn't leave this house unless I go with it. The money will be ready."

Then Bram's last two questions. She rests her inked hand flat on the desk.

"He was tired. He had been tired since the autumn." That's all she says to the first one, and she lets it stand. "Nobody was with him. He went out before light, to the dawn service. He often did. A novice found him at the foot of the steps when she came to sweep. The temple's healer laid him out. She wrote that he fell."

When Sefa says the notice blames a lost ticket, Hesper shakes her head once. "It doesn't say the ticket is lost. It doesn't say where the ticket is at all. That's my word, not his." She turns the day-book a little toward Sefa. "And the mark isn't beside Thole. He wrote *G.T.* and the hour, and nothing else. The mark is beside the temple." She pauses. "Sister Kell keeps the archive, not the court. Ask her whatever you like. But whatever she writes down goes into the temple's books."

She watches Pell weigh the purse and set it down again in the middle of the desk. "Then it's agreed. The fifty is yours to divide as you see fit." She leaves it lying where he put it.

As for walking into Thole's with one of them beside her: "I'll go. He has seen me twice. He hasn't seen me with company." Something in her face goes harder. "I would like to see him not open the door to a man in mail."

Last, the yard. "There's a door in the back wall, under the pear tree. It's bolted on this side and hasn't been opened since the winter. It gives onto the cart alley behind the smithies. The alley runs up behind the cooper's yard and comes out on the main street two doors below the trough." She stands. "I can be ready to go to Thole in a quarter of an hour. Tell me which of you is coming, and whether we go now or wait until your watcher has gone."

 *(Out of game: Bram's two questions and his lawful-route plan were honest talk, so they needed no roll. Hesper answers both in the story above: "he was tired since the autumn," nobody was with him, a novice found him, and she hands over the notice and the written authority but keeps the will.)*

*(Out of game: Sister Maudra is Bram's fallback if Thole refuses, so nothing happens there until the group goes to the temple.)*

Pell's copying, which the story skipped: before Hesper writes her letter, Pell takes Aubrey's pen from its rest. It's dry, so he dips it once in the inkwell and copies the mark onto a scrap of paper. He draws the circle, then the upright stroke, then the small hook at its foot. It takes a moment; nothing about it is uncertain. He blows the ink dry and puts the scrap inside his coat. His copy is a little heavier in the line than Aubrey's.
````

````text
The big man in mail steps up beside the desk. "What have you found, Pell?" He looks at the entry and the mark without touching the pages.

"Mistress Vallow, we'll try the lawful road first. We'll need that notice, your proved copy of the will, and your written authority to act for you. Keep the redemption money ready until we know whether Thole will accept it without the ticket."

Bram glances at Sefa and you. "If he won't, we find out precisely why. Sister Maudra may be able to help us after that." Then he turns back to Hesper. "One other thing. Did Aubrey seem hurt or frightened when he told you to keep it shut? And was anyone with him when he fell?"

Sefa steps to the desk without taking the notice from Hesper's side and bends a little over the open day-book. "Show me exactly where the margin mark lies," she says to you. "The widow says it isn't Aubrey's hand. If it's writing of any kind, I may be able to read it."

Her eyes move from the mark to the entries around it, then she looks up at Hesper. "Thole's wording is a leash with two knots. 'The bearer of the ticket' he has never given you, and 'the lawful heir producing it'. Producing the ticket, the will, or the coffer? A temple clerk could argue that for a month. We have until sundown, so we won't argue unless we must." A pause. "His notice says the pledge ended because the ticket is lost. But a notary of twenty years kept a day-book. If Aubrey wrote what was pledged and to whom, and some other hand put a mark beside it, that mark may tell us whether Thole has been here once already to tidy his side of the shelf."

You take Aubrey's pen from its rest. It's dry, so you dip it once in the inkwell before you copy the mark: the circle, then the upright stroke, then the small hook at its foot. It takes a moment, and nothing about it is uncertain. Your copy is a little heavier in the line than Aubrey's.

The day-book lies open where you left it, and the entry is easy to read: *Song. Archive. Sr. M.K.*, with the small inked figure in the margin beside it. Bram stands at one side of the desk. Sefa leans in over the page from the other, close enough to see the strokes of the ink.

Hesper takes the questions in order, the way she takes everything.

"The notice is yours." She slides the folded sheet across to Bram. "The authority I'll write now." She sits for the first time since you came in, takes a clean sheet from the drawer and writes without stopping. She doesn't reach for the rack of stamps. "Those are his. I won't put his seal on my business." She signs it, sands it and hands it to Bram. It runs four lines: *I, Hesper Vallow, widow and proved heir of Aubrey Vallow, notary of Beregost, authorise the bearer, Bram Halloway, to treat on my behalf with Master Gaspar Thole for the redemption of the coffer named in his notice.* "The will doesn't leave this house unless I go with it. The money will be ready."

Then she answers Bram's last two questions. She rests her inked hand flat on the desk.

"He was tired. He had been tired since the autumn." That's all she says to the first one, and she lets it stand. "Nobody was with him. He went out before light, to the dawn service. He often did. A novice found him at the foot of the steps when she came to sweep. The temple's healer laid him out. She wrote that he fell."

When Sefa says the notice blames a lost ticket, Hesper shakes her head once. "It doesn't say the ticket is lost. It doesn't say where the ticket is at all. That's my word, not his." She turns the day-book a little toward Sefa. "And the mark isn't beside Thole. He wrote *G.T.* and the hour, and nothing else. The mark is beside the temple." She pauses. "Sister Kell keeps the archive, not the court. Ask her whatever you like. But whatever she writes down goes into the temple's books."

She watches you weigh the purse and set it down in the middle of the desk. "Then it's agreed. The fifty is yours to divide as you see fit." She leaves it lying where you put it.

To your idea of walking into Thole's with one of you beside her: "I'll go. He has seen me twice. He hasn't seen me with company." Something in her face goes harder. "I would like to see him not open the door to a man in mail."

Last, the yard. "There's a door in the back wall, under the pear tree. It's bolted on this side and hasn't been opened since the winter. It gives onto the cart alley behind the smithies. The alley runs up behind the cooper's yard and comes out on the main street two doors below the trough." She stands. "I can be ready to go to Thole in a quarter of an hour. Tell me which of you is coming, and whether we go now or wait until your watcher has gone."
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 4

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 4 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: “I'll walk with you, Mistress Vallow. Bring the will and the redemption money. We go when you're ready—no sense letting the morning slip.”

I fold the authority and notice carefully into my pack.

“Pell, we'll give you those fifteen minutes to get into place. I won't hail you if I spot you. Mind yourself; we don't yet know that either fellow is watching this house.”

I turn to Sefa. “I'd welcome you beside us at Thole's. Another pair of ears may be worth more than the mail.”

While Hesper gets ready, I loosen my sword belt a notch so the hilt sits comfortably, keeping the blade sheathed. “We're going to offer payment, not threaten him. Let him give us his answer plainly.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa looks up from the mark and folds her arms, not quite reaching for the purse.

"The hand that drew this was copying something seen, not remembered. Slow strokes, made with care. A sign of a thing, not a word. If that is meant to point anywhere, the original may be somewhere a person could stand and look at it—a wall, a gate, a step. The route to the temple, maybe, or the tabernacle door." She pauses. "Have you seen this figure anywhere before? Not written. Drawn, scratched, marked on stone or wood?"

She meets Hesper's eye. "I'll walk in with you to Thole. Not before him, not behind. He has refused a widow twice. Let him see you don't come alone this time." A beat. "But I agree with Pell. Three people at your front door in daylight will draw the watcher like a candle. Let Pell go over the yard wall first. Then we go out the back together, by the cart alley, and meet him on the main street."

She glances between Bram and Pell. "If Thole opens the door to Hesper and a man in mail beside her, I'll be the one watching his face while she speaks. When he refuses—if he refuses—let his clerk look out the window and see we still don't leave." She pauses. "And ask around who Corwen Ashby is before we go. If Thole sees his buyer in the street, or we do, that changes the walk."

She moves toward the back door, then turns. "The watcher's gone from the corner by the time he leaves. But Pell, if the tiefling flinched and is still by the trough, ask him if he's hired, and by whom. That's faster than following him."

Pell Dunmore: Pell takes the fifty and counts it out on the desk in three piles: seventeen, seventeen, sixteen. He pushes the two larger ones toward Bram and Sefa and pockets the small one without comment.

"Go now," he tells Hesper. "Don't wait for him to leave. I want him to see you walk down that lane with Bram beside you. If he's only watching the house, he'll stay put. If he's watching for the coffer, he'll run to whoever pays him the moment you turn toward Thole's. Either way, I'll learn something."

He looks at Bram. "Give me a count of a hundred before you open the front door. I'll be in the alley by then. Walk slowly, and don't look at the corner. Let him have a good look at you."

To Sefa: "Your choice. Thole's with them, or the temple. If you go to Sister Kell, don't show her the mark first. Ask about Aubrey's visits and watch how she answers. Then show her." He touches the scrap inside his coat. "I'll keep my copy, so we've each got one if you want to draw your own. Whatever she writes down goes in their books, so tell her as little as you can."

He looks around at all three of them. "Thole's front step, or the cooper's corner, at noon. Whoever isn't there by the bell, the rest go looking."

Then he goes out to the yard. Under the pear tree he checks the old bolt before he touches it. Rust? A bar on the far side? He works it back slowly, a little at a time, so it doesn't shriek. He eases the door open just far enough to slip through and pulls it nearly closed behind him.

*(Stealth +9 up the cart alley behind the cooper's yard. I want to get to a spot near the main street where I can see the trough and the cooper's doorway without being seen. If there's a wall or a low roof with a view, I'll take that instead.)*
````

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Stealth: up the cart alley and onto a hidden perch with a view of the trough and the cooper's door, in broad daylight · roll · presses: prose, [Roll the die]

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Stealth: up the cart alley and onto a hidden perch with a view of the trough and the cooper's door, in broad daylight · decide · presses: prose, [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hesper hears Bram out with her eyes on the folded papers as they go into his pack. "Give me a quarter of an hour. The will is upstairs, and so is the money." She doesn't comment on the sword belt. Her eyes drop to it once and come back up.

Sefa's question she answers straight away. "No. Not drawn, not cut, not anywhere. I would remember." She looks at the day-book a moment longer. "And Ashby isn't in either book. I looked for him the day the notice came." When Sefa suggests leaving by the alley, Hesper shakes her head once. "I've gone out by that front door every day for twenty-two years. I'm not leaving my own house by the cart alley to go and pay a debt." Then, to Sefa, a little less flatly: "Come with me. I'd be glad of it."

Pell counts the fifty out on the desk: seventeen, seventeen, sixteen. He leaves the two larger piles on the wood in front of Bram and Sefa and pockets the smallest. Hesper watches him do it and says nothing. Then she goes up the stairs, and they hear a floorboard and a drawer overhead.

The yard smells of wet stone and fallen pears. The door under the tree is set deep in the wall, and its bolt has rusted brown along the slide. Pell tries it with two fingers first. It's stiff, but nothing is bent. He works it back a grain at a time, rocking it in its staple, and the rust gives with a dry whisper instead of a shriek. There's no bar on the far side. The door has swollen over the winter, though, and it binds at the foot. He lifts it on its hinges as he pulls, so it opens without scraping the sill. He slips through a gap the width of his shoulders and draws it nearly shut behind him.

The cart alley is rutted and black with coal grit. On the left the smithies back onto it, open sheds full of heat with a boy working a bellows in one. On the right are yard walls with ivy on them. Up ahead, the cooper's yard is stacked with staves and has a lean-to shed against the wall, roofed in old shingles, low enough to step up onto from a rain butt. A carter is unhitching at the alley's mouth with his back half turned. The bellows boy is watching the coals.

Pell keeps to the ivy side, where the wall's shadow runs along the ruts. He passes the bellows boy between two pumps of the handle, and the boy never lifts his eyes from the coals. At the cooper's wall he steps onto the rain butt's rim and then the lean-to roof, and lies flat along the shingles. Nobody in the alley turns. The roof's ridge meets the cooper's back wall at the height of his chin. Past the corner of the building he can see a slice of the main street: the trough, the corner of the lane, and the cooper's front step at an angle.

The stable lad is still on the edge of the trough. He's finished the bread and is brushing crumbs off his knee, and he watches each cart go by the way a bored boy watches carts. Every so often his eyes drift down Hesper's lane and drift back up. It's no more often than they drift anywhere else.

The tiefling is still in the cooper's doorway. While Pell watches, he straightens, raises his knuckles to the shut door, and holds them there a moment. Then he lowers his hand without knocking and leans on the wall again, arms folded, his jaw set.

Back in the study, the floorboard creaks overhead again, and Hesper comes down. She has a dark cloak over the grey coat. She carries a leather document case under one arm and a drawstring purse in her hand, and she holds the purse the way people hold money they can't spare. "The will, the proving, and a hundred and twenty," she says. "Counted twice." She goes down the hall to the front door and puts her hand on the bar, but she doesn't lift it. It's a little past nine. She waits for Bram and Sefa.

 Pell made it up the alley unseen. The carter, the bellows boy and the two men at the top of the lane never knew he went past. He lies on the cooper's lean-to roof with the street in view, and as far as anyone at the trough or the cooper's step can tell, there's nobody there.
````

## Round 5

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 5 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: I pocket my seventeen gold and join Hesper at the door.

“Ready. You lead us to his shop; I'll stay beside you.”

Once Sefa is with us, I let Hesper lift the bar and step out with her. I keep an easy pace, giving the corner no deliberate look, and leave my sword alone. On the walk I watch the people and doorways ahead, as I would with a wagon to escort.

Sefa Marrin: Sefa follows Hesper into the hall and stands a step behind her, nearer the wall than the door.

"I'll go with her." She says it like it was never in doubt. Then, lower: "When we come out, I'll walk on her far side from the trough and watch the lane as if I were looking for the morning cart. If he turns to follow, I'll see him."

She looks back at the desk, where her pile of silver and gold still lies. "Leave my share where it is. If we come back with the coffer, then I've earned it."

Then she falls quiet and waits by the bar.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hesper lifts the bar herself. She lays it against the wall, opens the door onto the bright lane and steps out without looking toward the corner. Bram comes out at her right shoulder, his seventeen gold in his purse. Sefa takes the far side, away from the trough, and turns her head down the lane as if she were waiting for the morning cart. Behind them the study is empty. Sefa's seventeen gold lies on the dead man's desk where she left it.

They walk up the lane at Hesper's pace, which is quick and even. The coal cart has finished at the smithy yard. Hammers ring on both sides of them.

Bram watches the corner ahead the way he'd watch a ford before the wagons go in. He looks at the doorways, the shade and the people sitting still. The tiefling in the cooper's doorway sees a man in mail coming and steps down off the step to make room. He ducks his head the way people do when they're used to being looked at. He doesn't watch them pass.

The stable lad does watch them, the way anyone watches a man in mail. Then his eyes go back to the carts. Bram spent ten years in coaching yards, and something about the boy doesn't fit. It's an hour past the bell. At that hour every lad in every inn yard on the Way is mucking out or hauling water, and this one sits on a trough with no horse, no bucket and no fork. Either he's skiving and doesn't care who sees it, or someone is paying him to sit there.

They turn onto the main street. Sefa looks back once at the turn, casually, like a woman checking for traffic. The boy is still on the trough. He hasn't moved and nobody has stood up to follow them. The main street is full of carts, and the doorways are full of people with their own business.

Thole's house stands on the main street, five doors past the cooper's, on the side toward the temple. It's narrow and stone-faced, with a brass-bound door and a small barred grille at head height. A painted board over the lintel reads *G. THOLE, LOANS ON SURETY*. Hesper knocks twice.

The grille opens. A pair of pale eyes looks out at Hesper, then at Bram's shield and mail, then past all three of them at the street. Nobody says anything for a while. Then a bolt slides back, then a second one, and the door opens.

Gaspar Thole is a soft, careful man of fifty in a good brown coat, his sparse hair combed flat. He lets them into a front room with a counter across it. On the counter there's a brass rail, a pair of money scales, and a big ledger lying open. At the back of the room, behind the counter, there's a second door. It's iron, painted dark green, with a keyhole the size of a thumb. Thole bolts the street door behind them before he says a word.

"Mistress Vallow." He goes behind the counter, which puts the brass rail between them. "You've brought company."

Hesper stops at the counter. She sets her document case on it, puts the drawstring purse beside the case, and rests her inked hand flat on the wood. "One hundred and twenty gold. The will and its proving. Master Halloway holds my written authority. I've come to redeem my husband's pledge."

Thole looks at the purse without touching it. He turns the ledger round on the counter so it faces them and puts a finger on an entry. The writing is the same careful hand as the notice: *Coffer, iron, banded, hasp sealed in yellow wax, A. Vallow, 120 gp, eight months.* Under it is a clause copied out in full: *redeemable until sundown of the term by the bearer of my ticket, or my lawful heir producing it.* His thumb rests on the next line down and stays there.

"Those were his words, mistress. Not mine. *My* ticket. *Producing it.*" He says it gently, the way a man explains a bill. "I wrote him a ticket, and he walked out with it in his hand. I don't know where it is now. If I give the coffer to you today, and tomorrow some fellow comes in holding that ticket, I owe him a coffer I no longer have. Then I'm the one standing before the temple court."

He wipes his palms on his coat, though the room isn't warm.

"Master Ashby has offered two hundred and fifty, to be paid at sundown. He hasn't asked me about any ticket." He glances at Bram's shield again, and then at the bolted door. "If I'm to carry that risk for you instead, I'd need three hundred."

Hesper doesn't move her hand. "You told me the terms stand as written."

"They do, mistress," Thole says. "That's my whole difficulty."
````

````text
The grille opens. A pair of pale eyes looks out at Hesper, then at your shield and mail, then past all three of you at the street. Nobody says anything for a while. Then a bolt slides back, then a second one, and the door opens.

Gaspar Thole is a soft, careful man of fifty in a good brown coat, his sparse hair combed flat. He lets you into a front room with a counter across it. On the counter there's a brass rail, a pair of money scales, and a big ledger lying open. At the back of the room, behind the counter, there's a second door. It's iron, painted dark green, with a keyhole the size of a thumb. Thole bolts the street door behind you before he says a word.

"Mistress Vallow." He goes behind the counter, which puts the brass rail between you. "You've brought company."

Hesper stops at the counter. She sets her document case on it, puts the drawstring purse beside the case, and rests her inked hand flat on the wood. "One hundred and twenty gold. The will and its proving. Master Halloway holds my written authority. I've come to redeem my husband's pledge."

Thole looks at the purse without touching it. He turns the ledger round on the counter so it faces you and puts a finger on an entry. The writing is the same careful hand as the notice: *Coffer, iron, banded, hasp sealed in yellow wax, A. Vallow, 120 gp, eight months.* Under it is a clause copied out in full: *redeemable until sundown of the term by the bearer of my ticket, or my lawful heir producing it.* His thumb rests on the next line down and stays there.

"Those were his words, mistress. Not mine. *My* ticket. *Producing it.*" He says it gently, the way a man explains a bill. "I wrote him a ticket, and he walked out with it in his hand. I don't know where it is now. If I give the coffer to you today, and tomorrow some fellow comes in holding that ticket, I owe him a coffer I no longer have. Then I'm the one standing before the temple court."

He wipes his palms on his coat, though the room isn't warm.

"Master Ashby has offered two hundred and fifty, to be paid at sundown. He hasn't asked me about any ticket." He glances at your shield again, and then at the bolted door. "If I'm to carry that risk for you instead, I'd need three hundred."

Hesper doesn't move her hand. "You told me the terms stand as written."

"They do, mistress," Thole says. "That's my whole difficulty."
````

````text
Bram pockets his seventeen gold and joins Hesper at the door.

"Ready. You lead us to his shop; I'll stay beside you."

Hesper lifts the bar herself. She lays it against the wall, opens the door onto the bright lane and steps out without looking toward the corner. Bram comes out at her right shoulder, his seventeen gold in his purse, his hand nowhere near his sword. You take the far side, away from the trough, and turn your head down the lane as if you were waiting for the morning cart. Behind you the study is empty. Your seventeen gold lies on the dead man's desk where you left it.

You walk up the lane at Hesper's pace, which is quick and even. Bram keeps an easy stride, watching the doorways and the people ahead. The coal cart has finished at the smithy yard. Hammers ring on both sides of you.

The tiefling in the cooper's doorway sees a man in mail coming and steps down off the step to make room. He ducks his head the way people do when they're used to being looked at. He doesn't watch you pass.

The stable lad does watch you, the way anyone watches a man in mail. Then his eyes go back to the carts. He sits on the trough with no horse, no bucket and no fork.

You turn onto the main street. You look back once at the turn, casually, like a woman checking for traffic. The boy is still on the trough. He hasn't moved and nobody has stood up to follow you. The main street is full of carts, and the doorways are full of people with their own business.

Thole's house stands on the main street, five doors past the cooper's, on the side toward the temple. It's narrow and stone-faced, with a brass-bound door and a small barred grille at head height. A painted board over the lintel reads *G. THOLE, LOANS ON SURETY*. Hesper knocks twice.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 5 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays flat and still, cheek to the warm shingles, breathing slowly through his nose.

*That's not a lookout,* he thinks, watching the tiefling's raised knuckles drop again. *A lookout doesn't knock on the door he's leaning on. That's a man working up the nerve to ask for something, and losing it.* He files it away. A flinch from a tiefling in Beregost these days might mean nothing worse than being tired of being stared at.

The boy is the one he watches more closely. The boy's bored, and boys aren't usually this patient about being bored. The crumbs are gone and he's still sitting there.

Pell doesn't move. He waits for the sound of Hesper's front door, or the sight of Bram's painted shield coming up the lane with two women beside him. When they come, he isn't going to watch them. He'll watch the trough and the step: who looks, who gets up, which way they go, and whether they hurry.

If the boy slides off the trough and heads up the main street, Pell will drop off the far side of the lean-to into the cooper's yard and go out after him, a street behind and on the other side.

*(Passive Insight 14 on the boy while I wait: is he watching the lane on purpose, the way a hired eye does, or is it just idle drifting? And does the tiefling have any business with the cooper, like a tool, a barrel, or a paper in his hand?)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell hears the bar come off Hesper's door before he sees anything. It's a dull scrape of wood carried up the lane between hammer strokes. The boy on the trough hears it too. His head doesn't turn, but he stops brushing at his knee, and his hand stays where it is, flat on his thigh, until the door has opened.

Then they come up the lane. Bram's brown-painted shield is at Hesper's right shoulder and Sefa walks on her far side, looking back down the lane as if waiting for a cart. Pell keeps his eyes off them and watches the corner.

The tiefling sees the mail and steps down off the step to make room, with his head ducked. He doesn't look after them, and his eyes go back to the cooper's door before they've passed.

The boy watches them come the way anyone watches a man in mail. Pell has watched a great many people pretend to be bored, and the boy gives himself away afterwards. As the three of them turn onto the main street toward the temple end, a loaded cart comes the other way and blocks his view. The boy puts a hand on the trough's rim and lifts himself up on it, just a few inches, to see past the cart which way they've gone. He holds there until he has seen. Then he sits back down, folds his arms, and lets his eyes drift over the carts again. The boy wasn't curious about them. He was making sure, the way a hired eye does when he knows somebody will ask him later.

He doesn't get up and he doesn't hurry. He leans back on his hands and squints up at the temple tower over the rooftops, the way a man checks the hour when he's being paid by the day.

The tiefling gives Pell a plainer answer. He has a stick in his hand, a length of hazel no longer than a forearm, split down its length. Pell has seen hundreds of them in Lower City yards: a tally, notched across both halves and broken apart, so that the man owed keeps one half and the man who owes keeps the other. He turns it over in his fingers and runs his thumb down the notches. The backs of his hands are pocked with small old burns, the kind iron scale leaves when it flies off the anvil. While Pell watches, the cooper's door opens a crack. A woman in an apron looks out, sees the tiefling and says something short that doesn't carry to the roof. Then she shuts the door. The tiefling puts the tally inside his coat and leans back against the wall. His jaw is set the way it was before.

The bell hasn't rung. A coal cart grinds past the alley mouth below Pell. The boy stays on the trough with his arms folded, and he has settled in to sit there a good while yet.

Nothing else changes at the corner unless Pell moves. He can lie here as long as he likes.
````

## Round 6

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 6 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Then let's keep to the writing,” I say evenly. “May we see the rest of that entry, beneath your thumb?”

I stay beside Hesper, hands clear of my sword.

“You're right that a missing ticket leaves a claim unsettled. But how does three hundred gold settle it where a hundred and twenty won't? Would you give her a written release against any later bearer?”

I pause. “And is there a lawful way to cancel the missing ticket before sundown—a declaration before the temple court, or a surety? We'd rather pay for that than buy the same coffer twice.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa hasn't moved from her place behind Hesper's shoulder. She keeps her expression mild, the way you might at a sickbed, and lets her eyes rest on Thole's face while he speaks.

Then, quietly, she says: "You wrote that clause yourself, or you copied it. Either way, it doesn't say two tickets. It says the bearer of *my* ticket, *or* my lawful heir producing it." She gestures once at the document case. "The heir is here, with the proving. Once she redeems, the pledge is ended. A man who came in tomorrow waving a ticket would be waving a dead leaf. The coffer would already be with its owner. You'd carry no debt to him, and the court would say so."

She glances at the heavy dark-green door behind him, then back to his face, and says nothing more.
````

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Persuasion: convincing Thole that the heir's redemption ends the pledge, so no ticket-holder could claim against him · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Persuasion: convincing Thole that the heir's redemption ends the pledge, so no ticket-holder could claim against him · decide · presses: prose, [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Thole hears Bram out without moving his thumb. When Bram asks to see the rest of the entry, he looks down at his own hand as if he'd forgotten it was there.

"That line is my own note, Master Halloway. It isn't one of his terms." He closes the ledger gently, the way you close a book on a sleeping cat, and keeps his palm on the cover. "The terms are all above it, and you've read them."

He takes the other questions in order, like items on a bill.

"A release from the mistress binds the mistress. It doesn't bind a stranger with a ticket in his hand. He'd come to me, not to her." He spreads his soft fingers. "Three hundred doesn't settle his claim. It pays for it. If he comes, I pay him out of the difference and I'm no worse off. That's what surety means."

"A declaration of loss, yes, that can be done. The court of contracts sits at the Song. You swear the ticket lost, the court posts notice on the temple door, and anyone holding it has a tenday to come forward." He gives Hesper a small, regretful look. "A tenday, mistress. Your term ends tonight."

Then Sefa speaks. At *the court would say so* his eyes go to her face, then to the green door behind him, then to the bolted street door. He wipes his palms down the front of his brown coat. Sefa is watching for it, and she sees what Bram can't from the side: the mention of a court doesn't frighten him. It interests him. Something in his face eases a little, like a man hearing a door he thought was locked turn out to be only shut.

Sefa presses the point gently, as if explaining it to a patient's family.

Sefa makes her argument quietly. Under *or* there are two roads, she says, and they don't meet. The heir's road ends the pledge, and a pledge that has ended can't be redeemed a second time by anybody. She doesn't press harder. She lets him see that she thinks he already knows it.

She gets through to him. Thole is quiet for a while, his hand flat on the closed ledger. When he speaks again, he is choosing his words carefully.

"You may be right, mistress. I think a court might well say so." He looks at Hesper, and for the first time he isn't explaining a bill. "But it's a court that has to say it, not me. If I say it and I'm wrong, I'm the one who pays. If the court says it…" He gives the street door a short look. "No one could quarrel with me for obeying the Song's own court. Not the bearer of any ticket. Not a buyer either."

He sets it out the way he'd set out terms. "Bring me a writing from the court of contracts, under the temple's seal, saying that the heir's redemption ends my ticket. Bring it before sundown and I'll take your hundred and twenty, mistress, on this counter, and give you the coffer and my receipt. No three hundred." He wipes his palms again, though only slightly this time. "Without it, the terms stand as written, and at sundown so does Master Ashby's offer."

Hesper hasn't moved. Her purse and her document case still lie on the counter between them. When she answers, she speaks to Thole, but her eyes go to Bram and Sefa.

"To get a writing from the court, someone has to ask the court for it, in my name. I told you this morning. My name doesn't go before the temple court." She says it as flatly as she said everything else. Then, more slowly: "I didn't say whose name could."

Thole waits behind his brass rail and doesn't open the ledger.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 6 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell lets the tiefling go from his mind. *A smith's hand owed money by the cooper's wife, and too proud or too frightened to knock. Not ours.* He keeps it in mind anyway, in case it turns out to matter.

The boy is the job. *Paid by the day, reports later. So someone's waiting for him to come and tell them where the widow went.* That's who Pell wants: whoever pays the boy, not the boy himself.

He stays flat and lets the morning pass, easing his weight now and then so the shingles don't creak. He doesn't go down to hurry things along. Every so often he looks to see where the boy's eyes go, whether toward Hesper's lane, toward the temple tower, or up the main street toward Thole's end. If someone stops at the trough to talk to the boy, Pell wants a good look at them: their face, their clothes, and which way they leave.

When the boy finally slides off the trough, Pell counts five breaths. Then he drops down the far side of the lean-to into the cooper's yard, out through the alley, and onto the main street two doors below. He picks the boy up from across the street and well back, with carts and people between them. He keeps his hood down, because hoods get noticed in daylight. He matches the boy's pace and never closes the distance. If the boy stops, Pell stops at a stall and looks at something. If the boy turns into a house or a yard, Pell doesn't follow him in. He notes the door and finds a spot across the way where he can watch who comes out.

If it comes close to the noon bell before the boy moves, Pell stays with the boy anyway. Bram and Sefa know where to look for him, and this is the one chance to see where the boy goes.

*(Stealth +9 to tail him in daylight on the main street, if you need a roll.)*
````

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Stealth: sitting still on the chandler's bench across the street while the man in grey looks out through the arch · roll · presses: prose, [Roll the die]

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Stealth: sitting still on the chandler's bench across the street while the man in grey looks out through the arch · decide · presses: prose, [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell stays flat on the shingles while the morning gets hot.

The sun comes over the cooper's roof. The shingles warm under his chest and smell of old tar. Every few minutes he moves his weight from one rafter to the next, slowly, and the roof keeps quiet. Hammers ring from the smithies. Carts go down the main street toward the Way and others come back up it. Twice a dog noses along the alley below him and moves on.

A little after ten the cooper's door opens again, and this time it's the cooper: a broad bald man in a leather apron. He and the tiefling stand close together on the step. Their voices are too low to carry, but Pell can read the hands well enough. The cooper spreads his palms, the tiefling holds out his half of the hazel tally, and the cooper goes back inside. He comes out with the other half and a small cloth bag. They fit the two halves together and the notches match. The cooper counts coins into the tiefling's palm. It takes him a while, and he counts some of them twice. The tiefling closes his hand, says something short, and walks off down the main street toward the Way. His jaw is still set, but he walks quickly now. The cooper looks at the empty step for a moment, then goes in and shuts the door.

The boy watched all of it with mild interest, the way he watches carts, and then forgot it.

He doesn't forget the lane. For two hours he does nothing a stable lad would do. He doesn't water a horse, fetch a bucket or run an errand. A woman with a basket goes down Hesper's lane and his eyes follow her until she knocks at a door three houses short of the Vallows'. After that they let her go. Once he looks up the main street toward Thole's end, and he looks for longer than idle curiosity would. Twice more he checks the temple tower. Nobody stops at the trough to talk to him. A girl selling cress offers him a bunch and he waves her off.

The sun is almost overhead when he moves. He looks at the tower one last time, slides off the trough, and wipes his hands down his breeches. Then he walks north up the main street, away from the temple and from Thole's, at an easy pace, like a lad going back to work.

Pell counts five breaths. He goes down off the far side of the lean-to into the cooper's yard among the stacked staves, out through the alley, and onto the main street two doors below the trough. When he gets there, the boy is about a hundred feet ahead with two carts and a knot of smiths' wives between them. Pell crosses to the other side and keeps his hood down.

Following him isn't hard. The boy never looks back. He stops once, at a baker's shutter, and spends a copper on a bun. Pell stops at a cutler's board and turns a paring knife over in his hands until the boy moves on. Beregost's main street runs straight and busy, and Pell is one more man in it.

At the north end of town the houses thin out and the street widens toward the Trade Way. A coaching inn stands there with a deep arched carriageway and a painted sign of a wheat sheaf, its gold faded nearly to red. The boy goes under the arch without slowing down, like a lad who has walked under it a thousand times. Inside the arch is a cobbled yard with stable doors along one side and a row of horses' heads looking out over them.

Pell doesn't follow him in. Across the street a chandler has put a bench out under his awning. Pell sits on the end of it and settles there like a man waiting for someone inside the shop. Through the arch he can see a slice of the yard about sixty feet away: a mounting block, a water butt, and a side door into the inn.

The boy goes to the water butt and waits there. He waits the way he waited at the trough, except that now he keeps looking at the side door.

The noon bell rings out from the Song of the Morning at the far end of town. On the third stroke the side door opens.

The man who comes out is about forty and dressed for travel, but well. He wears a grey riding cloak thrown back from his shoulders and good boots, and his beard is cut close. He doesn't hurry. He stops in front of the boy and says something, and he smiles as he says it. The boy talks. Pell can't hear a word at this distance, so he watches the boy's hands. Three fingers go up. Then the boy points back down the street, south, toward the end where Hesper lives. Then two fingers. He slaps his left forearm flat and holds it out from his body, the way a man carries a shield. Then he points south again, and his finger tilts a little toward the temple end.

The man in grey listens without interrupting. When the boy is done, he takes a coin from his purse and puts it in the boy's palm. It's silver; it catches the sun. Then he asks one more question, short. The boy shrugs and draws something vague in the air beside his own hip, small and quick.

The man in grey turns his head and looks out through the arch at the street, slowly, the way a man reads a room he's just walked into.

Pell doesn't move, and that's what saves him. He doesn't look away or drop his eyes, and he doesn't straighten up. A man on a bench under an awning at noon looks at whatever's in front of him, so Pell keeps looking at the arch with the same flat, half-bored patience the boy had on the trough. He has one elbow on his knee and a heel of the chandler's light falling across his boots. He's just a fellow waiting for his wife to finish buying candles.

The grey man's eyes travel slowly along the street. They take in the farrier's boy across the way, two carters arguing over a wheel, the cress girl who has worked her way up here, and the chandler's bench. They pass over Pell as though he were part of the bench and go on to the far corner. A loaded hay wain rolls between them, and when it has passed, the man in grey is looking somewhere else.

Pell gets something else out of that minute. As the man in grey turns back toward the side door, he lifts his chin toward the upper storey of the inn. Pell's eyes follow it. A shutter is open on the first floor above the yard, and a man is sitting in the window with his boots up on the sill. He's big and sunburnt, with his sleeves rolled up, and there's a sword belt hung on the shutter-hook beside his head. The grey man raises two fingers to him without breaking his stride. The man in the window takes his boots off the sill and goes back into the room.

The man in grey goes in by the side door and shuts it behind him.

The boy stands by the water butt a moment longer and turns the silver over in his fingers. He puts it away somewhere inside his shirt. Then he finally does what a stable lad ought to have been doing all morning: he takes a fork from beside the stable door and goes in to the horses.

The last of the noon bell dies away over the rooftops. At the far end of town, Thole's step and the cooper's corner are where they were, and Pell is sitting on a chandler's bench at the other end of Beregost. Nobody inside the Red Sheaf has any idea he's there.

*(Out of game: that natural 20 earned Pell Heroic Inspiration.)*
````

Rolls: Pell Dunmore Stealth: sitting still on the chandler's bench across the street while the man in grey looks out through the arch 29 vs 15 success

## Round 7

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 7 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Mine can,” I tell Hesper. “I'll ask for a ruling on the clause as written, without naming you. If they require your name, I'll stop and come back to you.”

I turn to Thole. “Put that offer in writing, please: a ruling under the temple seal that the heir's redemption ends the ticket, delivered before sundown, and you'll release the coffer for one hundred and twenty. Copy the exact clause beneath it. Sign it.”

My voice stays matter-of-fact. “That way we bring back what you need, rather than spend the afternoon misunderstanding each other.”

To Hesper, I add, “Keep your money and papers with you for now. We should meet Pell outside before going to the Song.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa lets out a slow breath, the kind a healer gives when the fever has broken and the patient still isn't safe.

"That's fairer than three hundred," she says quietly to Thole. "You want the temple's voice, not ours. If the court says it, no one can call you faithless." She turns a little so Hesper can see her face. "It doesn't have to be your name. Bram carries your authority. A writing from the court can be asked in your behalf, and the seal goes on the paper—not on you. It's a question of the pledge, not a complaint against you. Your name only stays the applicant's."

She looks to Bram. "You could stand before the clerk at the Song with her authority in your hand and ask for a simple declaration: that the heir redeeming today ends the ticket. You don't have to argue with anybody. Just bring back their writing."

Then to Thole, gently but without yielding. "If we bring that before sundown, you'll take the hundred and twenty and hand across the coffer while we're still in the room. That is what you just said."

Hesper does not answer at once. Her hand stays flat on the wood beside her money.

"If that's all it is," she says at last, "then you'll find me at the temple steps after midday. I'll sign whatever they need me to sign—on the steps, under the door. But I won't climb to the court." She straightens the document case so its corners line up with the counter's edge. "That's as far as I'm willing to go, even for you."

Thole looks from her to the document case, then back to the green iron door behind him.

"Bring me a writing under the court's seal, saying that the heir's redemption ends my ticket," he says again, as if repeating a line he has only just learned. "Before sundown. Then I'll take a hundred and twenty, and I'll bring you the coffer from the strong room." His voice doesn't change. "I won't take the cost of it out of the mistress, and I won't invite Master Ashby in early. You have my word on that."

He doesn't open the ledger. Through the shutters behind the counter, the light has begun to move off the street. The bells have not rung yet, but the morning is gone.

Bram has the authority folded in his pack. The folded notice is with it. Hesper lifts her document case from the counter and holds it in both arms, waiting for Bram's plan.

Your journal, new:
+ Progress: Thole will redeem the coffer for 120 gp before sundown—but only on a writing from the temple court (the contracts court at the Song) under its seal, saying the heir's redemption ends his ticket
+ Lead: The temple Steps

Where you are: Thole's counting-house · Time: Day 1 · midday (true noon; the bell has not rung yet)
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hesper listens to Bram without looking away from him. When he says he'll stop and come back to her if the court asks for her name, she nods once.

"Agreed." She turns to Thole. "Write *the heir*. Not my name."

Sefa makes her case to Thole first and then to Hesper, in the same low voice. Thole hears it with his hands folded on the closed ledger. Hesper waits until Sefa has finished.

"The applicant is whoever stands at the clerk's table," she says. "Let that be Master Halloway."

When Sefa turns back to the counter and says *while we're still in the room*, Thole tips his head to her. "On this counter, with you standing where you stand now. I said it, and I'll write it."

He does what Bram asked. He takes a clean sheet from under the counter, uncaps his ink, and then opens the ledger again. This time he turns it toward himself. He copies the clause out of it with his left hand spread flat across the lower half of the page, the way a man shields a hand of cards. He writes slowly, in the same careful hand as the notice, and when he finishes he turns the sheet round on the brass rail so Bram can read it:

*I, Gaspar Thole, lender on surety of Beregost, undertake as follows. If before sundown of this day there is brought to this counter a writing of the court of contracts of the Song of the Morning, under the seal of that court, declaring that the redemption of the coffer by the lawful heir of Master A. Vallow ends the ticket, I will accept one hundred and twenty gold pieces and deliver the said coffer at once, in the presence of the bearer. The clause, as written in my book: "redeemable until sundown of the term by the bearer of my ticket, or my lawful heir producing it." G. Thole, in his own hand.*

Then he writes it out again, word for word, on a second sheet. He sands both. One goes into the ledger between the pages, and he closes the ledger on it. He passes the other across the rail to Bram.

"That one's for the court and for this counter," he says. "I'd sooner it didn't go round the street." His eyes go to the bolted door and come back. "The court of contracts sits in the chapter house behind the temple. Its door is to the left of the great steps. Ask for the warden of contracts. He hears petitions himself."

Hesper keeps her eyes on the paper in Bram's hand while Thole talks about the steps.

When Bram tells her to keep the money and the papers, she lifts the drawstring purse off the counter and puts it inside her cloak. She takes the document case back under her arm.

"I'll go home," she says. "If the court asks for my hand, that's where you'll find me."

Thole stays behind his rail and makes no move toward the street door until someone asks him to. Through the grille a cart goes by outside, and somewhere down the street a smith's hammer starts up again. It isn't ten yet. There are hours left before noon, when you're due to meet Pell at Thole's step or the cooper's corner, and longer still before sundown.

*(Out of game: Hesper and Thole are mine to play, so their answers differ from what Sefa's paragraph had them say. In this scene it's still a little before ten; the noon bell happened only in Pell's thread.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) Bram's request for a ruling isn't made yet. He has to stand before the warden of contracts at the Song first, and the court's answer gets decided when he does.

2) Thole putting his offer in writing needed no roll. He had already made that offer aloud and was glad to have the court carry it, so he wrote and signed it, as the story shows.

3) Keeping the money with Hesper happened: she put the purse inside her cloak and took her case back. Meeting Pell is a plan, and it gets played when Bram and Sefa leave Thole's.

4) Sefa's words needed no new roll. She was holding Thole to terms her earlier Persuasion had already won, and he agreed, then wrote "in the presence of the bearer" into the paper.
````
