### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 7 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell gives it a slow count of thirty after the side door shuts. Then he stands, stretches like a man tired of waiting, and steps into the chandler's shop out of the sun.

He picks out the cheapest tallow dip on the board and puts two coppers down for it. He keeps his voice easy, one tradesman's gossip to another.

"Your neighbour's got quality in." He tips his head toward the arch. "Grey cloak, good boots, beard cut like a Waterdhavian's. Looks like a man who buys beeswax, not this." He turns the dip in his fingers. "Been here long? He and that big lad in the upstairs window?"

He doesn't name Ashby. If the chandler offers a name, Pell lets it go by like it means nothing to him. Whatever he gets, he thanks the man, pockets the candle, and goes.

He doesn't go back down the main street the way he came. He takes the first side lane that runs south, behind the houses, then cuts back onto the main street halfway down. Twice he stops in a doorway, as if to shake a stone from his boot, and watches the way he came for anyone who stops when he stops.

Then he heads for Thole's step, and the cooper's corner after that if the others aren't there. He's late by the bell, and he said the rest would come looking.

When he finds Bram and Sefa, he keeps it short and quiet:

"Your watcher's a stable lad at the Red Sheaf, north end. Paid in silver by a man in a grey riding cloak, about forty, travelling money. He's got a sellsword upstairs with his boots on the sill. The lad told him all three of you went out, the shield, and that you turned toward the temple end. Then the grey man asked him something, and the lad made a little sign at his hip." He shows it: small and quick. "I'd lay odds that's Ashby. How did Thole take the money?"

*(Out of game: I'll keep the Inspiration. If the chandler wants a roll to loosen up, Pell offers to buy something better than tallow.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell sits out a slow count of thirty after the side door closes. Then he gets up, rolls the stiffness out of his shoulders like a man who has given up on his wife ever finishing, and steps under the awning into the chandler's shop.

Inside it's dim and smells of tallow and warm wax. Candles hang by their wicks in bunches from the beams. A lean man in his fifties stands behind a board scarred with knife cuts. His cuffs are crusted white with drips and he has a pencil behind one ear. He watches Pell take the cheapest dip off the board and put two coppers down beside it. He sweeps them into his palm without looking at them.

At the quip about beeswax he gives a short laugh through his nose.

"Beeswax? Not him, friend. Lamp oil." He jerks his thumb toward the arch across the street. "Two flasks yesterday, the good clear stuff, and a new tinderbox. Paid on the nail. He's been at the Sheaf near a tenday. Iron buyer, they say. The forges down the street like him, because he pays ahead." He takes the pencil from behind his ear and taps a slate hanging on a nail by the door. Among the chalked sums someone has written in a neat, sloping hand: *Ashby & Corve. 2 fl. oil. Paid.* "Wrote that himself, when I said I like a name for my book. Like he was a firm." He puts the pencil back. "The big one upstairs came in with him. So did another one, a thin fellow who rides out early and comes back after dark. The big one sits in that window all day like a cat on a wall. Never buys so much as a wick."

Pell lets the name go past as though it were just another sum on the slate. He thanks the man, puts the dip in his coat and goes out into the glare.

He doesn't take the main street. A little way south he finds a side lane between a saddler's and a bakehouse. It runs behind the houses past middens, washing lines and a goat tied to a cart wheel, and he follows it. At the first bend he stops in a gateway, bends to his boot and shakes out a stone that isn't there. The lane behind him is empty except for the goat.

Halfway down the town he cuts back through a passage onto the main street. He stops in a cobbler's doorway and fusses with his boot again, and watches the street.

The sunburnt man is out on the main street, coming south. He has his sleeves rolled down now and a plain brown jerkin on, and the sword belt is buckled at his hip instead of hanging on a shutter-hook. He walks down the middle of the street at an easy, rolling pace, like a man with somewhere to be and plenty of time to get there. He doesn't look into side lanes or doorways. He looks ahead, toward the temple end. He passes the cobbler's doorway about thirty feet off and never turns his head. He isn't following anyone. He's going somewhere.

Pell lets him get well ahead before he steps out. He doesn't have to follow, because they're going the same way.

By the time the Song of the Morning's tower looms over the rooftops, it's getting on for one o'clock. Pell can see Thole's narrow stone front with its brass-bound door and painted board. The grille is shut. Across the street and three doors up there's an alehouse with a serving hatch on the street and a bench under it. The sunburnt man stops at the hatch and buys a cup. He sits down on the end of the bench, stretches his legs out and drinks slowly. From where he sits he can see Thole's door, the street in both directions, and past the last roofs the foot of the temple's great steps.

He looks like a man settling in for the afternoon. Thole's step is about a hundred feet ahead of Pell, in plain view of the bench.

*(Out of game: Bram and Sefa's thread is still a little before ten. Whether they're on Thole's step or at the cooper's corner at one o'clock depends on their next rounds, so the threads join when theirs catches up. From here, what Pell does with the man on the bench is up to you.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) Pell's count of thirty needed no roll. Nothing was uncertain, and he did it before stepping under the awning, as the turn shows.

2) Buying the dip and chatting needed no roll. Easy gossip with a shopkeeper who's just been paid is routine for Pell, and the chandler had nothing to hide. He gave the name *Ashby & Corve* off his own slate without Pell asking for it.

3) Checking for followers needed no roll. Nobody was following him, so there was nothing to beat. He stopped twice, and the lane and the street were clear. The sunburnt man walked past him on his way somewhere else and never looked his way.

4) Pell got within sight of Thole's step, about 100 feet off, and the turn stopped there. Where Bram and Sefa are at one o'clock is decided by their own rounds, and their thread is still just before ten. Now that a hired sword sits watching that door, whether Pell walks onto the step is his call.
````

Together after: [Pell Dunmore]

## Round 8

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 8 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: I read the undertaking through, then fold it carefully into my pack.

“Thank you. The door, please, Master Thole.”

Once we're outside, I speak quietly to Hesper and Sefa. “We'll walk you home first. It's barely a detour, and you have the redemption money on you.”

On the walk, I ask Hesper, “The undertaking names Aubrey. May I show it to the warden, provided I neither name you nor present your authority? I don't want to keep the letter of our promise and break its meaning.”

I glance to Sefa. “Then the chapter house, if you're willing. We'll return here by noon for Pell.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa gives Thole a last small nod, the kind that closes a bargain without haste.

"You've kept your side of the counter clean, and we'll keep ours." She lets her hands stay in sight as she steps back from the rail. "If Ashby comes early and asks why his offer isn't the one you took before sundown, tell him the term isn't ended yet."

She turns with the others, but while Bram takes the folded undertaking she glances once, without any obvious insistence, at the closed ledger under Thole's palm. Not at the clause—at the parts he shielded. She doesn't reach for it, only files it away.

Outside, on the step, she keeps her voice down and her eyes on the street. "To the corner, then. If Pell isn't there when the bell goes, one of us should go for Hesper's door and one to the temple steps." A pause. "He may have followed the tiefling. Let's hope he bought one of us something more than a sore foot."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Thole hears Sefa out with his hand still flat on the closed ledger.

"Until sundown the term is hers, mistress. I'll tell anyone who asks exactly that." He comes out from behind the rail. Before he touches the bolts he looks out through the grille at the street, the way a man checks the weather. Then he draws both bolts and opens the door onto the noise of the main street. "Before sundown," he says to Bram, and nothing more. The door shuts behind them, and the first bolt slides home before they've left the step.

Sefa had looked once at the ledger as they turned to go. Thole kept his palm across the cover the whole time. Under it the corner of the second copy stuck out between the pages, the one he'd closed in there himself. That was all there was to see.

On the step Hesper settles the document case under her arm and listens to Bram.

"Home, then." She doesn't argue about the detour. She sets off the way she came, at the same quick, even pace, with the purse inside her cloak and one hand over it.

They walk back up the main street toward the cooper's corner. As they come level with the cooper's, the shop door opens and a broad, bald man in a leather apron steps out onto the step. The tiefling in the road-dusty coat is still there. He takes something from inside his coat: half a split hazel stick, notched across. The cooper fits his own half against it, the two halves line up, and he starts counting coins into the tiefling's palm out of a cloth bag. Bram spent ten years on the Way watching drovers get paid off at the end of a run, and he knows what this is. It's a tally, and a man who has waited a good while for his wages is finally being paid them. Sefa sees it too. If Pell followed anyone this morning, it wasn't this man.

The stable lad is still on the trough. He watches the three of them turn down the lane, mildly, the way he watches carts.

Halfway down the lane Bram asks his question. Hesper doesn't slow her pace or turn her head to answer.

"Show it. Aubrey's name was in Thole's book before mine was anywhere." A few steps go by. "The court knows who his heir is. They proved the will in the spring, so my name went into their book once already. Once is enough." She shifts the case under her arm. "My letter is for treating with Thole. Keep it for Thole. If the warden wants to know who you're acting for, tell him you're acting on the undertaking. That's true."

At her own door she takes a key from her cloak, opens the lock and stands on the threshold with the door half open behind her. She puts her inked hand flat on the oak.

"I'll be here until sundown. If the court wants anything from me, send word and I'll come down to the step and sign it. Not past the step." She looks at Bram, then at Sefa. "The chapter house is open from the second bell. Thole was right about the door. It's on the left of the great steps."

Then she goes in, and they hear the bar drop on the inside.

It's about a quarter past ten. The lane rings with hammers, and the noon bell is a long way off.

*(Out of game: I left this out of Pell's thread. From the roof he would have seen Bram, Sefa and Hesper come back down the lane a little after ten, just before the cooper paid the tiefling. He saw it, and it changes nothing he did.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 8 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't step out of the cobbler's doorway. From there he sweeps the street ahead slowly: the painted shield, a grey coat beside it, Sefa. He checks Thole's step, the alehouse hatch, and the mouth of every lane between here and the temple.

*Sitting on Thole's door with the steps in view. So Ashby knows where the widow went, and now he wants to know whether she comes out with anything.* Then he thinks of the slate. *Lamp oil, the good clear stuff, and a new tinderbox. A man buying iron doesn't need either.* He doesn't like what that adds up to, with a coffer sitting in a moneylender's back room.

He won't walk the last hundred feet past that bench. If the others aren't in sight, he backs out the way he came, through the passage and down the lane behind the houses. Then he circles south and east to come at the cooper's corner from the cart alley behind the smithies, out of the sunburnt man's view. If they've given up on Thole's step, that's where they'll be, or on their way back to Hesper's.

If he does spot them on the street and they're heading toward Thole's or the bench, he doesn't wave or call. He drifts into their path from a side lane, falls in beside Bram for three steps, and says low, without looking at him: "Don't look at the alehouse bench. Big sunburnt man with a cup. He's Ashby's, and he's watching that door. Keep walking. Cooper's yard, back way."

*(Passive Perception 14 for the others on the street. Does Thole's shop have a back way or a yard I can see from a side lane on the way round? I'd note it for later without going near it.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell stays in the cobbler's doorway with one boot up on the step and looks down the street, one stretch at a time.

The sunburnt man is on the alehouse bench with his cup, his legs stretched out into the street. A carter has to swing wide of his boots and swears at him, and the man doesn't pull them in. Across the way Thole's brass-bound door is shut and the grille is closed. While Pell watches, the grille slides open a finger's width. Pale eyes look out at the street, settle on the bench for a long moment, then the grille closes again. The man on the bench doesn't seem to notice, or he doesn't care whether he's seen.

The rest of the street is ordinary early afternoon. A smith's wife has a basket of loaves on her hip. Two Amnian-looking drovers argue over a halter. A boy is chasing a hen out of a doorway. Between the cobbler's and the temple Pell picks out the mouth of every side lane: a passage by a farrier's, a gap with washing strung across it, a covered way under a bakehouse. Nobody is loitering in any of them. On the stretch of street he can see, there's no brown-painted shield, no grey coat and no Sefa.

So he backs out the way he came, back through the passage onto the lane behind the houses, past the goat on its cart wheel. From there he turns east and south behind the houses on the smithies' side. The back lanes are midden-walls, kitchen doors, and a woman scrubbing a pot on her step who doesn't look up. After a few minutes the lane narrows between two yard walls and comes out into the cart alley behind the smithies, which is rutted and black with coal grit, the same as it was this morning. The bellows boy is still at his handle.

The cooper's yard has a footway behind it. Its staves are stacked and the lean-to roof where Pell lay is empty in the sun. The footway runs on toward the temple end along the backs of the main-street houses, too narrow for a cart. Pell doesn't go down it. He stops at its mouth as if to retie a lace and counts the back walls by their chimneys.

The fifth wall past the cooper's stands higher than its neighbours, close to ten feet of grey stone with fresh limewash. Broken crockery is set in mortar along the top, the way careful men in the Lower City top their walls. In it there's a single narrow door, iron-studded, flush with the stone, with no handle on this side that he can see. Above it, on the upper floor, is one shuttered window. Low down near the foot of the wall, just above the dirt of the footway, is a small square vent, barred with three iron rods, black inside. Nothing about it says what room it breathes for. All the other houses along that row have kitchen doors and slop-drains, and this one has neither.

Pell finishes with the lace and walks the last few yards up the alley to the cooper's yard gate. Past the corner of the cooper's house he can see the main street. The trough at the top of Hesper's lane stands empty. Nobody's sitting on its rim now, and there's only a wet ring on the stone where someone set a bucket down. Down the lane Hesper's door is shut and her shutters are closed. It's a little past a quarter after one.

*(Out of game: Bram and Sefa weren't on the stretch of street Pell could see at that moment. Where they are at one o'clock, and whether they're at the cooper's corner, depends on their own thread, which is still at about a quarter past ten. No roll was needed: Pell was already unseen, and he backed away without anyone looking his way.)*
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text

## Round 9

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 9 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: I wait until the bar settles, then turn toward the main street with Sefa.

“The tiefling was waiting for wages. Saw his tally match the cooper's. The boy's still idle at the trough, though. We'll tell Pell when we meet him.”

I keep my voice low as we walk. “At the court, I'll show Thole's undertaking and ask whether they can give the ruling it requires. No authority letter, no petition in Hesper's name.”

I head for the chapter house door to the left of the temple steps. Once inside, I approach whoever receives petitioners.

“Bram Halloway. I'd like to speak with the warden of contracts about a pledge that falls due at sundown. The lender has put the question in writing.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa watches the door until the bar drops, then turns to Bram.

"Then the chapter house now. If its door isn't open yet, we sit on the great steps and watch the morning go—there are worse places to think." She starts back toward the main street, keeping close to the lane's far side. "When we're inside, let me put the question as a point of law before you hand over anything with your name on it. A dead notary's pledge, a lender's written undertaking, and the clause itself. The warden can rule on that, if he's the kind who likes a clean line. And we want his seal, not his pity."

She glances at the temple roof ahead, then at Bram's pack. "Thole swore no word of Hesper and no word of the widow. But he knows the heir is established. If the warden asks whose interest is served, tell him it's the repayment of a debt and the return of property—both of which the court has an interest in seeing done before sundown."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hesper's bar drops into its brackets with a dull knock. Bram turns back up the lane, and Sefa falls in beside him on the far side, close to the wall.

She hears him out about the tiefling and the boy, then nods once at the trough as they pass it. The boy is still sitting there. His eyes follow the mail up to the corner and let it go, the way they did before.

On the main street Sefa keeps her voice under the noise of the carts and lays out what she'd like to do: put the question as a point of law, and ask for the seal rather than the warden's sympathy. Then she adds what to say if anyone asks whose interest is served: a debt repaid, property returned, both before sundown.

The Song of the Morning stands at the south end of town, where the main street widens into a square paved in pale stone. The temple is rose-coloured granite, with a tower and a great flight of steps going up to doors painted gold. A novice with a broom is working down the steps one at a time, and a pair of drovers have stopped at the bottom to drink from the fountain. As Bram and Sefa cross the square the bell starts up in the tower: two strokes, then quiet. It's the second bell.

The door Thole told them about is set into the chapter house wall, left of the great steps. It's a plain oak door with a sun-disc carved over the lintel, and as they reach it a lay brother opens it from inside and props it with a wedge. Beyond is a whitewashed antechamber with benches along two walls and a high window. A young brother in a saffron tabard sits at a table with an open book, a sand-box and a rack of pens. Two smiths' men are already on one bench, arguing in low voices over a docket for charcoal and how many sacks were wet.

Bram goes to the table. The young brother looks up, hears him out, and dips his pen.

"Bram Halloway," he repeats as he writes it. "A pledge, due at sundown. The lender's writing." He sands the line and turns the book a little so Bram can see that's all he has put down. "The warden takes them in order. Those two are first." He tips his pen at the charcoal men.

The wait is short. The smiths' men go in through an inner door, their voices rise once behind it, and they come out a quarter of an hour later. One of them looks pleased and the other is still counting sacks on his fingers. Then the inner door opens again.

The warden of contracts is a lean man of about sixty, bald, with the tops of his ears burnt red by some summer of field work long ago. He wears a plain saffron robe, with ink up the side of his right hand like Hesper's. He looks at Bram's mail and shield, and then at Sefa's hands, which are folded quietly in front of her. He looks at those a moment longer than he looks at her face.

"Brother Lucan Pryce. I'm the warden." He holds the door for them. "Come in, Master Halloway. Mistress…?" He waits for Sefa's name and gives her a small nod when he has it.

The room behind the door is small, with a long table, three chairs and shelves of court books bound in red. A window looks onto the temple garden, where someone is tying up beans. Brother Lucan sits down on the far side of the table and holds out his hand for the paper. He doesn't ask what it is first.

Bram gives him Thole's undertaking. The warden reads it once quickly, then a second time slowly with his finger under the lines. At *Master A. Vallow* the finger stops.

"Aubrey Vallow." He says it to the page. "He drew half the leases this house holds. I proved his will myself in the spring." He squares the sheet on the table with two fingers and looks up. "So this is the widow's pledge. And Gaspar Thole wants a writing under my seal, so that the court takes the blame if a ticket turns up later and he doesn't."

He doesn't sound annoyed. He sounds like a man who has read a hundred papers like this one.

"I can rule on a clause. I've done it before. A ruling of the court costs two gold, and it goes into the court's book under the name of the pledge." He puts his hand flat on the undertaking. "But moneylenders don't usually come to me when they're only afraid of a lost ticket. They come when two people want the same thing and they don't want to be the one who chooses between them." He looks from Bram to Sefa and back. "So before I write anything, tell me. Who else wants this coffer?"
````

The room behind the door is small, with a long table, three chairs and shelves of court books bound in red. A window looks onto the temple garden, where someone is tying up beans. Brother Lucan sits down on the far side of the table and holds out his hand for the paper. He doesn't ask what it is first.

You give him Thole's undertaking. The warden reads it once quickly, then a second time slowly with his finger under the lines. At *Master A. Vallow* the finger stops.

"Aubrey Vallow." He says it to the page. "He drew half the leases this house holds. I proved his will myself in the spring." He squares the sheet on the table with two fingers and looks up. "So this is the widow's pledge. And Gaspar Thole wants a writing under my seal, so that the court takes the blame if a ticket turns up later and he doesn't."

He doesn't sound annoyed. He sounds like a man who has read a hundred papers like this one.

"I can rule on a clause. I've done it before. A ruling of the court costs two gold, and it goes into the court's book under the name of the pledge." He puts his hand flat on the undertaking. "But moneylenders don't usually come to me when they're only afraid of a lost ticket. They come when two people want the same thing and they don't want to be the one who chooses between them." He looks from you to Sefa and back. "So before I write anything, tell me. Who else wants this coffer?"
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram waits until the bar settles, then turns toward the main street with you.

"The tiefling was waiting for wages. Saw his tally match the cooper's. The boy's still idle at the trough, though. We'll tell Pell when we meet him."

He keeps his voice low as you walk. "At the court, I'll show Thole's undertaking and ask whether they can give the ruling it requires. No authority letter, no petition in Hesper's name."

Hesper's bar drops into its brackets with a dull knock. Bram turns back up the lane, and you fall in beside him on the far side, close to the wall.

You hear him out about the tiefling and the boy, then nod once at the trough as you pass it. The boy is still sitting there. His eyes follow Bram's mail up to the corner and let it go, the way they did before.

On the main street you keep your voice under the noise of the carts.

The Song of the Morning stands at the south end of town, where the main street widens into a square paved in pale stone. The temple is rose-coloured granite, with a tower and a great flight of steps going up to doors painted gold. A novice with a broom is working down the steps one at a time, and a pair of drovers have stopped at the bottom to drink from the fountain. As you and Bram cross the square the bell starts up in the tower: two strokes, then quiet. It's the second bell.

The door Thole told you about is set into the chapter house wall, left of the great steps. It's a plain oak door with a sun-disc carved over the lintel. As you reach it, a lay brother opens it from inside and props it with a wedge. Beyond is a whitewashed antechamber with benches along two walls and a high window. A young brother in a saffron tabard sits at a table with an open book, a sand-box and a rack of pens. Two smiths' men are already on one bench, arguing in low voices over a docket for charcoal and how many sacks were wet.

Bram goes to the table. "Bram Halloway. I'd like to speak with the warden of contracts about a pledge that falls due at sundown. The lender has put the question in writing."

The young brother looks up, hears him out, and dips his pen.

"Bram Halloway," he repeats as he writes it. "A pledge, due at sundown. The lender's writing." He sands the line and turns the book a little so Bram can see that's all he has put down. "The warden takes them in order. Those two are first." He tips his pen at the charcoal men.

The wait is short. The smiths' men go in through an inner door, their voices rise once behind it, and they come out a quarter of an hour later. One of them looks pleased and the other is still counting sacks on his fingers. Then the inner door opens again.

The warden of contracts is a lean man of about sixty, bald, with the tops of his ears burnt red by some summer of field work long ago. He wears a plain saffron robe, and there is ink up the side of his right hand like Hesper's. He looks at Bram's mail and shield, and then at your hands, which are folded quietly in front of you. He looks at those a moment longer than he looks at your face.

"Brother Lucan Pryce. I'm the warden." He holds the door for you. "Come in, Master Halloway. Mistress…?" He waits for your name and gives you a small nod when he has it.

He looks from Bram to you and back. "So before I write anything, tell me. Who else wants this coffer?"

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 9 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell stands at the cooper's gate a moment longer and looks at the empty trough and the wet ring on the stone.

*Nobody here. They went to Thole's, and the grille's shut. So either he turned them away and they went home, or they're out looking for me, which is what I told them to do.* He hopes it's the first. Three people searching Beregost for a man nobody's supposed to notice would undo his whole morning.

He doesn't take the lane. He goes back down the cart alley, past the bellows boy, to the door under the pear tree. It's still nearly shut, the way he left it. He listens at the gap first for voices in the yard or the study. Then he lifts the door on its hinges again so it won't scrape, slips through, and draws it closed and slides the bolt home behind him.

At the study's back window he raps twice on the frame, softly, so whoever is inside knows it's him and not the lane.

If they're inside, he comes in and keeps it quiet:

"Sorry I'm late. I was following the lad." He tells it plainly. "The watcher's a stable boy at the Red Sheaf, north end. A man in a grey riding cloak pays him in silver at the noon bell. The chandler across the way has him chalked on his slate as *Ashby & Corve*. He's an iron buyer by his own account, been there near a tenday. He bought two flasks of good lamp oil and a new tinderbox yesterday." He lets that sit. "He has two men with him. A thin one rides out early and comes back after dark. The big sunburnt one is on the alehouse bench across from Thole's right now with a cup, watching the door and the temple steps. Thole's seen him through the grille."

He looks at Bram, then Hesper. "So tell me about Thole."

If the house is empty, he doesn't wait in it. He writes a single line on a scrap from the desk, *Back by the yard. Waiting at the cooper's gate. P.*, and leaves it under the purse's dent on the desk. Then he goes out the way he came and keeps watch on the trough from the yard gate.

*(Out of game: Thole's back wall is the fifth chimney past the cooper's. It's high, limewashed and topped with crockery, with a flush iron-studded door and a barred vent near the ground. I'm noting it, not going near it.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell goes back down the cart alley the way he came. The bellows boy is still working his handle and doesn't look up. The door under the pear tree stands as he left it, a finger's width open. He listens at the gap. The yard is quiet except for a wasp working at the windfalls in the grass. No voices come from the house.

He lifts the door on its hinges, slips through, and lets it settle closed without a scrape. The bolt is easier going home than it was coming back, now that the rust has been worked. He crosses the yard past the rain barrel and raps twice, softly, on the frame of the study's back window.

For a moment nothing happens. Then a floorboard creaks somewhere inside. The study door opens a crack, and then all the way. Hesper comes in still wearing her grey coat, though the cloak is off. She holds the iron poker from the cold hearth low against her skirt. When she sees who's at the glass she puts it back where it came from, unhurried, and unlatches the window.

"Master Dunmore." Her eyes take in the coal grit on his knees and the tar on his coat front. "They aren't here."

She rests her inked hand flat on the sill.

"They brought me home at a quarter past ten. Thole won't take my hundred and twenty without a ticket, unless the temple's court of contracts gives him a writing under its seal saying the heir's redemption ends the ticket. If they bring that before sundown, he takes the money and hands over the coffer on his counter. He put it in writing. Master Halloway carries it." Behind her, two piles of seventeen gold lie on the desk where they were left this morning. "They went from my door to the chapter house. I haven't seen either of them since."

She glances once past him at the yard door, at the bolt he has just slid home, and then back to his face.

"The trough has been empty since noon. I looked from upstairs." She doesn't ask whether that was his doing. She asks the other thing. "Did you find out who sends him?"

*(Out of game: Bram and Sefa aren't here, so neither of the plans you set fits exactly. Hesper is home alone, and what you tell her is up to you.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) This happened and needed no roll. Pell went back down the alley, bolted the yard door behind him and rapped twice on the study window, and Hesper came to it.

2) Pell's report was meant for when Bram and Sefa were inside. They aren't there, and Hesper ended the turn by asking Pell who sends the watcher, so what he tells her and how much is your answer to give.

3) That line belonged to the same "if they're inside" plan, so Pell didn't say it. Hesper told him Thole's terms herself anyway: the court's sealed writing, the hundred and twenty, and the undertaking Bram carries.

4) The house isn't empty because Hesper is home, so the branch with the note and the wait at the cooper's gate didn't happen. Pell is still free to leave a note or go back to the gate after he answers her.

*(Correction: I wrote that two piles of seventeen lie on the desk. Bram took his share this morning, so only one pile is there, Sefa's seventeen gold.)*
````

## Round 10

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 10 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Master Corwen Ashby. Thole says he's offered two hundred and fifty gold, payable at sundown. He hasn't asked Thole about a ticket. That's all we know of his claim.”

I leave the undertaking on the table.

“Thole first asked three hundred to release the coffer without the ticket. Then he agreed to the original hundred and twenty if we brought your ruling. He seemed concerned about the buyer as well as any later bearer.”

I take out two gold pieces, but pause before setting them down.

“I can pay the fee. First, I need to be clear: I'm asking in my own name, on this undertaking. Would the entry name only Aubrey's pledge and me as applicant, without entering his widow as a petitioner?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa sits when she is asked, and sets her folded hands on the table's edge as though the whitewashed room were an extension of the hospice.

"Another man wants it," she says evenly. "By the lender's account, a buyer with more money and no ticket. He's offered more, and by the terms he can't so much as touch the coffer until the term ends at sundown. So he hasn't come to this hearing. If he had, you'd have two people at the table."

She doesn't say the name. She lets it lie with the rest of what the warden already knows.

"A widow has come to a lender to pay what the pledge says. The lender won't take it, not because she's failed the terms, but because the paper he wrote is two ways round at once. He's written us a promise instead: if the court gives a true reading of his own clause, he'll accept the lawful redemption. That's the whole quarrel. It's a matter of what the words mean before sundown." She pauses. "Whatever the coffer holds, and whoever else would like to carry it off, no one at this table is asking you to choose between two people. We're asking you to say what the clause means."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Lucan listens to Bram without interrupting. When Bram gets to the name, the warden's eyes stay on the undertaking, not on Bram.

"Ashby." He says it the way a man checks a figure he already half expected. "I've heard that name from the forges these ten days. Not at this table. A buyer who orders that much iron would usually have his contracts witnessed here, so the smiths can sue him if he doesn't pay. None of his have come to me." He leaves it there.

Bram takes out two gold pieces and holds them, waiting for his answer. Lucan glances at the coins and then back at the paper.

"The entry would read *the pledge of A. Vallow, deceased, to G. Thole*, with you as applicant, Master Halloway. The heir needn't be petitioned. She went into this court's book in the spring, when I proved the will. The book doesn't need her name a second time." Then: "Keep your two gold for now. I'm not sure yet that I have anything to write."

He turns to Sefa, and he gives her argument the attention he gave the paper. He doesn't say anything until she has finished.

"Two roads under *or*. That's well put, Mistress Marrin, and it's how the widow would want it read." He sets one finger under the copied clause and reads it aloud, slowly, the way he must read clauses back to every quarrelling party who sits in that chair. "*Redeemable until sundown of the term by the bearer of my ticket, or my lawful heir producing it.*" His finger stops on the last word. "*Producing it.* A clause can't leave *it* hanging. It has to point back to something, and the only thing before it is the ticket. The bearer of the ticket may redeem. So may the heir, if she produces the ticket. The clause doesn't set out two roads, one with a ticket and one without. It says who may hold the ticket."

He lifts his finger off the page.

"I can't write that the heir's redemption ends the ticket when the clause asks the heir for the ticket. If I put that under the seal, I'd be writing a different contract from the one Aubrey made, and Gaspar Thole would get exactly the shield he came here for." He squares the undertaking with two fingers and slides it back across the table to Bram. "If the heir produces the ticket, I'll gladly rule that her redemption ends every other claim on that coffer, Master Ashby's included, and I'll seal it. Without the ticket, all this court can offer is a declaration of loss, and you've heard how long that takes."

Through the window behind him, the brother in the bean rows straightens up and wipes his face.

"By Thole's own account, Aubrey wrote that clause himself." Lucan's voice stays dry, but he speaks more slowly now. "He drew half the leases in this house, and in twenty years I never caught him writing a word he didn't mean. If he wrote *producing it*, he knew where *it* would be." He looks from Bram to Sefa. "Do you?"

*(Out of game: no roll this time. Lucan is the court's own expert reading his own law, and his reading isn't a check. Sefa is still free to argue the clause with him.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 10 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays at the window rather than climbing in. He keeps his voice low, though the yard is empty.

"I did. The boy's a stable lad at the Red Sheaf, north end. At the noon bell a man in a grey riding cloak paid him a silver piece to tell what he'd seen: three of you going out, the shield, which way you turned. The chandler across the street has him on his slate in his own hand: *Ashby & Corve*. He calls himself an iron buyer and he's been at the Sheaf near a tenday. So your Master Ashby is real, he's been watching this house, and he isn't alone." He holds up two fingers. "One big sunburnt sellsword. He's sitting outside the alehouse across from Thole's right now with a cup, where he can see Thole's door and the foot of the temple steps. And a thin one who rides out before light and comes back after dark."

He pauses, then says the part he doesn't like.

"Yesterday he bought two flasks of good lamp oil and a new tinderbox. An iron buyer with a room at an inn has no use for either. I don't know what he means by it. But I'd keep the shutters barred and the poker where you can reach it, and I wouldn't open the front door to anyone tonight who isn't one of us."

He nods past her at the desk.

"One pile of seventeen still there. Whose did they leave?" He doesn't wait long for the answer. "And one question before I go. *Corve.* Was that a name Aubrey ever said, or one you've seen on any paper in this house? It doesn't need to be the ledgers. A letter, a seal, a bill."

Whatever she answers, he steps back from the sill.

"I'm going to the chapter house to find them. If the court gives that writing, Ashby's man will see us come down the temple steps with it, and I'd rather we knew how we're getting from the steps to Thole's counter before we go out the door." He touches the scrap with the mark inside his coat. "If Sister Kell's anywhere near the chapter house, I may ask her about this on the way. Bolt the window behind me."

He goes back out under the pear tree and down the cart alley. Then he takes the back lanes toward the temple end and comes at the chapter house from the side away from the main street. He keeps out of sight of the alehouse bench and the foot of the great steps.

*(Out of game: if I need Stealth to reach the chapter house without the sunburnt man seeing me, I'll roll it. Passive Perception 14 on the approach: Bram's shield, Sefa, or the thin rider anywhere about the temple.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hesper hears him out at the open window and doesn't interrupt. At the lamp oil and the tinderbox her face doesn't change. She glances once, briefly, at the cold hearth behind her where the poker hangs.

"Oil and a tinderbox. Yesterday." She says it the way she'd read a line back from a register. "And the buyer named in Thole's notice is the man who pays a boy to watch my door."

She thinks about it for a moment.

"A man buying a coffer at sundown has no need to know who calls on me. Not unless he thinks the coffer isn't all there is." She doesn't say what else there might be. Her eyes go past Pell's shoulder to the shelves of bound registers along the study wall, and then come back.

"The seventeen is Mistress Marrin's. She said she'd take it when she'd earned it."

Then his last question. She takes longer over it than any of the others.

"Corve. No. Not from his mouth, and not in either day-book. I'd have seen it." She stops there, then goes on, because a notary's widow doesn't leave a statement half made. "If it's in this house at all, it's in the registers. Those are his clients' business. I told you so this morning." She leaves it at that and doesn't offer to open them.

At *don't open the door to anyone* she nods once. "It stays barred. It's been barred since the winter."

When Pell steps back from the sill she closes the window and latches it. Through the bubbled glass he sees her go to the study door. A moment later she comes out into the yard with her cloak back on and follows him to the door under the pear tree. He lifts it on its hinges again and slips through into the alley. Behind him the bolt slides home, quick and firm, from a hand that has done it before.

The cart alley is as it was. The bellows boy is on his handle and the coal grit is warm underfoot. Pell doesn't go out to the main street this time. He takes the back lanes south and east past middens and kitchen steps. A woman is beating a rug over a wall, and a cat watches him from a woodpile. The hammers drop behind him as the smithies give way to gardens.

The Song of the Morning comes up over the rooftops: rose granite, the tower catching the full afternoon sun. Its back is a long garden wall with a wicket gate in it. Over the wall Pell can see a brother in a straw hat bent over the bean rows, and beyond the garden a row of small high windows along the chapter house. He follows the wall round the temple's east flank, where the chapter house stands between him and the square. In this lane the only thing that can see him is the tower.

At the corner, where the lane opens onto the pale stone square, he stops in the shadow of a buttress. From here he can see the length of the chapter house wall to the plain oak door with the sun-disc over its lintel. It stands propped on its wedge, about forty feet from him. Beyond it the great steps go up to the gold doors. A novice is sweeping the top of the steps. Drovers are watering a pair of mules at the fountain.

Farther off, down the main street past the last roofs, he can just make out the alehouse hatch. The sunburnt man is still on the end of the bench, his legs stretched across the cobbles and a fresh cup at his elbow. From there he has a clear view of the foot of the steps. The forty feet of wall from the buttress to the chapter house door is almost all in the temple's shadow, and the steps stand between it and the bench.

The thin rider is nowhere in sight. Neither is anyone else Pell would know.

It's getting on for a quarter to two.

*(Out of game: no roll for the approach. A lone man the sunburnt man has never seen, coming round by the back lanes, is ordinary work for Pell. Whether Bram and Sefa are still inside when he walks through that door depends on their thread, which is still at about eleven, so I've stopped you at the buttress, forty feet short of it.)*

 *(Out of game: Pell's approach needed no roll: he's a stranger the sunburnt man has never seen, alone in the back lanes, and that's routine work for him. He stops at the buttress forty feet short of the door because Bram and Sefa are inside it in a thread that's still at about eleven, so he walks in once their scene catches up.)*
````

````text
Hesper hears you out at the open window and doesn't interrupt. At the lamp oil and the tinderbox her face doesn't change. She glances once, briefly, at the cold hearth behind her where the poker hangs.

"Oil and a tinderbox. Yesterday." She says it the way she'd read a line back from a register. "And the buyer named in Thole's notice is the man who pays a boy to watch my door."

She thinks about it for a moment.

"A man buying a coffer at sundown has no need to know who calls on me. Not unless he thinks the coffer isn't all there is." She doesn't say what else there might be. Her eyes go past your shoulder to the shelves of bound registers along the study wall, and then come back.

"The seventeen is Mistress Marrin's. She said she'd take it when she'd earned it."

Then your last question. She takes longer over it than any of the others.

"Corve. No. Not from his mouth, and not in either day-book. I'd have seen it." She stops there, then goes on, because a notary's widow doesn't leave a statement half made. "If it's in this house at all, it's in the registers. Those are his clients' business. I told you so this morning." She leaves it at that and doesn't offer to open them.

At *don't open the door to anyone* she nods once. "It stays barred. It's been barred since the winter."

When you step back from the sill she closes the window and latches it. Through the bubbled glass you see her go to the study door. ## Round 11

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “No. His widow searched his papers and found no ticket. We hoped the clause offered a lawful way without it. You've made that plain.”

I put the coins away and take back Thole's undertaking.

“His day-book records visits to your archive—‘Song. Archive. Sr. M.K.’ There's an unfamiliar mark beside one: a circle pierced by an upright stroke, hooked at the foot. Does that mean anything to you?”

I pause. “We'd like to speak with Sister Maudra Kell about those visits. Can you direct us to her?”

Sefa Marrin: (Sefa's player wrote nothing this round.)
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Lucan watches Bram put the two gold pieces away and slide Thole's undertaking back into his pack. He doesn't say anything about the court's fee. When Bram describes the mark, the warden takes a clean scrap from the stack at his elbow and holds out a pen.

"Draw it for me, if you would, Master Halloway. As near as you remember it."

Bram draws it: a circle, an upright stroke through it, a little hook at the foot. Lucan turns the scrap toward the window and looks at it longer than he looked at Thole's undertaking. Then he squares it on the table with two fingers.

"It isn't a mark of this court. When we seal, it's in saffron wax with the sun at its rising, and the sun has rays. This has none." He keeps his eyes on the drawing. "Beside the archive, you say. Not beside Thole."

He doesn't ask anything more about it. He gives the scrap back to Bram instead of keeping it, and gets up.

"Sister Maudra Kell keeps the archive over the east walk of the cloister. She doesn't keep petitioners' hours, and she doesn't care for being sent people." He opens the inner door and speaks to the young brother at the petition table. "Brother Teo. Take Master Halloway and Mistress Marrin up to the archive. Tell Sister Maudra the warden sends them about Master Vallow's pledge, and that I have written nothing." He turns back to them in the doorway. "If you find the ticket, bring it here before you take it to Thole's. The court would like to see it before he does."

Brother Teo leaves his pens and leads them through a door at the back of the antechamber. Sefa walks beside Bram and says nothing. They come out into a cloister: four covered walks around a square of clipped grass with a sundial in the middle, and the noise of the square shut out behind the chapter house. A stair at the corner of the east walk goes up between two walls, worn into a hollow in the middle of each step. At the top there's a low door bound in iron with a bell-pull beside it. Teo pulls it once and steps back as though he's done this before and didn't enjoy it.

It takes a while. Then the door opens inward on dry air and the smell of old vellum. A small woman of about seventy stands in the doorway. She is straight-backed in a saffron habit gone pale with washing, with ink-black cuffs pulled down over her wrists and a pair of round spectacles pushed up onto her forehead. Behind her the long room runs the length of the east walk, shelves to the ceiling and a reading table under a row of high windows.

Teo repeats the warden's message in one breath. Sister Maudra listens without looking at him. She is looking at Bram's shield, then at Sefa. Her eyes stay a moment on Sefa's folded hands, and then come back to Bram's face.

"Aubrey Vallow's pledge." She doesn't move out of the doorway. "Brother Lucan has written nothing, and he sends you to me." She lets the spectacles drop onto her nose. "Before I let you in among my shelves, who sent you to Lucan? Did his widow send you?"
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell takes a breath in the buttress's shadow, then walks. He doesn't creep, because a man creeping along a temple wall in daylight gets looked at. He walks with his hands loose and his hood down, like a townsman with temple business, and keeps close to the wall where the shade lies thickest. He checks the bench once from the corner of his eye as the steps come between them, and doesn't look again.

At the propped door he stops on the threshold. He lets his eyes get used to the dimness before he steps in, and takes in the room: who's at a desk, who's waiting on a bench, whether a painted shield is leaning against a wall anywhere.

If there's a doorkeeper or a clerk, he keeps it plain and polite and says no more than he has to:

"Good day to you, brother. I'm looking for two friends of mine who came about a matter for the court of contracts this morning. A big man in mail with a brown-painted shield, and a young woman with him. Are they still within, or did they go?"

If they're inside, he goes to them and drops his voice:

"Hesper told me about Thole's terms. Where does the court stand?" Then, before they answer: "Mind, when we come out. Ashby's sunburnt man is on the alehouse bench with a clear view of the foot of the steps. We don't go down the front with any paper in hand. There's a wicket in the garden wall on the east side. We leave that way."

He doesn't take out the scrap with the mark. Not yet, and not in front of a clerk. If someone mentions Sister Kell or the archive, he notes which way they nod and keeps it in mind.

*(Out of game: Passive Insight 14 on whoever answers me at the door. Do they react to "court of contracts" or to the Vallow name, if it comes up?)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell leaves the buttress at a townsman's pace, hands loose and hood down, and keeps to the strip of shade along the chapter house wall. Halfway along, the great steps come between him and the bench. He glances once from the corner of his eye. The sunburnt man's boots are still out across the cobbles, and his head is tipped back against the alehouse wall. Pell doesn't look again. Nobody calls out and nobody turns. At the propped door he stops on the threshold and waits for the dimness to clear from his eyes.

The antechamber is whitewashed and cool, with benches along two walls and a high window throwing a slab of light across the floor. There's no painted shield against any wall, and no mail. A stout woman sits on one bench with a sealed box on her knees, the kind a dowry goes in, and a younger man beside her keeps his hands folded and looks at nothing. The inner door is shut. Voices murmur behind it, too low to make out.

The young brother in the saffron tabard sits at the petition table with his pens and his sand-box. The book lies open in front of him. Pell reads upside down without thinking about it, the way he always has. Partway down the page, in a careful clerk's hand, is *Bram Halloway — a pledge, due at sundown. The lender's writing.* A few lines below it is another entry, *Thunderhammer, for the yard — a witnessing, an order*, and a single neat line has been ruled through it. Beside it in the margin are two words: *not pursued.*

The brother looks up and hears Pell out. Pell says *court of contracts*, and it means nothing to the brother, because that's his whole day. The description is different. His eyes go to the mail and the brown shield in his head, and he knows them at once.

"Master Halloway." He touches the line in the book with the end of his pen. "And Mistress Marrin. The warden saw them this morning, and then he sent me to take them up to Sister Maudra in the archive." When he says her name the corner of his mouth tightens, just once. It's the look of a junior who has climbed one stair too many times today, and nothing more than that. "That was before noon. They haven't come back through here. The cloister has other doors, though, so I couldn't swear they're still up there."

He looks at the shut inner door, then at the woman with the box, who is next.

"I can't send you into the cloister on your own. It isn't open from the street." He dips his pen and holds it over the book, though not over Bram's line. "If you give me your name, I'll take it up to the archive when the warden's done with these two. Or you can wait on the bench. Anyone coming down from the east walk comes past this table, unless they go out by the garden."

*(Out of game: Bram and Sefa aren't in the room, so your warning about the bench and the wicket hasn't been said yet. It's still yours to give when you find them.)*
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

## Round 12

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 12 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “She hired us to recover the coffer, yes. I went to Lucan on my own application, after Thole offered to release it if the court approved redemption without the ticket. Lucan couldn't approve it.”

I keep my voice low in the quiet cloister.

“We're looking for the ticket now. Aubrey's day-book records two visits to your archive. We'd like to know what he came to consult, and whether he left anything in your keeping.”

I leave the drawing in my pack for now.

Sefa Marrin: Sefa dips her head in a small, proper bow to the old woman—the kind a novice gives a senior—rather than a nod.

"We came up from the warden's room, Sister, about Aubrey Vallow's pledge. It falls due at sundown." Her voice is soft enough for the cloister, but clear. "His widow is not with us, and no petition is being made in her name. The warden wrote nothing. He thought your shelves might hold what the day-books point to."

She pauses, letting the spectacled gaze rest on her. "He also said the matter would keep better if we asked you about Master Vallow's visits to the archive—the last one most of all. There's a small mark in his day-book beside it, and we'd be grateful for your eyes on it. But only if you're willing."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra hears Bram out from her doorway. She doesn't step back and she doesn't step aside. Behind her the long room is quiet. Dust turns in the light from the high windows, and the shelves go up into the gloom under the roof beams.

"Hired you." She says it flatly, the way she'd read back a shelf-mark. "To recover a coffer from a moneylender. That's a lender's errand, Master Halloway. It's honest work. It isn't the same as being sent to me."

Then Sefa bows, a proper novice's bow, low enough and held long enough, and the old woman's eyes move to her. Something in Maudra's face takes note of it. It isn't warmth. It's more the look of a librarian who has found a book shelved in the right place.

"Lucan sends people up my stair," she says. "He doesn't send reasons with them. That's how I know he means it when he says he has written nothing." She lets the spectacles drop onto her nose. "So. Master Vallow's visits."

She moves at last. It's only half a step, but it lets them see the reading table under the windows. There's a lectern at its end with a great book chained to it, closed, its boards black with age.

"The first time he read in the witness rolls. This house used to witness oaths between merchant houses. A sworn bargain, sealed here at the dawn office. That was long before my time, and long before his." Her voice stays brisk, but she's choosing each word before she spends it. "Notaries don't usually care about bargains two hundred years dead. He did. He read for an afternoon and asked me nothing."

"The second time he asked me a question. I'll not repeat it on a stair to people I met a moment ago."

Then Bram's last question. She takes longer over it than she took over the rest.

"Whether he left anything in my keeping." She doesn't say yes and she doesn't say no. "If a man left a thing with me, he'd have left it for someone. And I'd give it to that someone, or to a person that someone had plainly sent, with her word to show for it. Not to a person who'd been hired to fetch a box." She looks from Bram to Sefa and back. "Have you anything from her? In her own hand?"

Before either of them can answer, she turns to Sefa again.

"And you spoke of a mark, Mistress. Beside his visit." Her hands are folded at her waist, the ink-black cuffs pulled down. They have gone very still. "I'm willing. Show it to me."

Brother Teo stands two steps down with his eyes on the wall. He's plainly wishing he were back at his pens.
````

"Whether he left anything in my keeping." She doesn't say yes and she doesn't say no. "If a man left a thing with me, he'd have left it for someone. And I'd give it to that someone, or to a person that someone had plainly sent, with her word to show for it. Not to a person who'd been hired to fetch a box." She looks from you to Sefa and back. "Have you anything from her? In her own hand?"

Before either of you can answer, she turns to Sefa again.

"And you spoke of a mark, Mistress. Beside his visit." Her hands are folded at her waist, the ink-black cuffs pulled down. They have gone very still. "I'm willing. Show it to me."

Brother Teo stands two steps down with his eyes on the wall. He's plainly wishing he were back at his pens.
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram keeps his voice low in the quiet cloister. "She hired us to recover the coffer, yes. I went to Lucan on my own application, after Thole offered to release it if the court approved redemption without the ticket. Lucan couldn't approve it."

"We're looking for the ticket now. Aubrey's day-book records two visits to your archive. We'd like to know what he came to consult, and whether he left anything in your keeping."

Sister Maudra hears Bram out from her doorway. She doesn't step back and she doesn't step aside. Behind her the long room is quiet. Dust turns in the light from the high windows, and the shelves go up into the gloom under the roof beams.

"Hired you." She says it flatly, the way she'd read back a shelf-mark. "To recover a coffer from a moneylender. That's a lender's errand, Master Halloway. It's honest work. It isn't the same as being sent to me."

Then you bow, a proper novice's bow, low enough and held long enough, and the old woman's eyes move to you. Something in Maudra's face takes note of it. It isn't warmth. It's more the look of a librarian who has found a book shelved in the right place.

"Lucan sends people up my stair," she says. "He doesn't send reasons with them. That's how I know he means it when he says he has written nothing." She lets the spectacles drop onto her nose. "So. Master Vallow's visits."

She moves at last. It's only half a step, but it lets you see the reading table under the windows. There's a lectern at its end with a great book chained to it, closed, its boards black with age.

"The first time he read in the witness rolls. This house used to witness oaths between merchant houses. A sworn bargain, sealed here at the dawn office. That was long before my time, and long before his." Her voice stays brisk, but she's choosing each word before she spends it. "Notaries don't usually care about bargains two hundred years dead. He did. He read for an afternoon and asked me nothing."

"The second time he asked me a question. I'll not repeat it on a stair to people I met a moment ago."

Then Bram's last question. She takes longer over it than she took over the rest.

"Whether he left anything in my keeping." She doesn't say yes and she doesn't say no. "If a man left a thing with me, he'd have left it for someone. And I'd give it to that someone, or to a person that someone had plainly sent, with her word to show for it. Not to a person who'd been hired to fetch a box." She looks from Bram to you and back. "Have you anything from her? In her own hand?"

Before either of you can answer, she turns to you again.

"And you spoke of a mark, Mistress. Beside his visit." Her hands are folded at her waist, the ink-black cuffs pulled down. They have gone very still. "I'm willing. Show it to me."

Brother Teo stands two steps down with his eyes on the wall. He's plainly wishing he were back at his pens.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 12 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't look at the struck-through line or at Bram's entry for any longer than a man reading idly over a clerk's shoulder would. He gives the brother a small nod, the kind you give someone on his feet since dawn.

"Dunmore. Pell Dunmore." He waits while the pen writes it. "When you do go up, would you tell Master Halloway something for me? That I'm here, and that I'll meet them at the garden wicket on the east lane, not out front. He'll know why." He gives a brief rueful smile. "We've a cart to catch and I'd rather not lose them in the square."

He doesn't take the bench. He glances once at the shut inner door and the woman with the dowry box. Then he leaves the brother to his queue and steps back out into the strip of shade. He keeps to the wall the way he came and turns the corner at the buttress, out of the bench's sight.

In the east lane he finds a spot near the wicket gate where he can see over or through it into the bean rows and toward the cloister doors, and where nobody on the square can see him. He leans on the wall like a man waiting for a friend who's late, which is what he is.

*If they went out the garden way already, they went home or they went looking for me. If they're still up with Sister Kell this long, she's telling them something worth two hours.*

If the brother in the straw hat comes near, Pell asks him easily whether a big man in mail and a young woman came through the garden since noon.

He gives it until the temple bell or about half an hour, whichever comes first. If nobody comes by then, he goes back round to the petition table to ask again.

*(Out of game: passive Perception 14 while I wait. Any sign of Bram or Sefa in the cloister or garden, or anyone else taking an interest in the wicket or the east lane. Is the thin rider about?)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Teo writes the name in a small, upright clerk's hand, *Pell Dunmore, for Master Halloway*, and sands it. He listens to the message with his pen still raised.

"The garden wicket, the east lane." He adds it under the name. "It's latched on the garden side. Whoever's in the beans will let them out if they ask." He looks at the shut inner door, where the voices have started up again. "I'll take it up when the warden's done with these two. I can't say how soon that'll be. Dowries are slow."

The woman with the box shifts it on her knees and doesn't look up. Pell leaves them to it.

Outside, the strip of shade along the chapter house wall is as narrow as before. Pell walks it at the same easy pace. The great steps stay between him and the bench, and he doesn't look toward the alehouse. At the buttress he turns the corner into the east lane. The square drops out of sight behind him, and with it the novice's broom and the drovers' mules.

The lane runs between the temple's garden wall and the back of a row of sheds. The wicket is a plain plank gate set into the wall, about chest high, grey with weather. A string hangs through a hole beside the latch on the garden side. Over it Pell can see the bean rows: tall poles tied in pairs, with green running up them. A gravel path leads between them to a low door in the cloister's outer wall. Above that wall, along the east walk, is the row of small high windows he saw coming round. They're the archive's, if Teo's directions mean anything. All of them are shut except one near the far end, which is propped a hand's width open. They're set too high for him to see anything inside but the beams.

Pell leans his shoulder on the wall beside the wicket, where the lane is in shade and the square can't see him, and settles in like a man whose friend is late.

The brother in the straw hat is three rows in, pinching something off the leaves and dropping it into a clay pot at his feet. After a while he notices he has company and comes over to the gate, wiping his fingers on his habit. He's sixty if he's a day, brown as a walnut, with dirt worked into every crease of his knuckles.

Pell asks whether a big man in mail and a young woman have come through the garden since noon.

The brother thinks about it honestly, the way a man does when he's been asked something he'd like to get right. "I couldn't tell you. I've had my back to that door the whole afternoon. There's blackfly on the far poles." He holds up the pot so Pell can see a crust of tiny black bodies in the bottom. "If they came out, they'd have pulled the string themselves. Plenty of folk do. Nobody's asked me to let them out." He considers Pell over the gate. "You could wait inside on the bench by the well, if you like. It's cooler. Only I'd have to ask the cellarer, and he'd want to know your business." He says it without any suspicion, just as a fact about the cellarer.

He goes back to his poles before Pell has to decide, humming something under his breath that might be a dawn hymn.

The lane stays empty. A woman crosses the far end of it with a bundle of kindling and doesn't glance his way. A cat comes along the top of the shed roofs, considers him, and goes back. There's no thin man anywhere, nobody lingering where the lane meets the square, and nobody taking any interest in the wicket but Pell. The propped window over the east walk stays as it is.

*(Out of game: whether Bram and Sefa come down through the garden while you wait depends on their thread, which is still at about half past eleven in the archive, so I've stopped you here a few minutes into your wait. Teo has your message and will carry it up after the dowry hearing.)*
````

## Round 13

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “I have her letter, in her own hand. But it authorises me to treat with Thole—not to collect anything from you. I won't claim it says more than it does.”

I take out the letter and offer it for her to read.

“If you need her word addressed to you, I'll go back and ask her for it.”

Then I bring out my drawing of the mark and hold it where Maudra can see.

“This is as near as I remember it. Pell made a copy directly from the day-book; he has that one.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa doesn't reach inside her coat—she has no mark to show, and she won't pretend otherwise.

"Show her, Bram, the drawing you made for the warden," she says quietly.

While he reaches for it, she turns back to Sister Maudra. "We have the widow's word in her own hand, but it is made out for the lender's counter, not for these shelves." She says it without lowering her eyes. "If it's her word you need to open the door, then you've found the one honest fault in our business: we came to you too well-intentioned and too little armed. The warden sent us, but he didn't sign us her errand."

A short pause. "She wouldn't climb past the temple step, Sister. That's not pride. She's kept that house and that door since her husband fell, and this is the place where they laid him out. She'd sign at the foot of the steps if the court required it. The archive may ask more of her than she can climb."

She watches the old woman's face as Bram offers the scrap, and says nothing more.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra takes the letter from Bram, unfolds it and reads it in the doorway. She reads it twice, the second time slower. Her eyes stop where the seal ought to be and isn't.

"She didn't seal it."

"She wouldn't use his stamps," Bram says.

The old woman gives a short nod, as though that settles something on its own. She folds the letter along its creases and gives it back to him.

"It sends you to Thole. You told me so before I'd read a word of it, Master Halloway, and most men wouldn't have." She looks at Sefa. "And you're right, Mistress. It doesn't send you to me."

At what Sefa says about the steps, something in her face shuts, the way a book is shut.

"I know where they laid him." She doesn't say anything more about it. "She needn't climb anything. If she writes me two lines, my name and yours and that she sends you for what her husband left with the archive, then whoever brings me those two lines takes it back to her. That's all I need." She leaves the *if he left anything* unsaid this time.

Then Bram holds up his drawing.

She doesn't reach for it straight away. She looks at it where it is, in his hand, at the height of her eyes. Then she takes it by one corner and keeps her other hand folded at her waist. Sefa is watching her face, and she sees no puzzling-out in it. Maudra's eyes go from the circle to the stroke to the little hook at its foot, in order, the way a person checks a copy against something she already knows by heart. She takes a long time over the hook.

"Come in." She steps back out of the doorway at last. "Brother Teo. Your pens."

Teo is down the stair before she has finished saying it.

The archive is cooler than the cloister. It smells of vellum and dust, with something of old smoke underneath. The floorboards give a little under Bram's mail. Maudra goes to the reading table under the high windows and lays the scrap flat on the wood beside the chained book. She doesn't open the book. She stands with her fingertips on the edge of the table and looks down at the drawing, not at them.

"You wanted his second question. It was about a thing that looks like this." She lets that sit a moment. "He drew it in his day-book beside his visits to me. He didn't copy it from anything on my shelves. I'd know."

Then she looks up, first at Bram and then at Sefa.

"So I'll ask you plainly, and you'll answer me the way you answered about that letter. Have either of you seen this mark anywhere else? Cut in something, or pressed? In wax?"

 *(Out of game: none of the four needed a roll. They all happened in the turn above, and nothing about them was uncertain.)*

1) **Bram's letter:** Maudra read it twice and gave it back. She agreed it sends him to Thole and not to her. Then she named what she'd accept instead: two lines from Hesper, addressed to her.

2) **Bram's drawing:** Maudra took it by one corner, recognised it at once and invited you both in. It's on the reading table now.

3) **Sefa on the steps:** these were honest words, so no roll was needed. Maudra answered with "I know where they laid him" and told her Hesper needn't climb anything.

4) **Sefa prompting Bram:** it was just a word to a friend, and he showed the drawing.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell gives the old brother a nod over the gate. "Thank you, brother. I'll keep to the lane. No need to trouble the cellarer."

He settles back against the wall in the shade, arms folded. He watches the low door at the end of the gravel path, and now and then the propped window above the east walk.

*Two hours with the archivist. Either she's slow, or she's telling them something worth hearing.* He thinks of the struck-through line in Teo's book, *not pursued*, and of the lamp oil, and does the sums without meaning to. *Sundown's maybe five hours off. Then they need a hearing with the court, a seal, and a walk to Thole's counter past a man on a bench. That's tight.*

He doesn't fidget. He shifts his weight now and then so he doesn't stiffen, and keeps his ears open for the creak of the cloister door, a heavy tread in mail, or Sefa's voice.

If the low door opens and it's them, he pulls the string on the wicket himself so they don't have to stop, and keeps his voice low as they come through. "Not out front. Ashby's big man is on the alehouse bench and he can see the foot of the steps. Walk with me. Tell me on the way."

If the bell goes, or the shadow of the shed roofs reaches the middle of the lane, whichever comes first, he goes back round by the buttress and the wall's shade to Teo's table to ask whether the message has gone up.

*(Out of game: Passive Perception 14 while I wait, as before.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The old brother nods once over the gate, says "As you like," and goes back to his poles.

The afternoon is warm. Bees work the bean flowers. Somewhere behind the sheds a woman calls a child in twice, and the second time the child comes. The cloister door at the end of the gravel path stays shut. The propped window above the east walk stays as it is, a hand's width of dark under the eaves, and nothing moves in it.

After a while the brother comes back down the row with a tin dipper of water from the well and holds it out over the gate without being asked.

"You've been stood there long enough to want it." While Pell drinks, he tips his clay pot so Pell can see the black crust in the bottom. It's thicker now. "Four hundred, near enough. I stopped counting at the third pole." He thinks about that. "They come back by Lathander's day regardless. The beans don't seem to mind." He takes the dipper back, wipes the rim on his sleeve, and goes off to the far end of the rows, humming again.

No one else comes. A carter backs a load of firewood up to the sheds, unloads it in stacks, and goes without ever looking at the wicket. The cat comes back along the shed roofs and lies down in the sun. No thin man turns up, nobody lingers at the mouth of the lane, and nobody in the lane cares about anyone but themselves.

The bell doesn't ring. The shadow of the shed roofs creeps across the ruts and reaches the middle of the lane at about half past two.

Pell goes back round by the buttress and walks the strip of shade along the chapter house wall at the same easy pace. When the great steps come between him and the bench, he takes his one sideways look. The sunburnt man is still there. A girl from the alehouse is setting a fresh cup at his elbow and taking the empty one, and he doesn't take his eyes off the foot of the temple steps while she does it.

In the antechamber the slab of light from the high window has slid off the floor and partway up the far wall. The bench where the dowry woman sat is empty. The inner door is shut, and behind it her voice rises over a figure and falls again. A man's voice answers, dry and level.

Brother Teo looks up from his pens and, from his face, knows what Pell is going to ask.

"Still in. They've opened the box." He touches Pell's line in the book with the end of his pen, *Pell Dunmore, for Master Halloway*, with the message written under it. "I haven't been up. I said I'd go when the warden was done, and he isn't done." He glances at the door as the woman's voice climbs again. "Nobody's come down from the east walk past this table since I took them up. That's all I can tell you."

He goes back to his sand-box and leaves Pell the bench.

*(Out of game: Bram and Sefa's thread is still at about 11:40 in the archive, so until it catches up, what you learn at the temple stops short of where they are.)*
````
