````text
The bell doesn't ring. The shadow of the shed roofs creeps across the ruts and reaches the middle of the lane at about half past two.

You go back round by the buttress and walk the strip of shade along the chapter house wall at the same easy pace. When the great steps come between you and the bench, you take your one sideways look. The sunburnt man is still there. A girl from the alehouse is setting a fresh cup at his elbow and taking the empty one, and he doesn't take his eyes off the foot of the temple steps while she does it.

In the antechamber the slab of light from the high window has slid off the floor and partway up the far wall. The bench where the dowry woman sat is empty. The inner door is shut, and behind it her voice rises over a figure and falls again. A man's voice answers, dry and level.

Brother Teo looks up from his pens and, from his face, knows what you are going to ask.

"Still in. They've opened the box." He touches your line in the book with the end of his pen, *Pell Dunmore, for Master Halloway*, with the message written under it. "I haven't been up. I said I'd go when the warden was done, and he isn't done." He glances at the door as the woman's voice climbs again. "Nobody's come down from the east walk past this table since I took them up. That's all I can tell you."

He goes back to his sand-box and leaves you the bench.

*(Out of game: Bram and Sefa's thread is still at about 11:40 in the archive, so until it catches up, what you learn at the temple stops short of where they are.)*
````

Together after: [Pell Dunmore]

## Round 14

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “No. Only in the day-book, and our copies of it.”

I glance at Sefa, leaving her to answer for herself.

“Thole's notice describes yellow wax sealing the coffer's hasp. We haven't seen the coffer, and the notice names no mark on the seal. Hesper said Aubrey always used red.”

I look back to Maudra. “Is that what he asked you about—a seal?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa meets the old woman’s eyes and doesn’t glance at the drawing first.

“No, Sister. Not on stone, not on wood, not in wax—so far. We asked the widow before we came up the hill, and she said the same: in twenty-two years she’s never seen it anywhere in that house. We’ve seen it once, in Aubrey’s day-book, beside his last visit to you. I haven’t seen it since.”

She says it simply, the way she might note a symptom she can’t yet explain.

“But we haven’t gone looking for it yet. We came to you first, because the mark sits beside your stairs, and if it means what he thought it meant, we’d rather learn that here than from a stranger on the street.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Maudra hears Bram out with her fingertips on the table's edge. At *yellow wax* her fingers press down on the wood until the knuckles go pale. She waits until he has asked his question, and then a little longer.

"Yellow." She says it to the drawing, not to him. "Not red."

She turns to Sefa, hears her through to the end, and gives her a short nod. "Here first. That was right."

Then she leaves the table. She goes down the long room to a press at the far end, a tall cupboard of black oak with no lock on it, and comes back with a thin book bound in boards covered in faded saffron cloth. It's no bigger than a psalter. She doesn't open it at once. She sets it on the table beside the chained rolls and keeps her palm on the cover.

"You asked me plainly, Master Halloway, so I'll answer you the same way. Yes. He asked me about a seal." She opens the book. "This house made four of them, two hundred years ago, for the dawn office. When two merchant houses swore a bargain here, the oath went under one of these. Wax the colour of first light." Her finger rests on the page. "This is the register of them."

There are four drawings on the open page, done in brown ink by a careful hand much older than Aubrey's. Each one is a circle split by an upright key, the same figure as Bram's scrap. They aren't all the same, though. Each key has a different foot: a plain bar on the first, a split on the second, a ring on the third. The fourth ends in a small hook. A line of tiny letters runs round each rim. Under all four, in a different and later ink, someone has written a single entry: *Lost, all four, in the fire of the chapter house. Year of the Bright Blade.* That was ninety years ago.

Sefa can't help reading the rim script, any more than she can help reading a shop sign. It's an old, formal dawn-script, and it says the same thing around each of the four: *Witnessed at the light. It answers the hand it was given.*

"The hook," Maudra says. "That's the fourth. Aubrey drew it from the thing itself, not from this page. I showed him this page afterwards, and he went white." She closes the book. "The register says it burned. So either the register is wrong, or someone took it out of this house before the fire and kept quiet about it for ninety years."

She doesn't sit down.

"He asked me how one is opened. I'll tell you what I told him. It isn't broken. It's opened, by the hand it was set for. Any other hand, and what lies under it is gone. Burnt to nothing inside the box, and the box still shut." Her voice stays brisk, but it's quieter now. "Whose hand that one was set for, he didn't tell me. I didn't ask. I thought there would be time."

She looks at Bram, then at Sefa, and this time she holds their eyes.

"So now I owe two things. One is to a dead man, who left me something for his widow. The other is to this house, which has a seal sitting on a moneylender's shelf that by its own register burned before I was born." She lays her hand flat on the saffron boards. "I mean to keep both, if I can."

"Bring me her two lines, and I'll do what he asked of me. But when that coffer comes out of Thole's, I'll want to see that wax with my own eyes before anyone carries it further. Here, or on her own step. I don't mind which." A pause. "Will she allow that? Or must I go to Brother Lucan instead and tell him what's sitting at Thole's?"

Below the high windows the cloister is quiet. Somewhere in the garden the bean-row brother is humming.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell sits on the bench. He's close enough to Teo's table to talk without raising his voice, and he keeps an eye on the door to the square. He listens to the dowry argument rise and fall for a moment before he speaks.

"Long day for you." He nods at the inner door. "Something I don't understand, brother, and you'd know better than anyone. If the warden agrees to a writing for my friend's matter, a seal from the court saying the heir's money settles a pledge, who puts the seal on it? Him, or does it go higher? And can a thing like that be done the same day, or does it sit in a pile until the morning?"

He says it the way a man asks about a cart he has to catch, curious and a little worried about the hour.

"Only, the lender closes his book at sundown. If the warden has to sit again after this dowry, I'd like to know whether to tell my friends to hurry, or to start praying." He gives a small smile. "And if there's a novice with nothing to do, I'd put a copper in the poor box to have that message carried up now instead of later."

He keeps the coin in his fingers without offering it outright. If Teo says no, he puts it back in his purse and lets it go.

He doesn't mention Sister Kell or the archive, and the scrap stays inside his coat. He settles back against the whitewash to wait, watching the inner door and the square door in turn.

*(Out of game: Passive Insight 14 on Teo's answer. Does anything about the warden's seal, or a same-day writing, make him hesitate? Happy to wait here until Bram and Sefa's thread catches up.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Teo looks up from his sand-box when Pell speaks. He doesn't seem to mind the question. He seems glad of one he can answer.

"The warden seals it himself." He touches the edge of his table, as if the seal were in the room. "Saffron wax, the court's own sun. He keeps it in his room in a box on the shelf. If he rules, he writes it out at the long table, signs it, seals it, and hands it across while you sit there. It doesn't go higher." He thinks about it and adds, to be accurate: "Unless it touches the house's own property. Then it goes to the high priest, and that does sit in a pile. But a pledge between a notary and a moneylender doesn't."

"Same day, yes. Most of what he rules goes out the door with the people who asked for it." Then he looks at the shut inner door and frowns, working out the hour. "Only he stops hearing at the bell before the evening office. After that he's in choir until dark, and nobody fetches him out of choir. If your friends want anything from him today, it has to be before that bell. An hour or so short of sundown."

At the copper he goes a little pink about the ears and shakes his head.

"The poor box is by the door, and you're welcome to it. But it won't get anyone up that stair. A novice doesn't go up to Sister Maudra unless a brother sends him with a reason, and a layman's copper isn't one. She'd send him back down with his ears burning, and then she'd ask me why." He touches the line under Pell's name in his book. "I'll take it up myself when the warden's done. I said I would."

Pell is watching him as he talks, and there's nothing there to find. Teo doesn't hesitate over the seal or over *same day*. The only thing that bothers him is saying no to a man offering money, and it bothers him the way it would bother anyone young who has been raised not to take coin for his work.

Behind the inner door the dowry woman's voice rises over a figure again. A chair scrapes, someone says something about a silver ewer, and the dry voice answers her slowly, the way it has answered her for the past hour. It doesn't sound close to finished.

The slab of light from the high window has climbed a little further up the far wall. Out through the propped door the square is bright and quiet. A drover leads his mules away from the fountain, and the novice's broom has worked down to the bottom of the great steps. Nobody comes in.

Pell can sit on this bench as long as he likes. Nothing changes here until the dowry hearing ends.

*(Out of game: no roll needed. Teo was answering an honest question, and your passive Insight showed he has nothing to hide.)*
````

## Round 15

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “I'll ask her. I can't give her leave for her, but looking at the wax on her own step seems a fair request. We've promised the coffer stays shut. Your warning gives us better reason to keep that promise.”

I return the authority letter to my pack.

“Please give us time to bring her answer before you go to Lucan. We'll tell her exactly what you've told us, and ask for the two lines naming you and whoever she sends.”

I pause before turning to leave. “One thing for carrying it safely: can touching the wax trigger it, or only trying to open it?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa dips her head again, lower this time, like someone accepting a charge rather than arguing against one.

"I can't answer for her, Sister. I won't stand in her house and promise you a thing she hasn't given." She straightens and meets the old woman's eyes. "But I'll bring her your two lines, and I'll put it to her as plainly as you've put it here. If she'll allow you to see the wax with your own eyes, you'll have my word before we carry the coffer an inch. If she won't, I'll tell you that too, and you can still stand at the foot of the steps when it leaves Thole's—and see it there, where you won't have to step into her grief and she won't have to let the archive into her house."

She pauses, and the quiet of the long room presses in around the words.

"I don't think she'll refuse. She has kept that thing shut on her dead husband's word, and you are the first soul we've found who knows what it cost him to do the same."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra listens to Bram with her fingertips still on the saffron boards. When he asks for time before she goes to Lucan, she nods once, the way a clerk nods at an entry already made.

"You'll have it. I go down to choir at the bell before the evening office. If nothing has come from her by then, I'll stop at Lucan's door on my way." She doesn't make it sound like a threat. It's a deadline, and she gives it like one.

His last question makes her look down at the register.

"The rolls say the oath-taker opens it with his own hand, at the light. That's all they say." She thinks for a moment. "They say nothing of a hand that only carries it. Aubrey carried it into Thole's. Thole carried it to his strong room. Nothing has burned yet, so far as anyone knows." Then, more sharply: "Carry it by the bands. Don't lift it by the hasp, and keep everyone's thumbs off the wax. I've never seen one, Master Halloway. I've only read about them. Mind the difference."

She turns to Sefa and hears the whole offer. At *the foot of the steps* her mouth goes thin.

"Not those steps." She doesn't say why, and she doesn't need to. "Her own step, or this table. I said so, and I meant it. I'll not stand in the square and look at a temple seal in front of every carter in Beregost." She moves the register a little straighter on the table. "Bring me her answer and her two lines. You can bring them together."

Then Sefa finishes, and the old woman is quiet longer than she has been all morning. Below the high windows someone in the cloister crosses the grass, and the gravel crunches.

"It took the colour out of his face," she says finally. "I saw that much. I don't know what else it cost him, Mistress, and I'll not guess." She picks up the saffron register, carries it down the long room to the black-oak press, and puts it back on its shelf. Then she comes back and holds out Bram's drawing to him by one corner. "Yours. Take it with you."

She sees them to the door and holds it open. She doesn't come out onto the stair.

The steps are worn hollow in the middle. At the bottom the cloister is empty except for the sundial and its short shadow, pointing almost straight down the grass. As Bram's boot touches the flagstones of the east walk, the bell of the Song of the Morning begins overhead. It's the noon bell, the one you agreed to meet Pell by, at Thole's step or the cooper's corner. Past the chapter house the antechamber leads out to the square. Hesper's house is the other way, at the far end of the main street.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) Asking Hesper can't happen yet, because she's at home and Bram is still in the cloister. He'll put the question to her when he gets to her door.

2) No roll was needed, since it was an honest request and Maudra agreed in the story: she'll wait until the bell before the evening office.

3) No roll was needed. Bram means to ask Hesper for those two lines, which is the same errand as item 1, and Maudra answered that he can bring them with her answer.

4) No roll was needed, since it was a plain question and Maudra answered it in the story. The rolls only say the oath-taker's own hand opens it, Aubrey and Thole both carried it without anything burning, and her advice is to carry it by the bands and keep everyone's hands off the wax and the hasp.
````

````text
2) No roll was needed, since it was an honest request and Maudra agreed in the story: she'll wait until the bell before the evening office.
3) No roll was needed. Asking Hesper for the two lines is the same errand as item 1, and Maudra said you can bring them with her answer.
4) No roll was needed, since it was a plain question and Maudra answered it in the story.
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram speaks to the old woman: "I'll ask her. I can't give her leave for her, but looking at the wax on her own step seems a fair request. We've promised the coffer stays shut. Your warning gives us better reason to keep that promise."

He returns the authority letter to his pack.

"Please give us time to bring her answer before you go to Lucan. We'll tell her exactly what you've told us, and ask for the two lines naming you and whoever she sends."

He pauses before turning to leave. "One thing for carrying it safely: can touching the wax trigger it, or only trying to open it?"

---

Sister Maudra listens to Bram with her fingertips still on the saffron boards. When he asks for time before she goes to Lucan, she nods once, the way a clerk nods at an entry already made.

"You'll have it. I go down to choir at the bell before the evening office. If nothing has come from her by then, I'll stop at Lucan's door on my way." She doesn't make it sound like a threat. It's a deadline, and she gives it like one.

His last question makes her look down at the register.

"The rolls say the oath-taker opens it with his own hand, at the light. That's all they say." She thinks for a moment. "They say nothing of a hand that only carries it. Aubrey carried it into Thole's. Thole carried it to his strong room. Nothing has burned yet, so far as anyone knows." Then, more sharply: "Carry it by the bands. Don't lift it by the hasp, and keep everyone's thumbs off the wax. I've never seen one, Master Halloway. I've only read about them. Mind the difference."

She turns to you and hears the whole offer. At *the foot of the steps* her mouth goes thin.

"Not those steps." She doesn't say why, and she doesn't need to. "Her own step, or this table. I said so, and I meant it. I'll not stand in the square and look at a temple seal in front of every carter in Beregost." She moves the register a little straighter on the table. "Bring me her answer and her two lines. You can bring them together."

Then you finish, and the old woman is quiet longer than she has been all morning. Below the high windows someone in the cloister crosses the grass, and the gravel crunches.

"It took the colour out of his face," she says finally. "I saw that much. I don't know what else it cost him, Mistress, and I'll not guess." She picks up the saffron register, carries it down the long room to the black-oak press, and puts it back on its shelf. Then she comes back and holds out Bram's drawing to him by one corner. "Yours. Take it with you."

She sees you both to the door and holds it open. She doesn't come out onto the stair.

The steps are worn hollow in the middle. At the bottom the cloister is empty except for the sundial and its short shadow, pointing almost straight down the grass. As Bram's boot touches the flagstones of the east walk, the bell of the Song of the Morning begins overhead. It's the noon bell, the one you agreed to meet Pell by, at Thole's step or the cooper's corner. Past the chapter house the antechamber leads out to the square. Hesper's house is the other way, at the far end of the main street.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin]

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell turns the copper over once more, then gets up and drops it in the poor box by the door anyway. It's not for the stair, just for the long day. He comes back to the bench.

"Fair enough, brother. Thank you for that. It's more use to me than a novice would've been." He settles against the whitewash. "Before the bell for the evening office, then. I'll remember."

He thinks it through while he waits. *An hour short of sundown, so call it four hours, maybe less. The hearing, the writing, the seal, then down past the bench and along the main street to Thole's counter before he shuts his book.* He doesn't like how little room that leaves. He likes even less that Ashby's man is sitting there to see them do it.

He stays where he is with his hands loose on his knees. He keeps his eyes moving between the inner door, the square door and the foot of the stair Teo will climb. If a heavy tread in mail comes down from the cloister, or Sefa's voice, he's on his feet before they reach the table.

*(Out of game: Pell waits here, quietly, until Bram and Sefa's thread catches up or Teo goes up with the message. Nothing else for now.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The copper rings once on the bottom of the poor box. Teo hears it, glances up from his sand-box and looks back down quickly. His ears go a little pink again, but he says nothing about it.

The afternoon passes on the bench. The slab of light from the high window climbs the far wall a hand's width at a time. Behind the inner door the dowry woman's voice rises over the silver ewer once more and then, for the first time, doesn't rise again. A chair scrapes. A little after three the inner door opens and she comes out with the box under her arm. Her face is the face of someone who got most of what she came for and means to complain about the rest all the way home. The young man trails after her with his hands still folded. Neither of them looks at Pell.

Brother Lucan doesn't come out. His dry voice says something to the empty room, the inner door swings half shut, and through the gap Pell sees a lean, bald man in saffron at a long table, squaring a sheet of paper with two fingers.

Teo puts down his pen, sands the last line in his book and gets up.

"Now," he says, as much to himself as to Pell, and goes out through the door at the back of the antechamber.

He's gone a while. Out in the square the novice has finished the steps and leaned his broom against the fountain. A dog drinks from the overflow. Nobody comes in off the street.

When Teo comes back he's a little out of breath, and he stops at the table before he says anything, like a man getting a message straight in his head.

"Sister Maudra says they went down at the noon bell." He touches the line under Pell's name. "I told her who'd sent me up. She asked your name twice and what you looked like. Then she said, 'Tell him they're on an errand, and they're coming back to me.' That's all she'd say." He frowns down at his book. "I was at my bread in the refectory at the noon bell. This table was empty a quarter of an hour. So they could have come through here, or gone out by the garden, or by the cloister's west door. I couldn't tell you which."

He sits back down and picks up his pen.

"She didn't say when," he adds, being accurate. "Only that they're coming back."

It's about a quarter past three. The inner door stands half open on the warden's empty queue. Nobody else is waiting, and the bench is Pell's for as long as he wants it.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) Dropping the copper needed no roll, since nothing about it was uncertain. The turn opens on it: the coin rings in the bottom of the poor box and Teo's ears go pink.

2) Waiting on the bench needed no roll either. Pell watched the doors from about twenty to three until a quarter past. No mail came down from the cloister and Sefa's voice never sounded, so nothing came up that he had to get up for. The only ones who came out were the dowry petitioners, and then Teo with Maudra's answer.
````

## Round 16

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Pell first. He's expecting us.”

I head out through the antechamber toward Thole's, keeping a brisk walk with Sefa rather than running through the square. I look for Pell at the lender's step, then the cooper's corner.

If we find him, I draw close enough to speak privately. “The court needs the ticket. Maudra has something Aubrey left for Hesper, but needs Hesper's written leave to hand it over. We're going to fetch that now. What did you learn?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa pauses at the foot of the stair only long enough to glance up at the bell-tower through the cloister arch, then turns to Bram.

"That's noon. Pell will be at Thole's step or the corner, and we said we'd be there." She walks as she talks, toward the chapter house and the square beyond, keeping her voice low under the last of the bell. "We'll find him first. Then to Hesper together, all three of us. What Maudra told us about the seal changes how we carry that coffer, and Pell shouldn't hear it second-hand from a stranger."

She glances at Bram's pack. "In the street, he gets the short version: the mark is a temple witness seal that was supposed to have burned, and Sister Maudra wants Hesper's two lines and her own look at the wax before the coffer goes further. Her name and the rest can wait until we're behind Hesper's door."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram doesn't wait for the last stroke of the bell. He crosses the east walk at a quick, steady pace, mail rustling, and Sefa keeps up beside him, talking low while the bell's hum is still in the stones.

The antechamber is empty. Brother Teo's stool is pushed back from the petition table. His pens are in their rack, the sand-box is shut, and the book lies open with nobody to watch it. The benches along the walls are empty too, and the inner door is closed. Wherever the brothers eat at noon, everyone has gone there. The propped oak door lets in a slab of white light, and the two of them walk out into it.

At noon the square is bright and nearly empty. The fountain runs with nobody at it. A dog lies flat in the thin shade of the great steps, and the novice and his broom are gone. Bram and Sefa cross the pale stone and go up the main street. The smiths have stopped for their meal, so for once the hammers are quiet. The street smells of bread and onions, and people are sitting on doorsteps eating with their sleeves rolled up.

Thole's narrow stone front looks the way they left it. The brass-bound door is shut and the grille is closed. Nobody is on his step. Across the street the alehouse has its serving hatch open, and two carters are leaning on it with their backs to the street, arguing about a third man who isn't there. Nobody sits on the bench below it. Bram stops long enough for a man to look about him, the way he'd wait at a crossroads for a wagon that's running late. No one steps out of a doorway toward them. No one gives a sign from a side lane.

They walk on toward the top of Hesper's lane, five doors along. The cooper's door is shut. The tiefling has gone from the step where he stood all morning, and so has the stable lad from the trough. There's nobody on its rim at all, just green water and a few straws floating on it. Bram remembers what Hesper said this morning: *by the time I sit down to eat at noon, he's gone.* She was right about the hour.

Down the lane Hesper's door is shut and her ground-floor shutters are closed. Nobody is waiting at the cooper's corner. Pell isn't on Thole's step either. Neither of them has seen any sign of him since the yard door closed behind him this morning.

It's about a quarter past twelve. You agreed this morning that if anyone wasn't there by the bell, the rest would go looking. Hesper's door is fifty paces down the lane, and you have the whole afternoon before sundown.

*(Out of game: Bram's words to Pell weren't said, because Pell isn't at either spot.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell sits with that for a moment. *She asked my name twice and what I look like. An archivist doesn't care what a man looks like unless she's been told to expect him, or told to watch for someone.*

He leans forward, elbows on his knees, and keeps his voice easy.

"Thank you, brother. You've gone up that stair for me once already, and I won't ask you to make a habit of it." He nods toward the half-open inner door and the warden squaring his paper. "Is that the warden's queue done for the day, or will he sit again before the bell? If my friends come back with what she sent them for, I'd like to know they can still walk straight in."

Then, more carefully:

"And if Sister Maudra wanted my name and my face, maybe she'd want the rest of me. Would she see me if I asked, plain and proper? Not as a layman wandering the cloister. I'm with Master Halloway, on Mistress Vallow's matter, her late husband's business. If she says no, I'll sit here like a good dog until they come back." A small shrug. "But if they're coming back to her, I'd as soon be waiting where they're going."

He doesn't take out the scrap. If Teo says he can't ask, Pell lets it go and moves somewhere he can watch both ways in: the garden wicket and this table. He asks one last thing before he goes.

"The cloister's west door. Where does it come out? Which street?"

*(Out of game: if Maudra won't see him, Pell waits near the wicket, with a glance at the west door if it's close. Passive Perception 14 throughout, watching for Bram and Sefa or the thin rider.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Teo hears the first question out with his pen lifted. He turns on his stool to look at the half-open inner door.

"That's the queue done. It's only you." He sounds faintly surprised himself. "He hears whoever comes, right up to the bell. If your friends come back before it, they walk straight in. There's nobody left to wait behind."

The second question takes him longer. He puts the pen down in the rack, which he hasn't done all afternoon.

"I can't take you up, Master Dunmore. Not on my own say. I've been once already, and I had a reason when I went." He looks at his book, at the line with Pell's name in it. "She asks everyone what they look like. She asked me what the dowry woman looked like last month, and she never came down to see her." He thinks about whether that's fair to say, and lets it stand. "The warden sent your friends up. If anyone sends you, it'll be him, not me."

Then the last question, which he can answer cleanly.

"The west door opens on Almoner's Row. It's a lane down the temple's west side, past the alms-house and the healer's door, and it comes out on the main street just short of the square, three houses up from the fountain." He tips his head toward the propped street door to show where. "From the bench across from Thole's, you'd see anyone who came out of it. The garden way is the only way out that nobody on the main street sees."

Before Pell can get up, a chair scrapes in the inner room.

Brother Lucan stands in the half-open doorway with a squared sheet of paper in one hand. He has plainly heard every word, because the door was open and the antechamber is small. He looks at Pell's hands on his knees, then at his face. There's grit on Pell's knees and tar on his coat front, and Lucan takes in both.

"Master Dunmore. With Master Halloway, on the Vallow matter." He says the name to the room, not to Pell. "Your friends sat in that chair this morning. I told them what the court can do and what it can't. Then I sent them upstairs, and they went down again at the noon bell, and nobody's told me why." He folds the sheet once and holds it at his side. "I can send you up to Sister Maudra. She'll see you if I send you, or at least she'll open the door."

He doesn't step aside from the doorway yet.

"Before I do, you'll tell me one thing, since your friends told me only a name and a sum. Who is Master Ashby? And why does he want a dead notary's box badly enough to offer twice what's owed on it?"

Behind him, the window onto the garden shows the bean-row brother straightening up with his clay pot. Out through the propped street door the square lies bright and empty. It's a little before half past three.

*(Out of game: no roll needed. Teo answered honest questions honestly, and nobody new turned up while you listened: no Bram, no Sefa, no thin rider.)*
````

## Round 17

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 17 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “He said we'd go looking. Let's start where he left the house.”

I turn to Sefa. “Stay together. We'll ask Hesper if he's returned, get Maudra's letter, then check the cart alley.”

I walk down to Hesper's door and knock, standing where she can see me when she opens it.

“Bram and Sefa. Has Pell been back? He missed our noon meeting.”

Once we're inside, I keep it brief. “Maudra says Aubrey left something with her for you. She'll release it to someone carrying two lines in your hand, addressed to her, naming who you send to collect it.”

“And she recognised the mark. It's an old temple seal. She asks to inspect the wax on your own doorstep when we bring the coffer—not open it. Will you allow that?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa doesn't hurry. She stands at the corner with the sun on her face and makes a slow, careful circle with her eyes—cooper's step, trough, main street, the temple square behind them—like a person counting how many doors are visible from one place.

"He isn't here, and he isn't on any step we can see from here." She turns to Bram. "If he got a runner or a lead, he'll go to ground somewhere he can watch the street and the lane both. If he got trouble, it'll be off the main road, where nobody eating lunch on a doorstep wants to notice."

She glances down the lane toward Hesper's barred door. "We're fifty paces from her. Before we go beating the alleys, ask at her door whether Pell came back, or left word, or used the yard. She may also know which of these lanes a man can disappear down quickest." A short pause. "If there's no word behind that door, then we search. You take the cart alley and the back ways; I'll do the front street and the temple side. We meet at the trough when the shadow's half a hand further on—say the half-hour—and if one of us has him, the other will know by the look of us."

The bell has stopped, and the quiet of the noon hour lies over the lane like dust.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa stops at the corner and turns slowly in the noon sun. The cooper's step is empty. Green water stands in the trough with straw floating on it. Smiths sit eating on their doorsteps along the main street, and behind them the pale square lies quiet at the temple end. Nobody stands anywhere a man would stand to watch, and none of them is Pell. She says so, lays out her search, and follows Bram down the lane.

Bram knocks and stands back from the door so he's the first thing anyone at the shutters would see. A moment later the bar scrapes. The door opens a hand's width, and then wider.

"No," Hesper says before he has finished asking. "Not by the front. Nobody has knocked at the back. I've been in the study since you left me, and I'd have heard the yard." She lets them in and drops the bar behind them.

In the study the dust sheets still cover the front-room furniture, and Sefa's seventeen gold still lies on the desk. Hesper doesn't sit. Bram gives her Maudra's terms the way he was given them. When he gets to *something left with her for you*, Hesper's eyes go to the ledgers on the shelves, as if whatever it was ought to be there and wasn't.

"For me." She says it flatly. "He never said."

Then the mark, and the seal, and Maudra's wish to see the wax on the doorstep. Hesper looks at the dish of dull red wax beside the rack of stamps for some time.

"He sealed in red," she says. "Twenty years, in red." She puts her inked hand flat on the edge of the desk. "Looking isn't opening. She may look. On my step, with the coffer in your hands and not in hers. Nobody touches the wax. Then it comes in here and the door is barred." She looks up at Bram. "If she wants more than a look, she can ask me for it in my own house, and I'll tell her no in my own house."

She takes a clean sheet from the drawer and sits down to write. She doesn't hesitate over the words. When she's done she sands the sheet and turns it so they can both read it:

*To Sister Maudra Kell, keeper of the archive of the Song of the Morning. I send Bram Halloway, or Sefa Marrin, for what my husband Aubrey Vallow left with the archive. Hesper Vallow, his widow.*

"Either of you," she says, and gives it to Bram. "So it doesn't matter which of you gets up her stair."

She turns to Sefa about the search, since that's a question with a plain answer. "If a man wants to leave the main street unseen, he uses the cart alley behind the smithies, or the footway behind the cooper's. That one runs along the backs of the houses as far as the temple end. Too narrow for a cart. The forge boys use it." She thinks for a moment. "The yard door I haven't been to since you all went out. If he came back that way, he didn't come in."

Outside the shutters the hammers start up again one by one, as the smiths finish their bread. It's coming up on half past twelve. Bram has the two lines in his pack. The cart alley, the footway and the front street are all there to be searched, and sundown is hours off.

*(Out of game: no rolls this round. Hesper answered plain questions and made her own choices.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) This happened with no roll, since nothing about it was uncertain. Bram knocked and stood back where the shutters could see him, and Hesper opened the door.

2) This happened with no roll: it was an honest question. Hesper said no. Pell hasn't come back by the front, and nobody has knocked at the back.

3) This happened with no roll, since it was honest talk. Hesper agreed to let Maudra look at the wax on her step, with nobody touching it. She wrote the two lines naming Maudra, Bram and Sefa and gave them to Bram.

4) This happened with no roll. Sefa's read of the street found no sign of Pell, and she set out the search plan. Hesper added the cart alley and the footway behind the cooper's as the quickest ways off the main street. The search itself hasn't started yet, and that's yours to play next round.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 17 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell gets up off the bench. It's the warden's door, and a man doesn't answer the court sitting down. He keeps his hands where they can be seen and answers plainly.

"Twice what's owed. That's news to me, Brother. Thank you for it." He lets that sit a moment. "What I know of Ashby I learned today, and none of it from his own mouth. He's been at the Red Sheaf at the north end near a tenday. He calls himself an iron buyer, and the forges like him because he pays ahead. He writes himself into a chandler's slate as *Ashby & Corve*, like a firm. He has two hired men with him. One rides out before light and comes back after dark. The other's sitting on the alehouse bench across from Thole's this minute, with a cup and a clear view of your steps."

He pauses.

"And for three mornings he's paid a stable boy a silver piece to sit at the top of Mistress Vallow's lane and tell him who comes and goes. A man buying a box at sundown doesn't need to know who calls on the widow. That's what I don't like about him." He leaves out the oil and the tinderbox. That's a worry, not a fact, and the court deals in facts.

"Why he wants it, I don't know. Nor does she. Her husband told her once to keep it shut if it ever came to her, and she means to. So do we." He meets Lucan's eyes. "We're not here to cheat Thole of a copper. She'll pay him the hundred and twenty, lawful and in full, if your court tells him he may take it. That's all we want from anyone today: the box in her hands before sundown, still shut."

A small nod toward the folded sheet at Lucan's side, but he doesn't ask about it.

"If Sister Maudra will open her door to me, I'd be grateful. If my friends are coming back to her, I'd rather be standing there when they do than out here guessing."

*(Out of game: I won't show the mark to Lucan. If he sends me up, it stays in my coat until I've seen how Maudra takes the Vallow name.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Lucan hears Pell out standing in his doorway, with the folded sheet still held at his side. He doesn't interrupt. When Pell gets to the stable boy and the silver piece, Lucan's eyes leave Pell's face and go to the propped street door, then out to the bright square, the way a man looks toward a noise in the next room. They come back when Pell has finished.

"A silver a day to watch a widow's lane." He says it slowly, like a figure he means to check later. "And a sword sitting across the street from my door." He comes out of the inner room at last and puts the folded sheet down on Teo's petition table. He rests his hand flat on it while he goes on. "This court has a book for that kind of quarrel, Master Dunmore, and it isn't the book of contracts. It's not one I want to open on a dead notary's pledge, either." He lifts his hand off the paper. "You've told me what you know, and you've told me what you don't know, which most people leave out. That will do for now."

He doesn't say anything about the ticket or his ruling. He turns to Teo.

"Brother Teo. Take Master Dunmore up to the archive. Tell Sister Maudra I send him, and that I've written nothing yet." Teo is already getting up, and something in his face says he expected to climb that stair a third time today. Lucan looks back at Pell. "If the heir's coffer comes out of Thole's today, I'd like to hear about it from one of you before I hear it in the square."

He goes back into his room and leaves the door half open behind him.

Teo takes Pell out through the door at the back of the antechamber into the cloister. Four covered walks run round a square of clipped grass. The sundial's shadow lies long and slanting across the lawn now, nothing like the stubby noon mark. The chapter house shuts out the noise of the square behind them. Teo walks quickly and says nothing. At the corner of the east walk a stair climbs between two walls, every step worn hollow in the middle, to a low iron-bound door with a bell-pull beside it. Teo pulls it once and steps back two steps, the way he has done it all day.

It takes a while. Then the door opens inward onto dry air and the smell of old vellum. A small, straight-backed woman of about seventy stands in the gap. She wears a saffron habit washed pale and ink-black cuffs, and round spectacles are pushed up on her forehead. Behind her a long room runs away under high windows, with shelves up into the dark of the roof beams.

Teo gives the warden's message in one breath. Sister Maudra doesn't look at him while he says it. She looks at Pell's hands, then at the coal grit on his knees and the tar on his coat front, and last at his face. She stays in the doorway.

"Dunmore." It isn't a greeting. She's checking the name against something. "I told the boy they're on an errand and coming back. That hasn't changed because Lucan sent you up my stair." She lets the spectacles drop onto her nose. "Master Halloway told me this morning that you copied a mark out of Aubrey Vallow's day-book with your own hand, and that you carry it." Her hands stay folded at her waist. "Have you got it on you?"

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) This happened and needed no roll, since it was honest talk: Pell got up to answer, and Lucan heard him out from his doorway.

2) This happened and needed no roll, since it was true and Pell left out the oil. Lucan took in the silver a day for watching the widow's lane and the sword across from his door, and he now sees it as more than a contract quarrel.

3) This needed no roll. It wasn't an offer of payment, just a plain statement that Hesper will pay Thole the hundred and twenty if the court lets him take it. Lucan didn't take it up again: his ruling still needs the ticket.

4) This happened and needed no roll, since Lucan had already said he'd send Pell up once Pell answered him. Teo took him up the stair. Maudra now stands in her doorway and has asked whether he carries his copy of the mark, so the turn waits for Pell's answer.
````

They come back when you have finished.

## Round 18

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Thank you. We'll give Maudra your terms exactly.”

I turn to Sefa. “I'll check the yard and cart alley; you take the front street and temple side, as you suggested. Meet at the trough in half an hour. If either of us finds trouble, call for help rather than going into it alone.”

I go out into Hesper's yard and examine the back door before opening it: bolt, latch, and ground around the threshold. Then I follow Pell's route into the cart alley, looking for anything dropped, signs of a struggle, or somewhere a man might be lying out of sight.

If anyone is working along the alley, I ask whether they've seen Pell, describing his clothes and healer's satchel. I keep my sword sheathed and my ears open between the hammer strokes.

Sefa Marrin: Sefa folds her arms and nods once, satisfied.

"Then stay together, as you said. Footway behind the cooper's first—that's where he worked this morning, and if someone moved on him, it's narrow enough for no one to hear it from the street. We come out at the temple end, then back up the cart alley to the smithies. If he's sitting somewhere with a twisted ankle or watching a back door, we'll find him between the two."

She turns to Hesper. "Bar us out. If Pell comes while we're gone, keep him in the yard—don't open the front to a knock you don't know. Send a neighbour's child down the footway if you have to, and we'll come back at a run."

A pause, and she looks at the bar on the door behind them. "And keep my share on the desk where it is. If I'm not back to collect it, it'll mean I found something that needed carrying."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hesper hears Sefa out with one hand on the bar of the front door.

"Barred. It stays barred." She looks down the hall toward the study and the yard beyond it. "No child, though. The cooper's girl talks, and his wife talks more. If I need you I'll come to the footway myself." Her eyes pass over the seventeen gold on the desk and come back. "It stays where you left it."

She follows them through to the yard. When Bram stops at the door under the pear tree she stays on the step with her arms folded inside her cloak.

The door is as Pell left it at nine. It stands a finger's width open and the bolt is drawn back. The slide is bright along its length where the brown rust has been rocked off it, and the flakes lie on the sill below. Bram crouches. Under the pear the ground is soft and full of windfalls, and it shows a single set of prints, toes pointing out to the door. They're a light man's boots, and one toe is pressed deep at the threshold where he lifted the door on its hinges to keep it off the sill. No prints come back.

"If he comes back, this is the way he'll come," Hesper says behind him. "I'll leave it as he left it." She doesn't come down off the step.

The cart alley outside is black with coal grit, rutted, and holds no footprint for more than a cart's passing. The hammers have started again. There's no blood in the ruts, nothing dropped, no torn ivy on the yard walls, and nowhere along the open sheds a man could lie without the smiths falling over him. The bellows boy is at his handle in the same shed. When Bram stops and describes Pell to him, lean, quick, dark coat, a satchel at his hip, the boy pumps twice before he answers.

"Never saw nobody. I watch the coals." He doesn't look up. "Master clouts me if I don't."

The carter who was unhitching at the alley mouth this morning is gone, and so is his cart.

At the cooper's yard Bram stops a second time. The lean-to shed is roofed in old shingles that are furred with moss, and the rain butt stands below it. There's a black scuff of coal grit on the butt's rim where a boot went up. On the roof, from the eaves to the ridge, a man-length strip of moss has been pressed flat and dried pale in the sun. Its edges are clean. Nobody fought up there. Somebody lay there a long while, at the spot where the ridge gives a view past the cooper's corner to the trough and the top of the lane. Then he came down the same way, and the grit he brought down is on the butt as well.

Past the stacked staves the cooper's yard opens two ways. A gate in the side wall lets onto the main street, by the cooper's corner and the trough. Behind the yard a footway runs off along the backs of the main-street houses toward the temple end. It's too narrow for a cart, and it's empty as far as the first bend.

It's about a quarter to one.

*(Out of game: your two plans part here. Bram's splits you, with Bram on the back ways and Sefa on the front street, meeting at the trough. Sefa's keeps you together down the footway. Which way each of you goes is up to you.)*

*(Out of game, a correction to Pell's thread: at half past one Hesper told Pell she hadn't seen Bram and Sefa since a quarter past ten. That was my mistake. She'd have told him you came back at half past twelve asking after him and went out the yard to search, and I'm treating that as what she said.)*
````

````text
Bram says, "Thank you. We'll give Maudra your terms exactly."

He turns to you. "I'll check the yard and cart alley; you take the front street and temple side, as you suggested. Meet at the trough in half an hour. If either of us finds trouble, call for help rather than going into it alone."

Hesper hears you out with one hand on the bar of the front door.

"Barred. It stays barred." She looks down the hall toward the study and the yard beyond it. "No child, though. The cooper's girl talks, and his wife talks more. If I need you I'll come to the footway myself." Her eyes pass over the seventeen gold on the desk and come back. "It stays where you left it."

She follows you both through to the yard. When Bram stops at the door under the pear tree she stays on the step with her arms folded inside her cloak.

Bram examines the door before opening it. It is as Pell left it at nine. It stands a finger's width open and the bolt is drawn back. The slide is bright along its length where the brown rust has been rocked off it, and the flakes lie on the sill below. Bram crouches. Under the pear the ground is soft and full of windfalls, and it shows a single set of prints, toes pointing out to the door. They're a light man's boots, and one toe is pressed deep at the threshold where he lifted the door on its hinges to keep it off the sill. No prints come back.

"If he comes back, this is the way he'll come," Hesper says behind you. "I'll leave it as he left it." She doesn't come down off the step.

The cart alley outside is black with coal grit, rutted, and holds no footprint for more than a cart's passing. The hammers have started again. There's no blood in the ruts, nothing dropped, no torn ivy on the yard walls, and nowhere along the open sheds a man could lie without the smiths falling over him. The bellows boy is at his handle in the same shed. Bram stops and describes Pell to him, lean, quick, dark coat, a satchel at his hip. The boy pumps twice before he answers.

"Never saw nobody. I watch the coals." He doesn't look up. "Master clouts me if I don't."

The carter who was unhitching at the alley mouth this morning is gone, and so is his cart.

At the cooper's yard Bram stops a second time. The lean-to shed is roofed in old shingles that are furred with moss, and the rain butt stands below it. There's a black scuff of coal grit on the butt's rim where a boot went up. On the roof, from the eaves to the ridge, a man-length strip of moss has been pressed flat and dried pale in the sun. Its edges are clean. Nobody fought up there. Somebody lay there a long while, at the spot where the ridge gives a view past the cooper's corner to the trough and the top of the lane. Then he came down the same way, and the grit he brought down is on the butt as well.

Past the stacked staves the cooper's yard opens two ways. A gate in the side wall lets onto the main street, by the cooper's corner and the trough. Behind the yard a footway runs off along the backs of the main-street houses toward the temple end. It's too narrow for a cart, and it's empty as far as the first bend.

It's about a quarter to one.

*(Out of game: your two plans part here. Bram's splits you, with Bram on the back ways and Sefa on the front street, meeting at the trough. Sefa's keeps you together down the footway. Which way each of you goes is up to you.)*
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin]

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't reach for his coat straight away. He stands two steps below her on the worn stair, so he's looking up at her a little, and lets her finish taking him in.

"I have." He keeps his voice level and quiet, matching the room behind her. "Master Halloway's an honest man. He'd have told you I copied it in Aubrey's own ink with Aubrey's own pen, too, and that it came out a shade heavier than his line."

Then he takes the scrap out, unfolds it once, and holds it up in the light from the door. He doesn't hand it over. He watches her face, not the paper.

"It sits in the margin beside your name, Sister. *Song. Archive. Sr. M.K.*, twice in the autumn, and the mark beside the second. His widow says it isn't his hand and isn't anything she's seen. So either you'll know it, or you'll know who drew it."

He gives her a moment with it, then folds it again and holds it loosely.

"I'll tell you plainly what I want, since your stair's a long one. My friends are on an errand you sent them on, and I don't know what it is. Thole shuts his book at sundown. Your warden stops hearing at the bell before evening office. Ashby's man is on a bench across from Thole's door, and Ashby bought lamp oil and a tinderbox yesterday." A small pause. "If what you sent them for brings them back up this stair, I'd like to be standing here when they come, and know what I'm guarding on the walk down. If you'd rather keep me on the step, I'll keep. But I'd rather come in."

*(Out of game: Passive Insight 14 on Maudra when she sees the mark. Does she recognise it, and is it surprise, fear, or confirmation?)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra lets him finish. She doesn't reach for the scrap when he holds it up in the light from the door. She looks at it where it is, the way she might check a shelf-mark against a catalogue.

Pell is watching her face, and nothing in it is surprised. She looks at the circle, then the stroke, and stays longest on the hook at the foot. Her mouth sets a little, like a clerk finding a figure that agrees with one already in the book. She has seen this mark before, and seeing it again tells her nothing new. When he folds the scrap she lets it go without a word.

The lamp oil is different. At *a tinderbox yesterday* her folded hands tighten at her waist, and for a moment she looks past him, down the stair toward the cloister and the town beyond it. What crosses her face is closer to anger than fear.

"Oil." She says the word as if writing it down. "A man who means to read a thing doesn't buy oil for it."

She doesn't explain that. She steps back out of the doorway instead.

"Come in, Master Dunmore. Lucan sent you, and Master Halloway spoke well of your hand." She turns to the stair. "Brother Teo. Your pens."

Teo goes down without a word, and his steps are quick on the hollowed stones.

The archive is cool and dim and smells of vellum, with something like old smoke underneath. Shelves run up into the dark under the roof beams. A reading table stands under the row of high windows, and one of the windows near the far end is propped a hand's width open. At the end of the table a great book with black boards sits on a lectern, chained and shut. Maudra points him to the bench along the table and stays standing herself.

"Their errand is theirs to tell you. I won't tell it for them." She takes a cloth from her sleeve and wipes a mark off the table edge that Pell can't see. "They know the way back up. When they come, you'll be sitting there, and you can ask them."

She looks at the propped window, then back at him.

"The man on the bench. You said he watches Thole's door. What else can he see from there?"

*(Out of game: no roll needed. Your passive Insight read her: she knew the mark already, and the lamp oil angered her. Bram and Sefa reaching this stair depends on their own thread, so here it's about twenty to four and the archive is quiet, with Maudra waiting for your answer.)*
````


## Round 19

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: I point out the scuffed butt and flattened moss to Sefa.

“Looks like his perch. Clear view of the trough, and he came down without a struggle. If he followed the boy, this is where he'd have started.”

I look toward the narrow footway. “Together, then. We'll check this through to the temple end. If there's no sign of him, we take Maudra her letter and ask whether he's been there.”

I lead down the footway at a steady walk, checking the bends and recessed doorways before passing them, listening for voices or movement ahead.

Sefa Marrin: Sefa crouches, not touching the butt, and tilts her head to take in the pressed strip of moss on the roofline. She says it quietly, half to Bram and half to the yard itself:

"He was up there. Not running. Lying on the ridge where he could watch the corner, the trough and the lane all at once. He came down this way." She points at the grit on the rim, then toward the side gate and the footway. "When he left, he didn't go back to the yard. The gate's the quick road to the main street; the footway's the quiet one. If he'd kept watch all morning and caught a scent, he'd take the quiet road rather than step out into the open."

She straightens and looks to Bram. "Let's do the footway together first, now we know he was here. Half an hour and the hammers won't hear anything either. One man alone in that narrow way asking questions is exactly what whoever moved on him would have used. Two of us will do."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram points at the scuff on the rain butt and the pale strip of moss on the roof. Sefa crouches beside the butt without touching it and reads the same things from the other side: the grit, the flattened moss, the clean edges. They agree on what it means and on what to do next, so neither of them argues. Bram goes first into the footway and Sefa follows close behind him.

It's a mean little path, barely wider than Bram's shield. Back walls of the main-street houses rise on the left. On the right are the yard walls of the lane behind, grown over with ivy and capped with moss. The hammers from the smithies carry over the roofs but sound far off here. Bram takes each bend slowly, the way he'd take a blind corner on the Way with wagons behind him. He looks before he steps, checking every recessed doorway and every gap between walls. Nobody is lying in any of them. There's no blood, and nothing has been dropped. A cat jumps off a midden-heap ahead of them and is gone. There are kitchen doors, a slop-drain, a privy with its door hanging open, and a woman's voice behind a shutter scolding someone about the bread.

After the fifth chimney past the cooper's, the left-hand wall changes. It rises almost ten feet of grey stone, freshly limewashed, with broken crockery set in mortar along the top. A single narrow door sits flush with the stone, studded with iron, and has no handle on the outside. One shuttered window is set above it. Down near the dirt of the footway there's a small square vent with three iron bars across it. As Bram passes, he can feel cool air coming out of it, the way air comes out of a cellar or a stone room that never gets the sun. Every other house in the row has a kitchen door and a drain. This one has neither. Bram counts the chimneys again and knows whose back this is. Thole's front was five doors past the cooper's.

Two houses further on, someone is sitting in the way.

He's a boy of about fourteen, sitting on a back step with his knees up and his chin on his chest, asleep. A heel of bread has dropped from his hand into the dirt. He has on a leather apron burnt through in a dozen places, and the backs of his forearms are freckled with small pink scale-burns. Bram grew up in a smithy yard and knows all of it at a glance: the red rims round the eyes, the soot worked into the creases of his neck, a sleep so heavy it comes on halfway home. This boy has been at a forge all night.

Bram's mail wakes him. He jerks upright and blinks at the shield, then at Sefa. Then he pulls his feet in, because there's no other way past him.

"Sorry, master. Sorry." He scrubs his face with a sooty palm. "Didn't mean to drop off. Been at the fire since the evening bell." He looks at the bread in the dirt and decides it isn't worth picking up. "Old Thunderhammer's had the lot of us on picks. Night after night. Two hundred of them, and then forty sets of chain after." He says it to the wall and not to them, the way tired boys complain to anyone who stands still long enough. "For a mine. Nobody can tell me whose mine. Master says it pays ahead and I'm to shut up about it."

He gets up, sways once, and flattens himself against the doorframe so they can get by.

Past him the footway runs on another hundred feet or so. The back walls give way to sheds, and then the path comes out between two woodstores into a lane that runs along the back of a row of sheds. On the other side of the lane is a long garden wall. A plain plank wicket is set in it, chest-high and grey with weather, and a latch-string hangs through a hole beside it. Over the wall are bean poles tied in pairs, and past them a gravel path runs to a low door in the cloister's outer wall. The tower of the Song stands high above it all. From here the square is round the corner, out of sight, and so is the main street.

There's been no trace of Pell anywhere along the footway. It's coming up on one o'clock.

*(Out of game: Pell's thread has already shown Sister Maudra telling him at about twenty to four that you two are "on an errand and coming back", so your thread can't reach her before then. If you mean to go straight up to her now, tell me and I'll correct his thread instead.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell sits where she points, on the end of the bench nearest the door, and keeps the scrap folded in his hand on his knee.

"Thole's door, first of all. The main street both ways, from the alehouse to past the fountain. The foot of your great steps, though not the top; the steps hide the chapter house wall from him." He counts it off quietly. "And Brother Teo tells me anyone coming out by your west door onto Almoner's Row comes out three houses up from the fountain, in plain sight of him. So the front, the west door and Thole's are all his. The only way out of this house he can't see is the garden wicket on the east lane. That's how I came round, and that's how I'd bring anything out."

He glances up at the propped window at the far end, then back to her.

"He's been on that bench since a little after noon and he hasn't left it. He doesn't hide. He lets Thole see him through the grille. To me that says he's not there to learn anything. He's there so Thole knows he's watched, and so somebody knows the moment a widow's party walks in that door with money."

A small pause. He doesn't unfold the scrap again.

"You knew the mark, Sister, and the oil made you angry. I won't ask you for their errand. But if what they're fetching is something a man might burn rather than read, tell me that much, so I know whether I'm guarding a box or a fire. And if they come up your stair with it, I'd have them go out by the beans."

*(Out of game: Pell waits here with Maudra for Bram and Sefa. Passive Perception 14 on the stair and the propped window.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra doesn't sit down. She hears him out on her feet with one hand on the back of the reading-table chair, and when he gets to the garden wicket she turns her head toward the propped window at the far end and looks at it.

"The beans." She says it as though she were writing down where something is shelved. "The garden door is the cellarer's. He leaves it on the latch until the evening office and bars it after." She looks back at Pell. "So that way is open until the bell, and after the bell it isn't."

The window stays propped a hand's width open. Bees go past it, and below it the old brother in the bean rows is still humming, the same few notes over and over. Nothing on the stair behind Pell creaks.

When he gets to the man on the bench, the one who doesn't hide and lets Thole see him through the grille, Maudra's mouth goes thin.

"Then he isn't sitting there to watch anybody. He's sitting there to be seen." She turns that over. "A man sends a sword to sit across from a lender's door when he expects the lender to sell to him at sundown and wants him to remember it. Gaspar Thole would sell his own coat to a man who frightened him. He'd tell himself it was the terms."

She leaves Thole there and comes to his last question. She takes her time with it, and she doesn't hide that she's choosing what to say.

"A box or a fire." She repeats it flatly. "A box, Master Dunmore. It won't catch light on its own while you carry it. Carry it by the bands and keep everyone's thumbs off the wax. I've told Master Halloway the same." Her hands go still at her waist. "As for the oil, I can only tell you what I make of it. A man who buys oil for a thing he's paying good gold for doesn't care what's in it. He wants nobody else to read it. That tells you it's worth reading, and someone has worked out what it would cost them if it were read."

That's all she gives him. She doesn't say what it holds, and she says nothing of their errand or of anything else in her keeping. She goes down the long room to a shelf halfway along, takes down a thick roll-book, carries it back and opens it at the far end of the table from him. She starts reading with one finger under the lines, as if he were furniture she has agreed to keep in the room.

"When they come, I'll hear the bell-pull," she says without looking up. "So will you."

The light from the high windows lies slantwise across the table. The stair stays quiet and the bell-pull doesn't move. Through the propped window comes the brother's humming, the scrape of a hoe, and once, far off in the town, a cart. It's getting on for four o'clock.

Pell can wait here as long as he likes. Nothing changes in the archive until someone pulls that bell.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text

Gaspar Thole would sell his own coat to a man who frightened him. He'd tell himself it was the terms."

She leaves Thole there and comes to your last question. She takes her time with it, and she doesn't hide that she's choosing what to say.

"A box or a fire." She repeats it flatly. "A box, Master Dunmore. It won't catch light on its own while you carry it. Carry it by the bands and keep everyone's thumbs off the wax. I've told Master Halloway the same." Her hands go still at her waist. "As for the oil, I can only tell you what I make of it. A man who buys oil for a thing he's paying good gold for doesn't care what's in it. He wants nobody else to read it. That tells you it's worth reading, and someone has worked out what it would cost them if it were read."

That's all she gives you. She doesn't say what it holds, and she says nothing of their errand or of anything else in her keeping. She goes down the long room to a shelf halfway along, takes down a thick roll-book, carries it back and opens it at the far end of the table from you. She starts reading with one finger under the lines, as if you were furniture she has agreed to keep in the room.

"When they come, I'll hear the bell-pull," she says without looking up. "So will you."

The light from the high windows lies slantwise across the table. The stair stays quiet and the bell-pull doesn't move. Through the propped window comes the brother's humming, the scrape of a hoe, and once, far off in the town, a cart. It's getting on for four o'clock.

You can wait here as long as you like. Nothing changes in the archive until someone pulls that bell.
````

Together after: [Pell Dunmore]

## Round 20

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Before we leave the tired boy, I ask, “Seen a lean man in a dark coat pass here today? He carries a healer's satchel. Or a lad about your age, dressed for stable work?”

I don't keep him standing longer than his answer takes.

To Sefa, I say, “No sign of a struggle. He may be following something well beyond these lanes. Let's get Maudra's parcel moving, and ask whether he's called there.”

I head round to the chapter house and the archive by the route we know, with Hesper's letter ready.

? We're going directly to Maudra now, at about one o'clock. Please correct the conflicting chronology rather than delay our arrival to fit another thread.

Sefa Marrin: Sefa stands back so the boy doesn't have to flatten himself any further against the door.

"Easy. You've earned your bread twice over by the look of you." She gives him a small, tired smile. "We're looking for a man, not a thief. Dark coat, a satchel at his hip, quick on his feet. He'd have come down this way around noon, maybe in a hurry. Did you see him?"

She doesn't mention the roof or the perch. She only watches his face.

"Two hundred picks and forty sets of chain for a mine nobody names. And you working nights for it." She says it as if it's the oldest complaint on the Coast. "Master ask you about our man, did he?"
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The boy blinks at Bram, then at Sefa, and works out slowly that they're asking him something.

"Lean? Dark coat?" He rubs one eye with the heel of his hand. "Didn't see nobody, mistress. I was asleep before I sat down, near enough." He thinks hard, because she was kind to him. "Nor no stable lad. They don't come this way. They go up the main street, the Sheaf boys and that." He gives a short laugh with no fun in it when Sefa mentions the picks. "Master don't ask me nothing, except is the fire hot. And he don't like me asking him neither. Last time I said *whose mine*, he said it was the Sheaf man's mine and I could go and ask him, and then he clouted me." He picks the bread out of the dirt after all, blows on it, and goes in through the back door. The bolt slides behind him.

There's no trace of Pell anywhere along the footway. Bram and Sefa come out between the woodstores into the east lane. They don't go through the wicket. They go round by the corner of the temple, where a buttress throws its shadow over the cobbles, and along the chapter house wall toward the plain oak door. Most of that wall lies in the temple's shade, and the great steps stand between it and the main street.

Far down the main street, past the last roofs, someone has taken the bench under the alehouse hatch since you walked past it at a quarter past twelve. He's a big man, sprawled with his legs out across the cobbles and a cup at his elbow, too far off to make out his face. Then the steps come between you and him, and the strip of shade takes you the rest of the way to the door with the sun-disc over it.

The antechamber is cool again. The inner door is shut, and a voice behind it is going on about the price of a cart axle. Brother Teo looks up from his pens, sees the mail, and sighs, though not at Bram.

"She said you'd be back." He's already putting the pen in the rack. "The warden's got a wheelwright in with him. I'll take you up."

It's the same cloister and the same hollowed stair. Teo pulls the bell once and steps back. The door opens sooner this time. Sister Maudra stands in it with her spectacles already down on her nose, as though she'd been reading at the table and simply got up.

Bram holds out Hesper's two lines. She reads them in the doorway and doesn't hurry. Then she reads them again, the way she read the letter of authority this morning.

"*I send Bram Halloway, or Sefa Marrin.*" She folds the sheet once along a crease that wasn't there before and keeps it. "And her answer about the wax?"

Bram gives her Hesper's terms as Hesper gave them. Maudra may look, on Hesper's own step, with the coffer in his hands or Sefa's and nobody touching the wax. Then it goes inside and the door is barred. If she wants more than a look, she can ask in Hesper's house and be refused in Hesper's house. Maudra hears it all without interrupting. At *refused in her own house* one corner of her mouth moves, and it isn't displeasure.

"That's Aubrey's wife," she says. "Wait there."

She goes down the long room to the black-oak press, but not to the shelf where the saffron register lives. She kneels, which costs her something at seventy, opens the bottom of the press and takes out a box of plain deal no bigger than a hand. She comes back with something out of it held flat on her palm.

It's a sheet of Aubrey's good paper, folded small, and not sealed. Across the outside, in the small square hand from the day-books, is written: *For my widow, if it comes to it. A.V.*

"I've not opened it," Maudra says. "I didn't need to. He told me what it was when he gave it me." She holds it out to Sefa, since Sefa is nearer, and lets go of it only when Sefa has it. "You may look. It's hers, and she sent you."

Inside is a slip of stiff card, cut square. It's a moneylender's ticket in a careful hand that takes care over its figures, the same hand as the notice in Bram's pack: *Ticket. One coffer, iron, banded, the hasp sealed in yellow. Pledged by the bearer against one hundred and twenty gold pieces, the term eight months, to sundown. No. 41. G. Thole.*

"Brother Lucan wanted to see it before Thole does," Maudra says. "He'll be at his wheelwright a while yet."

When Bram asks after Pell, giving his coat, his satchel and his quick feet, she shakes her head. "Nobody of that sort has been up my stair. Nobody's been up it since you left but the boy with my bread." She looks past them down the stair at Teo, who studies the wall.

Then she puts her hand on the doorframe.

"So. You have the ticket, and the court will have it in an hour, and Thole before sundown." She says it like an entry in the catalogue. "Then the coffer goes to her step. And how am I to know when it's on her step, Master Halloway? I won't wait in the square for it. Do I come down with you now, or will one of you climb my stair again to fetch me?"

*(Out of game: correction accepted. Bram and Sefa reach Maudra at about five past one. In Pell's thread, what Teo and Maudra said about you after 1:45 is withdrawn, and Pell's player will get it rewritten once your thread catches up.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) This happened and needed no roll, since it was an honest question: the boy saw neither Pell nor any stable lad, and he let slip that his master calls the buyer "the Sheaf man."

2) This happened and needed no roll, since it was a word to Sefa. Bram's plan stood as he said it: you took the parcel straight to Maudra, and she told him nobody of Pell's description had been up her stair.

3) This happened and needed no roll. Bram went round the buttress and along the shaded chapter house wall to the door, Teo took you both up, and Bram handed Maudra Hesper's two lines, which she kept.
````
