### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell nods once at the cellarer's latch and the evening bell, and files it beside Lucan's deadline. *Both close at the same bell. So it's the seal, then Thole's counter, and the box back through the beans before then. Or we bring it out some other way.*

He doesn't disturb her reading. After a while he speaks quietly, without getting up.

"Sister. With your leave, I'll stand at that end window instead of sitting by the door. If they come back by the garden, I'll see them on the path before they reach your bell. If anyone else comes down the east lane after them, I'll see that too." A small shrug. "I'll keep my hands off your shelves."

If she allows it, he goes down the long room on quiet feet, well clear of the chained book on its lectern, and stands to one side of the propped window, back from the light, the way he'd stand at any window he didn't want to be seen in. He watches the gravel path, the wicket, and as much of the east lane as he can see over the wall.

If she says no, he stays on the bench and listens for the bell-pull.

While he waits, he works through the walk from Thole's door to the wicket in his head: which lanes, which back ways, how far the bench can see. He wants a route ready before anyone needs one.

*(Out of game: Pell waits for Bram and Sefa. Passive Perception 14 on the garden, the lane and the stair.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra doesn't look up from her roll-book at first. Her finger finishes its line before she answers.

"The end window." She says it flatly, the way she'd note where a book had been moved to. "Stand back from it, and leave the prop where it is. If that shutter swings, the cellarer will be up my stair asking why." Then she goes back to her reading, and that's all the permission he gets.

Pell walks the length of the long room on quiet feet. He goes wide around the lectern and its chained black book, past shelves of rolls tied with faded tape. The floorboards hold still under him in a way they didn't under Bram's mail. He stops to one side of the propped window, a pace back from the light, where the sill's shadow covers him and the gap still gives him a view.

The angle is better than he expected. Straight below is the gravel path through the bean rows to the cellarer's low door. The old brother is still at the far poles with his straw hat tipped back, working down the last pair. Past the garden wall is the plank wicket with its string hanging, and past that the east lane: the rutted stretch where the shadow of the shed roofs now reaches almost to the wall, and the stacks of firewood the carter left. Across the lane, between two woodstores, there's a narrow gap Pell couldn't see properly from the ground. A footpath barely a shield wide comes out of it. He can follow it with his eye a little way west along the backs of the main-street houses before the roofs hide it. It runs the same way as the footway behind the cooper's, toward the town.

The lane stays quiet. A girl drives three geese along it with a stick and goes out the far end. The cat is back on the shed roofs, asleep now. Nobody stops at the wicket, and nobody comes out of the gap between the woodstores. The stair behind him and the bell-pull by the door stay silent. Behind him Maudra turns a page about once a minute.

From the window the tower's shadow lies long across the cloister roofs to the east. It's a little after four.

*(Out of game: Pell's thread is nearly three hours ahead of Bram and Sefa's, so whatever they do next decides what he sees from this window. He can keep watching, or go and do something else in the meantime.)*
````

## Round 21

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Come with us now, if you're able, Sister. We'll wait for Lucan, then go to Thole's and straight to Hesper's step. Better than leaving you uncertain when to come.”

I look to Sefa and the ticket. “That stays safe with you. We have what we need to redeem it.”

To Maudra, I add, “Our missing companion is Pell Dunmore. If he comes asking after us, please have word left that we're taking the lawful route through Lucan and Thole, then back to Hesper.”

I head downstairs with Sefa and ask Brother Teo for the next available hearing.

“We've found the ticket. The warden asked to see it before the lender does. We'll wait our turn.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa takes the folded paper with both hands, as carefully as she'd take a sealed letter from a dying patient's pillow, and doesn't open it until Maudra says she may. When she sees the ticket inside, she lets out a slow breath and reads it twice.

"The whole blessed thing hinges on this," she says quietly. "And he left it with you, not in the house. He knew Thole's terms better than anyone."

She folds it again, as Aubrey folded it, and tucks it into an inner pocket where her hand can find it without looking.

"Brother Lucan goes first, before Thole touches it." She glances at Bram. "If the wheelwright takes an hour, we use it. We sit in the antechamber and wait our turn, and we keep hold of the ticket until the warden has seen it and put his ruling under the court's seal. Thole only gets what the undertaking promises—the full sum and the coffer across his counter—after the law has spoken."

Then she turns back to Maudra, and her voice softens.

"We'll send for you, Sister, not leave you standing in the square. When we have the coffer out of Thole's, one of us will come up your stair or send Brother Teo to knock." She pauses. "But I'd rather you weren't waiting alone at Hesper's door with a crowd forming. If the time comes late and the street gets long shadows, meet us at the back—Hesper's yard door, under the pear tree. She'll allow you in that way, and no one on the lane will have a seat for it."

She holds the old woman's eyes. "You said you'd keep both debts. So will we. Her step, her barred door, her husband's seal. All of it."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa reads the stiff little card twice in the stair light, folds it back into Aubrey's paper along his creases and puts it in an inner pocket. Sister Maudra watches it go in and doesn't comment.

She answers Bram first.

"Come down with you now. To Lucan's table, then a moneylender's counter, then the main street." She repeats it back to him like a list of shelf-marks, and her tone makes it plain what she thinks of the list. "A sister of this house walking into Gaspar Thole's behind a man in mail. By the evening office every forge in Beregost would have it that the temple had come to take a widow's box away from her. No, Master Halloway."

Then Sefa offers her the yard door under the pear tree, and the old woman goes quiet for a moment.

"Her step, she said." Maudra considers it. "Her back step is as much hers as her front one, and nobody sits on a trough to watch it." She gives a short nod. "The yard, then. When it's out of Thole's and on its way to her, send Brother Teo up my stair. I'll come round by the garden and the forge boys' path. I walked it when I was young enough to be scolded for it." She doesn't say how long ago that was. "I'll be slower than you. Don't take it inside before I get there."

She hears the last thing Sefa says, about keeping both debts, all of it, and for a moment she just looks at her.

"See that you do, Mistress."

On Pell she doesn't need telling twice. "Dunmore. If anyone comes asking under that name, Teo will tell him you've gone to Lucan and then to Thole's, and then to the widow." She puts her hand on the door. "Go on. You've less afternoon than you think."

The iron-bound door shuts behind them before they reach the second step.

Brother Teo is at the bottom of the stair, and he follows them back along the east walk without being asked. The antechamber is cool and dim. The wheelwright's voice is still rising and falling behind the inner door, something about a hub and whether a spoke counts as a fault if it only cracked once it was on the road. Teo sits down at his table, finds Bram's line in the book and writes a second one under it in his small upright hand: *Bram Halloway, again. The ticket.* He sands it and adds Maudra's message for Pell in the margin below.

"After the wheelwright," he says. "He's nearly done. He's started saying the same things twice."

He turns out to be right. They wait a little under half an hour on the bench. The slab of light from the high window creeps across the floor, and nobody comes in from the square. Then the inner door opens, and a big man with sawdust in his beard comes out, still arguing over his shoulder. Brother Lucan stands in the doorway behind him. He sees Bram and Sefa on the bench and lifts his eyebrows a fraction.

"That was quick," he says. "Come in."

He sits down at the long table and holds out his hand, as he did this morning. Sefa takes Aubrey's folded paper from her pocket and opens it on the table herself. She doesn't let go of the card until his fingers are on it.

Lucan reads the outside first, *For my widow, if it comes to it. A.V.*, and says the initials quietly to the page. Then he reads the ticket, once quickly and once slowly with a finger under the lines. The finger stops on *the hasp sealed in yellow* and stays there a moment longer than the words need. He says nothing about it. He moves on to *No. 41. G. Thole*, takes down a red-bound book from the shelf behind him, finds a page and runs his finger down a column.

"Thole registers his ticket numbers with this court every quarter, so nobody can forge them. Forty-one, issued in the month Aubrey's day-book gives." He closes the book and squares the card in front of him with two fingers. "It's real."

He leans back.

"You understand what you're holding? *By the bearer of my ticket.* Mistress Marrin is the bearer, sent by the heir. Thole's own terms require him to take a hundred and twenty from her across his counter, and he doesn't need a word from me to do it." He taps Thole's undertaking, which Bram has laid beside the ticket. "He asked for my seal because he had no ticket in front of him. Now there is one."

"Even so, I'll write it if you want it. A ruling that the heir's redemption, made by her bearer with this ticket, ends every other claim on the coffer, Master Ashby's included, from the moment the money is paid. Thole can show it to anyone who comes knocking afterwards, and so can she." He folds his ink-stained hands on the table. "Two gold. It goes in the book under the pledge, with you as applicant, Master Halloway. Do you want it, or will you take the ticket to Thole as it is?"

Through the window behind him the bean-row brother has started on the far poles. It's getting on for twenty to two.

*(Out of game: no rolls this round. Maudra made her own choice, and Lucan checked the ticket against the court's own register.)*

*(Out of game: this puts Bram and Sefa in Lucan's room at about 1:40, when Pell's thread has him reaching the antechamber at 1:50. As Bram's player asked, Pell's afternoon from 1:50 on will be rewritten to fit: at 1:50 Teo tells Pell you're in with the warden, and Maudra's message is in the margin of Teo's book.)*
````

````text
"The whole blessed thing hinges on this," she says quietly. "And he left it with you, not in the house. He knew Thole's terms better than anyone."

She folds it again along Aubrey's creases and tucks it into an inner pocket.

"Brother Lucan goes first, before Thole touches it." She glances at you. "If the wheelwright takes an hour, we use it. We sit in the antechamber and wait our turn, and we keep hold of the ticket until the warden has seen it and put his ruling under the court's seal. Thole only gets what the undertaking promises, the full sum and the coffer across his counter, after the law has spoken."

Then she turns back to Maudra, and her voice softens.

"We'll send for you, Sister, not leave you standing in the square. When we have the coffer out of Thole's, one of us will come up your stair or send Brother Teo to knock." She pauses. "But I'd rather you weren't waiting alone at Hesper's door with a crowd forming. If the time comes late and the street gets long shadows, meet us at the back, Hesper's yard door, under the pear tree. She'll allow you in that way, and no one on the lane will have a seat for it."

She holds the old woman's eyes. "You said you'd keep both debts. So will we. Her step, her barred door, her husband's seal. All of it."
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays where he is, a pace back in the sill's shadow, and lets his eyes follow the footpath out of the woodstore gap until the roofs swallow it.

*Same line as the cooper's footway. Fifth chimney past the cooper's is Thole's back wall, the high limewashed one with the crockery and the flush door.* He walks it in his head. Thole's counter, then out by that back door if Thole will open it, along the footway to this gap, across the lane, and in at the wicket. The whole way stays behind the houses and the bench never sees it. *If Thole won't open his back door, it's the front, and then it's a walk past a sword. Better to know which before we're carrying anything.*

He speaks over his shoulder without turning from the window, quiet enough not to break her reading for long.

"Sister. The bell before the evening office. How long before sundown does it ring, as near as you can say? An hour, or less?" A beat. "And does the cellarer bar the garden door the moment it rings, or when he's done with his supper?"

Whatever she answers, he thanks her and goes back to watching. He keeps on the gravel path, the wicket, the gap between the woodstores, and the stretch of lane where the shadows are lengthening. If Bram's mail or Sefa shows on the path or in the lane, he'll be at the bell-pull door before they reach it. If anyone else comes out of that gap or stops at the wicket, he wants a good look at them first.

*(Out of game: Pell keeps waiting at the window for Bram and Sefa. Passive Perception 14 on the garden, the lane and the footpath gap.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra finishes her line before she answers, the way she has every time he's spoken. Her finger stays on the page.

"An hour, near enough. A little under at midsummer." She turns the page. "The cellarer bars his door on his way into choir. The brothers eat after the office, not before. He's not been late to choir in all the years I've known him, and he's proud of it." She goes back to her reading. "You'll hear the bell from that window better than I do from this end."

Pell thanks her and keeps watching.

Down in the garden the old brother has reached the last pair of poles. He stands up, rubs the small of his back with both fists, tips the clay pot over the compost heap by the wall and knocks it out on his palm. Then he picks up his hoe and walks back up the gravel path toward the cellarer's door, still humming. The door opens and shuts behind him. Now the bean rows are empty, and the poles throw long stripes of shadow across the gravel.

A few minutes later something moves in the gap between the woodstores. A boy comes out of the footpath into the east lane. He's lanky, in a leather apron burnt through in a dozen places, and he carries an empty wicker basket on one hip. He doesn't look round or hurry, and he doesn't glance at the wicket. He crosses to the sheds where the carter stacked the firewood, loads the basket with split logs until it's heaped, gets it up onto his shoulder and goes back into the gap the way he came. The footpath takes him and the roofs hide him a few yards in. From the window Pell can see that the path is used, and that a boy with a full basket fits in it without turning sideways.

After that the lane is empty again. The cat on the shed roofs has rolled into the last patch of sun. The shadow of the sheds now covers the ruts and climbs the foot of the garden wall. Nobody stops at the wicket. The bell-pull by the archive door stays still, and nothing comes up the stair.

It's about twenty past four. Nothing else happens at this window before the bell unless you want it to.

*(Out of game: Bram and Sefa's thread is at about twenty to two, so whether they come down that path is up to their rounds. I'll hold you here until their thread catches up.)*
````

## Round 22

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Yes, please. With Ashby's claim expressly covered, as you said.”

I set two gold pieces on the table.

“Enter me as applicant under Aubrey's pledge, as we agreed. We'd also be grateful for a receipt for the fee.”

While he prepares the ruling, I turn quietly to Sefa. “Next stop Hesper's for the redemption money. We should tell her about the yard arrangement before promising it on her behalf. Then Thole.”

Before we leave, I ask Lucan, “If Thole refuses the ticket and payment despite this ruling, whom should we fetch?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa doesn't hesitate.

"Take the ruling." She says it quietly, but with the certainty of someone closing a door on a draught. "The ticket makes Thole take our money. The seal makes every man behind us—Ashby, a later ticket-bearer, anyone with a long face and a short memory—argue with the temple instead of with Hesper. At two gold, that's cheap armour for the walk home."

She glances at the ticket still under the warden's fingers, then at Bram. "Once it's paid and the coffer leaves Thole's counter, the street won't care which of them was right. I'd rather we carry the court's voice with us than have to explain a moneylender's clause to a crowd."

She sits back a little. "Let Brother Lucan write it while the room is still ours."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Lucan looks at the two gold pieces on the table and leaves them where Bram put them while he hears the rest.

"Applicant, Bram Halloway. Under the pledge of A. Vallow, deceased, to G. Thole." He takes a sheet from the drawer, a heavier stock than the petition book's, and uncaps his ink. "A receipt as well. You'll have both."

He writes slowly in a small, upright court hand and doesn't stop to think. Sefa makes her case for the seal while he writes. He doesn't look up, but at *argue with the temple instead of with Hesper* the pen pauses for half a word and then goes on. When he's done, he turns the sheet round on the table for them to read:

*Court of contracts of the Song of the Morning. On the application of Bram Halloway, under the pledge of A. Vallow, deceased, to G. Thole, lender on surety. The court has seen ticket No. 41 in the lender's hand, entered in this court's register of his tickets. It rules: redemption of the coffer named therein, made before sundown of the term by the bearer of that ticket, sent by the lawful heir and proved here in the spring, ends the pledge and every other claim on the coffer from the moment the sum is paid, including any claim of purchase by Master Corwen Ashby or any other. L. Pryce, warden.*

He sands it and takes a small box down from the shelf behind him. It's plain wood, and inside is a brass seal with a rayed sun. He warms a stick of saffron wax at the candle, lets a coin-sized pool fall at the foot of the sheet, and presses the sun into it. He holds it there for a breath, then lifts it. The rays come up clean.

The receipt takes him less time: *Received of Bram Halloway, two gold pieces, the court's fee for a ruling under the pledge of A. Vallow. L. Pryce.* He sweeps the two coins into a drawer and slides the receipt across after the ruling. Then he picks up ticket No. 41, folds it back into Aubrey's paper along its old creases, and gives it to Sefa rather than Bram.

"The bearer carries the ticket," he says. "Keep it that way until it's on Thole's counter."

Then he gets to Bram's last question. He puts his hand flat on the court book while he answers.

"If Gaspar Thole sees that seal and still won't take a lawful redemption, come back to this room before the bell. I'll send Brother Teo to his door with a summons, and he'll stand in that chair and explain himself to me. A lender in Beregost who defies this court's seal lends to nobody for long, and he knows it." He takes his hand off the book. "He won't refuse. He asked for this. What he'll do is read it twice, wipe his palms, and look at his door."

He squares the court book on the shelf with two fingers and sits back.

"I'll want to hear when it's done, Master Halloway. Before the square does."

The sheet with the court's sun on it lies on the table in front of Bram, still warm at the foot. Outside the window the bean-row brother has worked his way down to the far poles. It's a little before two o'clock. Hesper's door is the length of the main street away, and Thole's is on the way there.
````

````text
He sets two gold pieces on the table.

"Enter me as applicant under Aubrey's pledge, as we agreed. We'd also be grateful for a receipt for the fee."

While the warden prepares the ruling, Bram turns quietly to you. "Next stop Hesper's for the redemption money. We should tell her about the yard arrangement before promising it on her behalf. Then Thole."

Then he asks the warden, "If Thole refuses the ticket and payment despite this ruling, whom should we fetch?"

Brother Lucan looks at the two gold pieces on the table and leaves them where Bram put them while he hears the rest.

"Applicant, Bram Halloway. Under the pledge of A. Vallow, deceased, to G. Thole." He takes a sheet from the drawer, a heavier stock than the petition book's, and uncaps his ink. "A receipt as well. You'll have both."

He writes slowly in a small, upright court hand and doesn't stop to think. You make your case for the seal while he writes. He doesn't look up, but at *argue with the temple instead of with Hesper* the pen pauses for half a word and then goes on. When he's done, he turns the sheet round on the table for you both to read:

*Court of contracts of the Song of the Morning. On the application of Bram Halloway, under the pledge of A. Vallow, deceased, to G. Thole, lender on surety. The court has seen ticket No. 41 in the lender's hand, entered in this court's register of his tickets. It rules: redemption of the coffer named therein, made before sundown of the term by the bearer of that ticket, sent by the lawful heir and proved here in the spring, ends the pledge and every other claim on the coffer from the moment the sum is paid, including any claim of purchase by Master Corwen Ashby or any other. L. Pryce, warden.*

He sands it and takes a small box down from the shelf behind him. It's plain wood, and inside is a brass seal with a rayed sun. He warms a stick of saffron wax at the candle, lets a coin-sized pool fall at the foot of the sheet, and presses the sun into it. He holds it there for a breath, then lifts it. The rays come up clean.

The receipt takes him less time: *Received of Bram Halloway, two gold pieces, the court's fee for a ruling under the pledge of A. Vallow. L. Pryce.* He sweeps the two coins into a drawer and slides the receipt across after the ruling. Then he picks up ticket No. 41, folds it back into Aubrey's paper along its old creases, and gives it to you rather than Bram.

"The bearer carries the ticket," he says. "Keep it that way until it's on Thole's counter."

Then he gets to Bram's last question. He puts his hand flat on the court book while he answers.

"If Gaspar Thole sees that seal and still won't take a lawful redemption, come back to this room before the bell. I'll send Brother Teo to his door with a summons, and he'll stand in that chair and explain himself to me. A lender in Beregost who defies this court's seal lends to nobody for long, and he knows it." He takes his hand off the book. "He won't refuse. He asked for this. What he'll do is read it twice, wipe his palms, and look at his door."

He squares the court book on the shelf with two fingers and sits back.

"I'll want to hear when it's done, Master Halloway. Before the square does."

The sheet with the court's sun on it lies on the table in front of Bram, still warm at the foot. Outside the window the bean-row brother has worked his way down to the far poles. It's a little before two o'clock. Hesper's door is the length of the main street away, and Thole's is on the way there.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin]

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell watches the boy and his basket until the roofs hide them. *A used path, wide enough for a basket. I'd like to know where it comes out before I'm carrying a coffer down it.*

He turns from the window, keeping his voice low.

"Sister. I'll be gone a half hour, no more. I want to walk that footpath across the lane and see if it runs where I think, behind Thole's. I won't go near his wall, only look at it." A beat. "If they pull your bell before I'm back, keep them here. Tell Master Halloway I'm walking the back way and to wait for me. Don't let them go out the front."

He doesn't wait for her to say yes. He walks back down the long room, wide of the lectern, and down the hollowed stair. In the cloister he asks the first brother he meets, politely, to let him out by the garden. If nobody's about, he uses the cellarer's latch himself and leaves it exactly as he found it. Down the gravel between the bean poles, out through the wicket by its string, and he pulls it shut behind him.

He crosses the lane at a townsman's pace and goes into the gap between the woodstores like a man taking a shortcut home. He doesn't hurry, but he doesn't dawdle either. He counts the back walls and chimneys as he goes and notes every gate, every turn and any place two people would have to go single file. He wants to see where the path meets the footway behind the main-street houses, and whether it brings him in sight of the high limewashed wall with the crockery on top.

If it does, he stops well short of it. He has a lace to retie, or a stone in his boot. He looks at the flush iron door: fresh scuffs on the sill, a worn track in the dirt, any sign it's been opened lately. Then he looks along the footway both ways for anyone who has no reason to be there. The thin rider, or the boy with the basket coming back.

Then he turns round and walks the whole way back to the wicket, timing it in his head.

*(Out of game: Stealth +9 if needed. Passive Perception 14 on the route and around Thole's back wall. I'm not touching the door or the vent.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sister Maudra finishes her line before she looks up.

"Walk the path, look at the wall, come back inside half an hour." She says it back flatly, the way she'd read a catalogue entry. "If they pull my bell, I'll tell them what you said. Whether they wait is up to them." Then she goes back to her page.

Pell goes down the long room, keeping wide of the lectern, and takes the hollowed stair. The cloister is empty apart from the sundial and its long shadow. At the end of the east walk a short passage runs to the cellarer's low door. The old bean-row brother stands there at a stone trough, scrubbing out his clay pot with a handful of straw. When Pell asks to be let out by the garden, he nods at the latch without stopping.

"Lift and pull. Then drop it again behind you. He counts that latch, the cellarer." He thinks about it. "Not really. But he'd notice."

Pell lifts it, pulls, and drops it behind him the way he found it. He goes down the gravel between the bean poles, where the shadows now stripe the whole path, pulls the wicket's string, and goes out. The gate claps shut behind him.

He crosses the lane at a townsman's pace and walks into the gap between the woodstores like a man taking a shortcut home.

It's the footway. He knows it a few yards in. The back walls of the main-street houses rise on the right and ivy-grown yard walls run along the left. There's barely a shield's width between them the whole way. Two people can't pass anywhere without one turning sideways, and a man carrying something in both arms would fill it. For the first hundred feet or so the backs are sheds and a woodstore. There are no gates, only one privy door hanging open on a broken strap. Then come kitchen doors and a slop-drain. A back step has a crust of bread trodden into the dirt below it, and the door above it is shut. Behind it a forge roars as the bellows go. Two houses further on, the right-hand wall rises.

It's ten feet of limewashed stone with broken crockery along the top, just as he saw it at one o'clock from the other end. Pell stops twenty feet short, puts a boot up on a mounting stone that serves some kitchen door, and works at a lace.

The iron-studded door sits flush in the wall. Nothing about it says anyone uses it. A cobweb runs across the crack between the door and the stone at shoulder height, unbroken and grey with dust, not new. The dirt at the sill is the same as the dirt everywhere else in the footway. There's no worn track, no scuff and no swept arc where a door has swung out. Rust has run from the lowest stud down the limewash in a brown streak that nobody has scrubbed off. Whatever goes in and out of Thole's goes by the front. The shutter above is closed. Cool air breathes from the barred vent near his boots, the same as before, and there's no sound in it.

He looks both ways along the footway. A woman further up toward the cooper's end is tipping a pail into the drain. She goes back in without looking his way. There's no thin man, and no lanky boy coming back with his basket. Nobody is anywhere they have no reason to be.

He finishes the lace, turns round, and walks it back at the same pace, counting as he goes. From Thole's wall past the forge step and the sheds to the woodstore gap, then across the lane to the wicket, it's a little under three hundred paces. That's about five minutes for a man walking without hurrying. It would take longer with something heavy to carry and somebody slower behind him.

The wicket's string hangs where it did. Over the wall the bean rows are empty, and up on the east walk the archive window is still propped open a hand's width. It's about twenty to five.

*(Out of game: Pell's thread is about two and a half hours ahead of Bram and Sefa's, so nothing he found here depends on what they do at Thole's, and the rewrite of his afternoon from 1:50 still stands.)*

 *(Out of game: no rolls were needed for any of the three, and all three happened in the turn above.)*

1) Pell walked the footway from the woodstore gap and found it runs straight on to Thole's high limewashed wall. Nobody was watching the back lanes and the route was ordinary, so there was nothing to roll for.

2) His passive Perception covered the look at the iron door. It hasn't been opened in a long time: there's a dusty cobweb unbroken across the crack, no track or scuff at the sill, and an old rust streak below the lowest stud.

3) His passive Perception covered the look up and down the footway as well. The only person there was a woman emptying a pail, and she went back inside. There was no sign of the thin rider or the boy with the basket.
````

## Round 23

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “You'll hear from us when it's done. Thank you, Brother.”

I let the wax cool before packing the ruling where it won't be crushed, then leave with Sefa for Hesper's house.

On the way, I keep an eye out for Pell without lingering at either meeting place. At Hesper's door I knock and identify us.

Once we're inside, I tell her, “We have the ticket Aubrey left for you, and the court's sealed ruling. Thole must accept the hundred and twenty now. Have you heard from Pell?”

I show her the ruling, then add, “Maudra agreed to look without touching. She proposed coming by the footway to your yard instead of the front step. Will you allow that? We still need to send for her when the coffer is on its way.”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa takes the folded ticket back with both hands and tucks it into the inner pocket again, pressing it flat with her palm.

"Then we're as armed as we're going to be." She nods once to Brother Lucan. "The court will hear it's done before the square does. I'd sooner carry that news myself than let a carter bring it."

She waits until Bram has the ruling and receipt secure, then moves toward the door. "Hesper's first. The money, and we tell her about the yard—she hasn't signed up to unbar the back for anyone yet, and it's her door, not ours. If Pell has come back while we were up here, all the better. If he hasn't, we leave her word that we've gone to Thole."

She glances down the antechamber as if measuring the light. "Then Thole, while the seal's fresh and the day still has teeth. Main street, not the footway. Let anyone watching see a man in mail and a woman with the temple's sun in her pocket, not two furtive figures down the back."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Lucan watches Bram leave the ruling flat on the table until the saffron wax has gone from glossy to dull. Only then does Bram slide it into his pack between the undertaking and the notice, where the stiff sheets on either side will keep it from bending. The receipt goes in after it.

Sefa puts Aubrey's folded paper back in her inner pocket and presses it flat with her palm. When she says she'd rather carry the news herself than leave it to a carter, the warden looks up at her.

"So would I, Mistress." He's already reaching for the next blank sheet. "A carter tells it wrong, and then I have to hear it twice."

That's the end of it. He doesn't get up. The inner door swings half shut behind them.

They're halfway across the antechamber when Brother Teo looks up from his pens. He was waiting for them, and he puts a finger on his book before Bram reaches the propped door.

"Master Halloway. There's word for you." He turns the book around. Under Bram's two lines, in the same small upright hand, is a third: *Pell Dunmore, for Master Halloway.* Below it Teo has written the message. "He came in while you were with the warden, a quarter of an hour ago, maybe a little less. Lean, quick, grit on his knees. I told him you were inside. I don't interrupt the warden, so he left this." Teo reads it out exactly, the way a clerk does: "*That I'm here, and that I'll meet them at the garden wicket on the east lane, not out front. He'll know why.*" He looks up. "He said something about a cart to catch as well. Then he went out by the square door and turned left along the wall."

Maudra's message for Pell is still in the margin next to it, written before he ever arrived.

Through the propped door the square is bright and nearly empty in the early afternoon. The great steps rise on the right. Past them, down the main street and too far off to see a face, is the alehouse bench. Bram has seen a big man sprawled on it before. Left along the chapter house wall, in its strip of shade, is the buttress at the corner and the east lane behind it, where Bram and Sefa came out of the footway an hour ago. From the wicket that footway runs behind the houses all the way to the cooper's yard and the cart alley.

It's a little before two.

*(Out of game: these are Pell's own words to Teo, moved to 13:50 under the retcon. Pell's player confirms in the rewrite where he is now.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell pulls the wicket's string and lets himself back into the garden. He goes up the gravel between the striped shadows, lifts the cellarer's latch, and drops it again behind him exactly as he found it. If the old brother is still at his trough, Pell gives him a nod on the way past.

"Latch is down. He'll count it right."

Then he climbs the hollowed stair. He doesn't pull the bell, just knocks once with a knuckle so Maudra knows it's him and not his friends.

When she lets him in, he says it quietly, the way he'd give a figure to a clerk:

"It runs where I thought, Sister. Three hundred paces, five minutes walking, from your wicket to Thole's back wall. But his back door hasn't been opened since the winter. There's a cobweb across the crack, rust down the limewash, no track in the dirt." A small shrug. "So whatever comes out of Thole's comes out the front, past the bench. Then left along the main street, into the first lane that runs behind the houses, and down the footway to here. That's a hundred feet in the open, perhaps, with a sword watching. I can live with that if we know it's coming."

He goes back to his place at the end window, a pace back in the sill's shadow, and settles to watch the path, the wicket and the woodstore gap.

*A hundred feet in the open, with a sword on the bench who's been paid to be seen.* He runs it again in his head. *If Bram walks out with the box, the big man won't try anything in the street in daylight. Not with the Fist's cousins and every smith's wife watching. He'll follow. So the footway's the danger. A shield wide, one in front, one behind. That's where I'd be, if I were him. Or the thin one.*

He wants to be the one walking behind whoever carries it.

*(Out of game: Pell waits at the window for Bram and Sefa or the bell. Passive Perception 14 on the garden, the lane and the gap.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell pulls the wicket's string, and the gate claps shut behind him. He goes up the gravel between the bean poles, where the shadows now stripe the whole path. At the cellarer's low door he lifts the latch, slips through, and drops it back into its keeper the way he found it.

The old brother is still at the stone trough at the end of the passage. He's finished with the pot and is rinsing dirt out of his knuckles one finger at a time. Pell nods to him on the way past.

"Latch is down. He'll count it right."

The brother shakes the water off his hands. "He'll count it wrong anyway. He always finds one more than there is." He says it without any malice and goes back to his fingers.

The cloister is empty. The sundial's shadow is a long finger across the grass now. Pell climbs the hollowed stair and knocks once with a knuckle beside the bell-pull. After a moment the bolt draws, and Sister Maudra stands in the gap with her spectacles pushed up on her forehead and a finger still holding her place in the roll-book.

She hears him out in the doorway. She doesn't interrupt the paces or the cobweb, and she doesn't interrupt the rust streak down the limewash. At *a hundred feet in the open* she lets the spectacles drop onto her nose.

"I walked that footway at fifteen. It hasn't got any wider." She steps back to let him in. "Then whoever carries it doesn't stop to talk to anybody on the main street. Not a neighbour, and not a man on a bench who calls out good day." That's the whole of her advice. She goes back down the long room to her end of the table, sits, and finds her line again.

Pell takes his place at the end window, a pace back in the sill's shadow, with the prop left as it was.

Below him the bean rows are empty. The wicket's string hangs still. While he watches, the lanky forge boy comes out of the woodstore gap again with his empty basket on his hip. He heaps it from the cut-wood stacks, gets it onto his shoulder, and goes back into the footway the way he came, as unhurried as before. After that there's only the cat, who has given up on the sun and gone somewhere else. The shadow of the sheds has climbed halfway up the garden wall. Nobody stops at the wicket, nobody lingers at the mouth of the gap, and behind him Maudra turns a page about once a minute.

It's about ten to five. Nothing else happens at this window before the bell unless you want it to.

*(Out of game: as agreed, everything in Pell's thread after 13:50 still waits to be rewritten. Once Bram and Sefa pick their next move at 13:58, this scene may change to fit it.)*

 *(Out of game: none of the four needed a roll, since each was routine with nothing uncertain riding on it, and all four happened in the turn above.)*

1) Pell pulled the wicket's string, the gate clapped shut behind him, and he went up the gravel through the bean rows.
2) He lifted the cellarer's latch, slipped through, and dropped it back the way he found it.
3) He nodded to the old brother at the trough and said his line about the latch. The brother answered that the cellarer always counts one more than there is.
4) He knocked once with a knuckle beside the bell-pull, and Maudra drew the bolt and let him in.
````

````text
"I walked that footway at fifteen. It hasn't got any wider." She steps back to let you in. "Then whoever carries it doesn't stop to talk to anybody on the main street. Not a neighbour, and not a man on a bench who calls out good day." That's the whole of her advice. She goes back down the long room to her end of the table, sits, and finds her line again.

You take your place at the end window, a pace back in the sill's shadow, with the prop left as it was.

Below you the bean rows are empty. The wicket's string hangs still. While you watch, the lanky forge boy comes out of the woodstore gap again with his empty basket on his hip. He heaps it from the cut-wood stacks, gets it onto his shoulder, and goes back into the footway the way he came, as unhurried as before. After that there's only the cat, who has given up on the sun and gone somewhere else. The shadow of the sheds has climbed halfway up the garden wall. Nobody stops at the wicket, nobody lingers at the mouth of the gap, and behind you Maudra turns a page about once a minute.

It's about ten to five. Nothing else happens at this window before the bell unless you want it to.

*(Out of game: as agreed, everything in your thread after 13:50 still waits to be rewritten. Once the others pick their next move at 13:58, this scene may change to fit it. None of your four actions needed a roll, since each was routine with nothing uncertain riding on it.)*
````

## Round 24

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: “Good. Thank you for keeping his words straight.”

I turn to Sefa. “Let's hear him before we walk out front. He may know who that bench is watching.”

I go left along the chapter house wall toward the east lane and garden wicket, keeping Sefa beside me. When we reach Pell, I keep my voice low.

“We looked for you at noon. Glad you're on your feet. We have Aubrey's ticket and the court's seal; just need Hesper's money. What's this about a cart?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa nods once, already turning toward the square door rather than the propped front.

"The wicket, then. Left along the wall, in the temple's shade. He didn't choose that corner for the cool." She keeps her voice low under the antechamber's hush. "Whatever cart he means, I'd rather hear it from his mouth than from a crier in the square."

She pauses at the threshold, just inside the oak door, and glances back at Bram. "He said *not out front*—so we don't step out side by side and make a procession. You first, I'll be two paces behind, like a man going about his business and a woman keeping to the shade. We're not running. We want to look like people with time, not people with a sealed court ruling in their satchel."

Her hand drifts, without thinking, to the pocket where the ticket rests, then falls back to her side.

"From the wicket we'll learn what he's seen. Then Hesper's—whether that means the main street or the footway depends on what he tells us."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Teo nods at the thanks as if it were part of the job, and maybe it is. He's already sanding a line in his book before Bram turns away.

Sefa stops just inside the propped oak door and says her piece low. Then she lets Bram step out first and follows two paces behind him, keeping to the shade.

The square is bright and quiet in the early afternoon. Water runs in the fountain with nobody at it. Bram turns left at once and keeps close to the chapter house wall, where the temple's shadow runs in a strip about an arm wide. He walks at the pace of a man with an errand but no hurry. Sefa comes behind him at the same pace, one hand loose at her side, looking like a woman who happens to be going the same way.

The great steps rise on their right. For a few strides before the steps cut it off, the main street opens up between the last roofs, all the way down to the alehouse hatch. The big man is still on the end of the bench with his legs out across the cobbles and a cup at his elbow, too far off to see his face. His head is turned toward the foot of the steps and the square in front of them. It isn't turned toward the strip of shade along the wall. Then the steps come between them and him, and there's only rose granite on the right and whitewash on the left.

Nobody calls out, and no one in the square looks their way twice.

At the buttress on the corner the wall turns, and they step out of the square into the east lane. The sheds are on one side. On the other is the long garden wall with its plank wicket and the latch-string hanging through its hole. Across the lane, between the two woodstores, is the narrow mouth of the footway the two of them came out of an hour ago. Over the wall the bean poles stand in their pairs, and a brother in a straw hat is bent over the far rows with his back to the cloister. It's a few minutes past two.

*(Out of game: under the retcon we agreed, Pell is waiting by this wicket at about 14:03, so your two threads meet here. Pell's afternoon from 14:00 on is now replaced: the footway walk, the second visit to Lucan and Maudra, and the window are all set aside. Bram's words to him will be heard when his player answers next round, in the joined scene.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays where he is, a pace back in the sill's shadow. He watches the boy's basket go into the gap and doesn't think about him again.

While he waits, he checks his kit by touch without looking down. Shortsword loose in its scabbard. Both daggers where his hand expects them. The three potions in the satchel, stoppers turned outward so he can reach them without fumbling. He doesn't string the bow. In a footway a shield wide it would only be in the way.

*One in front, one behind. Bram carries it, because the mail and the shield are what a man on a bench respects. Sefa in the middle. Me at the back, where I can see the gap close behind us.* He turns it over once more and leaves it there.

He says nothing to Maudra. The page turns behind him about once a minute, and he lets it.

*(Out of game: understood about the timeline. Fine with any of Pell's afternoon being reshaped to fit where Bram and Sefa actually are. If you'd rather jump him to meet them, he'll go wherever they turn up.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Under the retcon, Pell was never in the archive. At ten to two he left Teo's table, walked the shaded strip along the chapter house wall and turned the corner at the buttress into the east lane. He has been at the garden wicket about ten minutes.

The lane is quiet in the early afternoon. Over the wall the bean poles stand in their tied pairs, and the brother in the straw hat is bent over the far rows with his back to the cloister. Across the lane, between two woodstores, is the narrow mouth of a path. From the ground Pell can't see where it goes. A cat sits on the shed roofs and watches him.

Pell leans on the wall beside the latch-string and checks his kit by touch, without looking down. The shortsword sits loose in its scabbard. Both daggers are where his hand expects them. In the satchel he turns the stoppers of the three red flasks outward so his fingers find them without fumbling. The bow stays unstrung on his back.

Then he hears iron rings shifting on a man who walks, coming from the square end of the lane. It's an unhurried, heavy step. A brown-painted shield comes round the buttress into the lane, and two paces behind it, keeping to the shade, comes a woman in dark leather. It's a few minutes past two.

*(Out of game: as agreed, Pell's afternoon after 1:50 is replaced. The archive, Maudra, the second talk with Lucan and the footway walk never happened, so Pell knows only what he had by 1:50. The three of you are now together at the wicket, and Bram's words are spoken next round.)*
````

## Round 25

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 25 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: I stop by the wicket, leaving the footway clear, and lower my voice to Pell.

“We looked for you at noon. Glad you're on your feet. We've got Aubrey's ticket and the court's sealed ruling. Hesper has the redemption money ready at home.”

I glance back toward the corner, then give him my attention.

“What did you find—and what's this cart we need to catch?”

Sefa Marrin: Sefa steps out of the temple's shadow at the corner and scans the lane as she walks: the shed mouths, the woodstores, the wicket. When she picks Pell out by the garden wall, she doesn't wave. She closes the last few yards at the same even pace, then stops close enough to speak low.

"Pell." Her eyes go to the grit on his knees, then back to his face. "You've been down, then up. Tell us where—and what cart you meant. We have the day by the throat, but we've been bright about it, not quiet."

She glances once over her shoulder toward the square, then back. "Bram has a sealed ruling from the temple, and I'm carrying the ticket itself. Sister Maudra found it for us. So if that cart can wait, we go to Hesper's now; if it can't, we hear about it before we step back out in front of any man on a bench."

Pell Dunmore: Pell comes off the wall as soon as he sees the shield, and he meets them a few steps short of the buttress so they stop in the shade, out of sight of the square. He keeps his voice low.

"Don't go back out front. Ashby's big sunburnt man is on the alehouse bench across from Thole's with a cup. He can see Thole's door, the street both ways and the foot of the great steps. He's been there since noon and he doesn't care who sees him." He glances past Bram's shoulder toward the corner. "Did you come round the front of the steps just now, or along the chapter house wall?"

He doesn't wait long for the answer.

"Short version of my morning. The watcher's a stable lad at the Red Sheaf, north end. A man in a grey riding cloak pays him silver at the noon bell to tell what he saw. The lad told him three of you went out, a shield and two women, and which way you turned. The chandler across the street has the man on his slate in his own hand as *Ashby & Corve*, an iron buyer, there near a tenday. He has two hired swords. The big one's on that bench. A thin one rides out before light and comes back after dark." A beat. "And yesterday Ashby bought two flasks of good lamp oil and a new tinderbox. I don't like that, with a sealed box he's meant to be buying."

He nods back toward the chapter house.

"Hesper told me Thole's terms: no money without a ticket, unless the court of contracts gives him a sealed writing that the heir's redemption ends it. Teo told me the warden sent you up to Sister Maudra before noon, and that's all I know." He looks from Bram to Sefa. "So. Does the court give the writing? What did the archivist want with you? And how long have we got before the warden stops hearing?"

He tips his head at the gap between the woodstores across the lane.

"That path runs west behind the houses, toward the cooper's end. I haven't walked it yet. If we're carrying anything out of Thole's later, I'd rather bring it back that way than past the bench."

*(Out of game: understood on the retcon. Pell knows only what he had by 1:50, and the footway is just a gap he's noticed.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell comes off the garden wall as the shield rounds the buttress. He meets them halfway down the lane, so the three of them stop in the shed's shadow with the corner of the temple between them and the square. The plank wicket is a few steps behind him. Across the ruts is the gap between the two woodstores. From down here it's only a dark slot between stacked logs.

Bram stops where he leaves the lane clear and keeps his voice under the hum of the bees. He says they looked for Pell at noon, that they have the ticket and the court's seal, and that Hesper has the money at home. Then he asks what Pell found, and what cart he meant. Sefa comes up at the same even pace without raising a hand. Her eyes go to the coal grit on Pell's knees and the tar on his coat before they go to his face. She adds that she's the one carrying the ticket and that Sister Maudra found it for them. Then she looks back once toward the square.

Pell gives it to them short and low. He tells them about the bench and what the big man on it can see. He tells them the watcher is a stable lad at the Red Sheaf, paid in silver at the noon bell by a man in a grey riding cloak, and that the man wrote himself onto a chandler's slate as *Ashby & Corve*. There are two hired swords, a big one and a thin one. Then the lamp oil and the new tinderbox. He doesn't say a word about the cart. Then he asks his own questions, one after another.

Over the wall the old brother in the straw hat has straightened up between two bean poles. He watches three armed people muttering beside his gate the way a man watches weather coming. Nobody looks back at him. After a moment he sniffs, bends again and goes on pinching blackfly into his clay pot.

Nobody else is in the lane. The cat on the shed roofs has its eyes shut. From the square round the corner comes the sound of the fountain, and nothing else. It's a few minutes past two, and Pell is waiting for answers.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) There was nothing uncertain in a question to a friend, so no roll. Bram asked, and Pell answered about the watcher, the Red Sheaf, Ashby & Corve and the oil, but he said nothing about the cart.

2) This was honest talk, so no roll. Sefa told Pell that she carries the ticket, that Maudra found it and that Bram has the court's seal. She asked him about the cart rather than telling him about it, and he left that unanswered.

3) This was honest talk, so no roll. Pell gave his whole report, then asked whether you came by the steps or along the wall, whether the court gave the writing, what Maudra wanted and how long the warden keeps hearing. Those questions are put to Bram and Sefa, so the turn stops there for their answers.
````

````text
Over the wall the old brother in the straw hat has straightened up between two bean poles. He watches the three of you muttering beside his gate the way a man watches weather coming. Nobody looks back at him. After a moment he sniffs, bends again and goes on pinching blackfly into his clay pot.

Nobody else is in the lane. The cat on the shed roofs has its eyes shut. From the square round the corner comes the sound of the fountain, and nothing else. It's a few minutes past two, and you're waiting for answers.

*(Out of game: this was honest talk, so no roll. You gave your whole report, then asked whether they came by the steps or along the wall, whether the court gave the writing, what Maudra wanted and how long the warden keeps hearing. Those questions are put to Bram and Sefa, so the turn stops there for their answers.)*
````
